Limiting absorption principle for a hybrid resonance in a two-dimensional cold plasma
Maryna Kachanovska, Étienne Peillon

Abstract
We study a limiting absorption principle for the boundary-value problem describing a hybrid plasma resonance, with a regular coefficient in the principal part of the operator that vanishes on a curve inside the domain and changes its sign across this curve. We prove the limiting absorption principle by establishing a priori bounds on the solution in certain weighted Sobolev spaces. Next, we show that the solution can be decomposed into regular and singular parts. A peculiar property of this decomposition enables us to introduce a radiation-like condition in a bounded domain and to state a well-posed problem satisfied by the limiting absorption solution.



1
Introduction

Time-harmonic electromagnetic wave propagation in a cold plasma is described by the Maxwell’s equations with a frequency- and space-dependent tensor of dielectric permittivity. Various degeneracies of this tensor lead to plasma resonances, which, mathematically, is described by the occurance of singular solutions to the underlying PDEs. Much attention in the last decade was devoted to the mathematical and numerical analysis of the situation of a hybrid plasma resonance in two dimensions, where the diagonal of plasma tensor vanishes on a given spatial curve, but the off-diagonal entries are bounded away from zero, see the recent works
[
13
,
33
]
and references therein. Inside a domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the time-harmonic magnetic field
D\ni\mathbf{x}\mapsto B_{3}(\mathbf{x})
satisfies the following PDE, see
[
33
,
8
]
or
Appendix
A
:
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\big((\alpha\mathbb{N}+i\nu\mathbb{H})\nabla B_{3}^{\nu}\big)-\omega^{2}B_{3}^{\nu}=0\text{ in }D,

(1)

where
\omega\geq 0
is a given fixed frequency,
\nu>0
is an absorption parameter, and the tensors
\mathbb{N},\mathbb{H}:\,\overline{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
are Hermitian positive definite. The behaviour of the coefficient
\alpha:\,D\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is responsible for an unusual behaviour of solutions to (
1
). In particular, in the situation of a hybrid resonance,
\alpha=0
on a loop
I\subset D
. In this work we concentrate on the case when, in the vicinity of
I
,

\displaystyle\alpha(\mathbf{x})=\operatorname{dist}(\mathbf{x},I),\quad\text{ where $\operatorname{dist}(\mathbf{x},I)$ is a signed distance from $\mathbf{x}$ to $I$.}

We are interested in establishing a limiting absorption principle for the problem (
1
) equipped with appropriate boundary conditions and a sufficiently regular right-hand side. Studies of (
1
), up to our knowledge, were initiated by B. Després and his many co-workers (L.-M. Imbert-Gerard, M.-C. Pinto, R. Weder, A. Nicolopoulos, O. Lafitte, P. Ciarlet Jr., cf.
[
13
,
14
,
22
,
32
,
33
,
9
,
10
,
12
,
36
]
). In these references, with an exception of
[
33
]
, the first-order Maxwell system leading to (
1
) is considered. The following results are available in the existing literature.

The limiting absorption principle has been proven in (a) a slab geometry with
\alpha
,
\mathbb{N}
depending on a single variable; (b) in the 1D case, (c) in a very peculiar 2D situation where the separation of variables was possible. Singularities of the obtained solutions are quite well-understood in these cases; in 1D, an important connection between (
1
) and the Bessel equation has been established. For these results, please see
[
13
]
for a thorough analysis of (a) with the third-kind integral equations,
[
12
]
for (b), and
[
37
]
for (c).

In particular, all these works seem to indicate that limiting absorption solutions to (
1
) (
B_{3}^{+}=\lim\limits_{\nu\rightarrow 0+}B^{\nu}_{3}
in a certain topology) posses in particular a logarithmic and a jump singularity across the interface
I
(i.e. are not in
H^{1}(D)
).
Well-posed problems satisfied by the limiting absorption solution have been suggested in
[
9
]
and
[
33
]
, with a full theoretical justification available in one dimension only. An improvement over
[
33
]
was proposed in the work
[
8
]
, which relaxed the regularity requirements necessary for the formulation of
[
33
]
and has shown that it is injective even without the penalization terms. Moreover, under some technical assumptions, the limiting absorption solution appears to satisfy this formulation.

From the above discussion, we see that in what concerns (
1
), the following is missing:

1.

proof of the limiting absorption principle for (
1
);

2.

a well-posed problem satisfied by the limiting absorption solution;

3.

regularity results for limiting absorption solutions, especially in the case when
\mathbb{N}
is a matrix.

In this work we fill in these gaps. Unlike in the existing papers, we are able to treat the case when
\mathbb{H},\,\mathbb{N}
are no longer scalars, and depend on both variables
x,y
. We also prove the corresponding results for a large class of sufficiently regular domains. For the moment we concentrate our efforts around the case
\omega=0
.

Because we felt that the problem under consideration is already quite complicated, we decided to present the summary and proofs of the results of the paper for a simplified geometry first, and next argue that their extension to more general geometries is quite trivial. Thus, we refer the readers interested in the final results of the paper to the last section of the manuscript, namely, Section
6
; a presentation of these results to a simplified geometry, as well as more detailed comments can be found in Section
2.2
.

This article is organized as follows. In Section
2
we introduce a simplified geometry, for which we will perform most of the computations, and outline the key results of the paper. Section
3
is dedicated to preliminary results, namely, studies of (
1
) posed in subdomains with
\alpha>0
(resp.
\alpha<0
). Next, we establish the limiting absorption principle in Section
4
. Section
5
is dedicated to a formulation of a well-posed problem satisfied by the limiting absorption solution. In Section
6
we show how all the arguments presented in previous sections can be altered to consider more general geometries and comment how
\omega\neq 0
can be treated. In particular, we present the related results for a sufficiently regular domain with a hole.

2
A simplified problem, notation, principal results

2.1
A simplified problem on a rectangle

2.1.1
The geometry and the boundary-value problem

Let
\Omega
be a rectangle divided in two sub-rectangles and an interface between them:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Omega=(-a,a)\times(-\ell,\ell),\quad\Omega_{p}=(0,a)\times(-\ell,\ell),\quad\Omega_{n}=(-a,0)\times(-\ell,\ell),\quad\Sigma=\{0\}\times(-\ell,\ell),\quad a>0.\end{split}

(2)

x=-a
y=-\ell
x=a
y=\ell
\Omega_{p}
x>0
\Omega_{n}
x<0
\Gamma_{n}^{-}
\Gamma_{p}^{-}
\Gamma_{n}^{+}
\Gamma_{p}^{+}
\Sigma
\Gamma_{n}
\Gamma_{p}
\mathbf{n}

Figure 1:
An illustration to the simplified domain considered in Section
2.1
.

We denote by
\mathbf{x}=(x,y)
an element of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
The part of the boundary of
\Omega
(resp.
\Omega_{\lambda},\,\lambda\in\{p,n\}
) intersecting the lines
y=\pm\ell
is denoted by
\Gamma^{\pm}
(resp.
\Gamma^{\pm}_{\lambda}
,
\Gamma^{\pm}_{\lambda}:=\partial\Omega_{\lambda}\cap\{y=\pm\ell\},\,\lambda\in\{p,n\}
). Let us set
\Gamma:=\partial\Omega
. We define the unit normal
\mathbf{n}
to
\partial\Omega\cup\Sigma
. It is directed into the exterior of
\partial\Omega
; and, when considering
\Sigma
, from
\Omega_{n}
into
\Omega_{p}
, see Figure
1
.

Next, let us define two matrix-valued functions
\mathbb{A},\,\mathbb{T}:\,\overline{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
that satisfy the following assumptions.

Assumption 2.1
.

•

\mathbb{A},\,\mathbb{T}\in{C}^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
.

•

For all
\mathbf{x}\in\overline{\Omega}
,
\mathbb{A}(\mathbf{x}),\mathbb{T}(\mathbf{x})
are both Hermitian, positive-definite matrices.

In particular,
defining for
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{v}=p_{1}v_{1}+p_{2}v_{2},\quad|\mathbf{p}|^{2}=\|\mathbf{p}\|_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}^{2}=\mathbf{p}\cdot\overline{\mathbf{p}},

it holds, for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
, all
\mathbf{x}\in\overline{\Omega}
,
\mathbb{T}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{p}\cdot\overline{\mathbf{p}}\geq c_{\mathbb{T}}\|\mathbf{p}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}},\quad\mathbb{A}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{p}\cdot\overline{\mathbf{p}}\geq c_{\mathbb{A}}\|\mathbf{p}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}},
where
c_{\mathbb{T}},c_{\mathbb{A}}>0
.

•

Moreover,
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{T}
satisfy the following periodicity constraints:

\displaystyle\partial_{y}^{k}\mathbb{A}(.,\ell)=\partial_{y}^{k}\mathbb{A}(.,-\ell),\qquad\partial_{y}^{k}\mathbb{T}(.,\ell)=\partial_{y}^{k}\mathbb{T}(.,-\ell),\quad k=0,\,1.

We will use the following notation for the values of
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{T}
on
\Sigma
:

\displaystyle\left.\mathbb{A}\right|_{\Sigma}=\left(\begin{matrix}a_{11}&a_{12}\\
\overline{a_{12}}&a_{22}\end{matrix}\right),\qquad\left.\mathbb{T}\right|_{\Sigma}=\left(\begin{matrix}t_{11}&t_{12}\\
\overline{t_{12}}&t_{22}\end{matrix}\right).

An immediate corollary of the above assumption reads.

Corollary 2.2
.

The matrix-valued function
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\mathbb{M}_{\nu}(\mathbf{x}):=x\mathbb{A}(\mathbf{x})+i\nu\mathbb{T}(\mathbf{x})
satisfies:

\operatorname{\operatorname{Im}}\left(\mathbb{M}_{\nu}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{p}\cdot\overline{\mathbf{p}}\right)\geq\nu c_{\mathbb{T}}\|\mathbf{p}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}
,
\operatorname{\operatorname{Re}}\left(\mathbb{M}_{\nu}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{p}\cdot\overline{\mathbf{p}}\right)=x\mathbb{A}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{p}\cdot\overline{\mathbf{p}}
, for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

We study the following family of well-posed problems, parametrized by the absorption parameter
\nu>0
: given
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, find
u^{\nu}\in H^{2}(\Omega)
, s.t.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu})=f,\\
&u^{\nu}=0\text{ on }\Gamma_{p}\cup\Gamma_{n},\\
&u^{\nu}(x,\ell)=u^{\nu}(x,-\ell),\quad\text{ a. e. }x\in(-a,a),\\
&\partial_{y}u^{\nu}(x,\ell)=\partial_{y}u^{\nu}(x,-\ell),\quad\text{a. e. }x\in(-a,a).\end{split}

(3)

Remark that in the above problem the matrix-valued function
\mathbf{x}\mapsto x\mathbb{A}(\mathbf{x})
in the principal part of the operator degenerates on
\Sigma
, is positive definite in
\Omega_{p}
and negative definite in
\Omega_{n}
. The problem is regularized by adding the elliptic viscosity term
i\nu\operatorname{div}(\mathbb{T}\nabla.)
. The goal of this manuscript is to show that
u^{\nu}
converges in a given topology to a function
u^{+}
and write a well-posed problem for
u^{+}
.

2.1.2
Notation

Sobolev spaces with periodic and homogeneous boundary conditions

Recall that
L^{2}(\Omega)
is a space of complex-valued square-integrable functions on
\Omega
. We will use the following notation:

\displaystyle\|v\|^{2}=\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\int_{\Omega}|v(\mathbf{x})|^{2}d\mathbf{x}\quad(u,v)=(u,v)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\int_{\Omega}u(\mathbf{x})\overline{v(\mathbf{x})}d\mathbf{x}.

We also have, for
m\geq 1
,
H^{m}(\Omega):=\{v\in L^{2}(\Omega):\,\|v\|^{2}_{H^{m}(\Omega)}:=\|v\|^{2}+\sum\limits_{|\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|_{1}\leq m}\|D^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}v\|^{2}<+\infty\}.
In a similar manner, we define the spaces
L^{2}(\Omega_{\lambda})
and
H^{m}(\Omega_{\lambda})
,
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
. We will use notation
\|.\|_{\mathcal{O}}:=\|.\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O})}
; the meaning of the scalar product
(.,.)
as a scalar product of
L^{2}(\Omega)
or
L^{2}(\mathcal{O})
will be clear from the context.

Typically all the functions we consider are periodic in the direction
y
and have vanishing traces on
\Gamma_{p}
(resp.
\Gamma_{n}
).
The associated spaces will be denoted by calligraphic letters (
\mathcal{C}^{m}
,
\mathcal{H}^{s}
etc.). We avoid putting the indices
0
in the definitions of such spaces (cf. e.g.
H^{1}_{0}
), since the functions we consider, in general, do not vanish on
\Sigma
.

Standard trace operators and their restrictions

For a piecewise-regular
u
, i.e.
\left.u\right|_{\Omega_{\lambda}}\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega_{\lambda}})
,
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
, we define associated trace operators:

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{\mathcal{D}}u=\left.u\right|_{\mathcal{D}},\qquad\gamma_{n}^{\mathcal{D}}u=\left.x\mathbb{A}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{n}\right|_{\mathcal{D}},\qquad\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\mathcal{D}}u=\left.(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{n}\right|_{\mathcal{D}}\qquad\mathcal{D}\subset\Sigma\cup\Gamma.

(4)

We introduce additionally, for
\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma
, right and left traces and a trace jump

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}u:=\lim\limits_{\Omega_{p}\ni\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\rightarrow\mathbf{x}}u(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}),\qquad\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,n}u:=\lim\limits_{\Omega_{n}\ni\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\rightarrow\mathbf{x}}u(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}),\qquad[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u]:=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}u-\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,n}u.\end{split}

(5)

Sobolev spaces of functions on
\Omega_{\lambda}
,
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
.
Given
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
, with an obvious abuse of notation where the spaces
H^{1/2}(\Gamma_{\lambda}^{+})
and
H^{1/2}(\Gamma_{\lambda}^{-})
are identified, we define:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\;:=\;

\displaystyle\left\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\lambda}):\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{\lambda}^{+}}u-\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{\lambda}^{-}}u=0,\quad\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{\lambda}}u=0\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\;:=\;

\displaystyle\left\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega):\left.u\right|_{\Omega_{\lambda}}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\lambda}),\,\lambda\in\{n,p\}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)

\displaystyle\;:=\;

\displaystyle\left\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega):\left.u\right|_{\Omega_{\lambda}}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\lambda}),\,\lambda\in\{n,p\}\right\}.

Additionally, we have, with
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{+\infty\}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{k}(\overline{\Omega}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{u\in C^{k}(\overline{\Omega}_{\lambda}):\,\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{\lambda}}u=0,\,\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{\lambda}^{+}}D^{\mathbf{\beta}}u=\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{\lambda}^{-}}D^{\mathbf{\beta}}u,\quad|\mathbf{\beta}|\leq k\},\quad,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{k}_{comp}(\overline{\Omega}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{u\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\overline{\Omega}_{\lambda}):\operatorname{dist}(\operatorname{supp}u,\Sigma)>0\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{k}(\overline{\Omega})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{u\in C^{k}(\overline{\Omega}):\left.u\right|_{\Omega_{\lambda}}\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\overline{\Omega_{\lambda}}),\quad\lambda\in\{n,p\}\}.

For
k\geq 1
, we also define
\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Omega_{\lambda})=\overline{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}_{\lambda})}^{\|.\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\lambda})}}=\overline{\mathcal{C}^{k+1}(\overline{\Omega}_{\lambda})}^{\|.\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\lambda})}},

and, for
s\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{s}(\Omega_{\lambda})=\overline{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}_{\lambda})}^{\|.\|_{H^{s}(\Omega_{\lambda})}}=\overline{\mathcal{C}^{k}(\overline{\Omega}_{\lambda})}^{\|.\|_{H^{s}(\Omega_{\lambda})}},\quad k\geq 1.

In the above
\|v\|_{H^{s}(\Omega_{\lambda})}^{2}=\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\lambda})}+|v|_{H^{s}(\Omega_{\lambda})}^{2}
, with
|.|_{H^{s}}
being the usual Sobolev-Slobodeckii seminorm (cf.
[
30
, (3.18)]
for the respective definition). Recall that, for
0<s<1/2
,
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\Omega_{\lambda})=H^{s}(\Omega_{\lambda})
.

The above definitions extend naturally to
\Omega
instead of
\Omega_{\lambda}
.

Sobolev spaces with weights.
For
\delta\leq 2
,
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
, we define the family of Hilbert spaces (see
[
25
, Theorem 1.3, Theorem 1.11 and its proof]
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{\lambda}):=\{v\in L^{2}_{loc}(\Omega_{\lambda}):\,\|v\|_{L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{\lambda})}:=\||x|^{\delta/2}v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\lambda})}<\infty\},\\
&\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{\lambda})=\{v\in L^{2}(\Omega_{\lambda}):\,\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{\lambda})}^{2}:=\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\lambda})}+\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{\lambda})}<\infty,\;\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{\lambda}}u=0,\;\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{\lambda}^{+}}u-\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{\lambda}^{-}}u=0\}.\end{split}

(6)

Remark 2.3
.

To facilitate the distinction between these spaces, let us consider behaviour of functions from these spaces close to the interface
\Sigma
:

•

for
\delta<1
, the trace operator
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,\lambda}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{\lambda});L^{2}(\Sigma))
, cf. Corollary
B.8
in Appendix
B
.

•

for
1\leq\delta<2
, this is not true. In particular,
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{comp}(\Omega_{\lambda})
are dense in
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
, cf. Proposition
B.3
.

We single out two spaces of functions that do not admit traces on
\Sigma
:

\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{\lambda}):=\mathcal{H}^{1}_{1}(\Omega_{\lambda}),\,\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{\lambda}):=\mathcal{H}^{1}_{2}(\Omega_{\lambda}),

and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{reg}=\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega):=\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{n})\times\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p}),\quad\mathcal{V}_{sing}=\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega):=\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{n})\times\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p}).

(7)

The introduction of these two spaces will be motivated further in the paper, see Section
3.1
.

For
0\leq\delta<1
, we define the following space (remark that this space is defined globally on
\Omega
, unlike (
6
); it is Hilbert as argued in
[
25
, Theorem 1.11]
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega):=\{v\in L^{2}(\Omega):\,\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega)}^{2}:=\|v\|^{2}+\||x|^{\delta/2}\nabla v\|^{2}<\infty,\quad\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}\cup\Gamma_{n}}u=0,\quad\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma^{+}}u-\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma^{-}}u=0\},

and for
\delta\geq 1
, we will make use solely of

\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)=\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{n})\times\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p}).
Importantly, for
\delta<1
,
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)\neq\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega)
. We will also need
L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega)=L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{n})\times L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p}).

Spaces on the interface
\Sigma
.

Fractional Sobolev spaces
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\Sigma)
are defined via

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{s}(\Sigma):=\{v\in L^{2}(\Sigma):\,\exists V\in\mathcal{H}^{s+1/2}(\Omega),\text{ s.t. }\,v=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}V\},

for
s>0
(cf. also Theorem 3.37 of
[
30
]
), with the standard induced norm.
We will also need

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle\;:=\;

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)\right)^{\prime},

the dual space of linear forms. Let us define
\langle.,.\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}
the duality bracket
linear
in both arguments:

\displaystyle\langle g,h\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}=\langle g,h\rangle_{\Sigma}:=\int_{\Sigma}g(y)h(y)dy,\quad\text{ when }V=\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\quad g\in L^{2}(\Sigma).

Similarly,
\langle g,h\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=\int_{\Sigma}g(y)h(y)dy
.

The Neumann trace on
\Sigma
.

We will need the conormal trace defined on
\Sigma
for the problem (
3
) with
\nu=0
, whose strong counterpart is
\lim\limits_{\Omega_{\lambda}\ni\mathbf{x}\rightarrow\mathbf{x}_{0}\in\Sigma}({x}\mathbb{A}(\mathbf{x})\nabla u(\mathbf{x}))\cdot\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{x}_{0})
. Remark that for
\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega_{\lambda}})
-functions this quantity vanishes, and we will be interested in the classes of functions where this is no longer the case. We will thus heavily use its variational characterization, which we recall for the convenience of the reader. Let us define the weighted space,
\delta\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\delta}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega_{\lambda}):

\displaystyle=\{v\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{\lambda}):\,\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla v)\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{\lambda}),\quad(\gamma_{n}^{\Gamma^{+}_{\lambda}}+\gamma_{n}^{\Gamma^{-}_{\lambda}})v=0\},\quad\lambda\in\{n,p\}.

(8)

Given
u\in\mathcal{H}_{0}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega_{\lambda})
, the conormal trace
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma,\lambda}u
is well-defined via the generalized integration by parts formula (Theorem 2.2 in
[
16
]
), e.g. for
\lambda=p
,

\displaystyle\langle\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma,p}u,\varphi\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}:=-\int_{\Omega_{p}}\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)\,{\Phi}d\mathbf{x}-\int_{\Omega_{p}}x\mathbb{A}\nabla u\,{\nabla\Phi}d\mathbf{x},\quad\forall\Phi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})\text{ with }\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\Phi=\varphi.

The above definition of the conormal trace
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma,p}u\in\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)
extends verbatim to the space
\mathcal{H}_{\delta}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega_{\lambda})
, provided that
0<\delta<1
, since the expression

\int_{\Omega_{p}}\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)\,{\Phi}=\int_{\Omega_{p}}x^{\delta/2}\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)\,x^{-\delta/2}{\Phi}d\mathbf{x}

is well-defined as the Lebesgue’s integral, see Lemma
C.2
. Thus defined conormal trace satisfies

\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma,p}\in\mathcal{L}(\,\mathcal{H}_{\delta}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\,\Omega_{\lambda});\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)),\quad 0\leq\delta<1.

Cf. also Theorem 2.2.22 of
[
1
]
, as well as the discussion after Lemma 4.3 in
[
30
]
. We will use these facts in the paper without referring to this discussion.

We will also need
[\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u]:=\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma,p}u-\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma,n}u
, as well as the conormal trace for the problem (
3
) with
\nu>0
:

\displaystyle\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma,\lambda}u:=\lim\limits_{\Omega_{\lambda}\ni\mathbf{x}\rightarrow\mathbf{x}_{0}\in\Sigma}(({x}\mathbb{A}(\mathbf{x})+i\nu\mathbb{T}(\mathbf{x})\nabla u(\mathbf{x}))\cdot\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{x}_{0}),\quad\lambda\in\{n,p\}

Auxiliary notation.
We will use the following notation, for the domain
\mathcal{O}
being one of the domains
\Omega_{\lambda}
,
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
,
\Omega
or
\Omega\setminus\Sigma
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{s-}(\mathcal{O}):=\bigcap_{0<\varepsilon\leq s}\mathcal{H}^{s-\varepsilon}(\mathcal{O}),\quad\mathcal{H}^{t-}(\Sigma):=\bigcap_{0<\varepsilon\leq t}\mathcal{H}^{t-\varepsilon}(\Sigma),\quad 0<t<1/2.

We will say that a sequence
v_{k}
converges to
v
in
\mathcal{H}^{s-}(\mathcal{O})
if
v
converges in
\mathcal{H}^{s-\varepsilon}(\mathcal{O})
for all
\varepsilon>0
.

Let us define a special set

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\delta}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in\Omega:\,|\operatorname{dist}(\mathbf{x},\Sigma)|<\delta\},

(9)

and the family of cutoff functions, parametrized by the parameter
\varepsilon>0
, and supported in
{\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\varepsilon}}
:

\displaystyle\Omega\ni(x,y)\mapsto\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x):=\varphi_{1}\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right),\quad\varphi_{1}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,&|x|\leq 1/2,\\
0,&|x|\geq 1,\\
\in(0,1),&|x|\in(1/2,1),\end{array}\right.\quad\varphi_{1}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}).

In what follows, we will write
a\lesssim b
to indicate that
a\leq Cb
, for some constant
C>0
, independent of the absorption parameter
\nu>0
(cf. (
3
)) and data (traces/right-hand sides) of the problem, but possibly dependent on
\Omega
,
\Sigma
,
\mathbb{T}
,
\mathbb{A}
.

For brevity, we will sometimes write

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f\text{ for }\int_{\Omega}f(\mathbf{x})d\mathbf{x},\quad\int_{\Sigma}f\text{ for }\int_{\Sigma}f(y)dy,\qquad\text{ BCs for boundary conditions}.

By
\mathbb{A}^{t}
,
\mathbb{T}^{t}
and so on we will denote transposes of matrices
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{T}
etc.

2.1.3
A preliminary well-posedness result and motivation

For all
\nu>0
, the problem (
3
) is well-posed. Indeed, let the form
a_{\nu}:\,\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)\times\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be defined by

\displaystyle a_{\nu}(u,v):=((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u,\nabla v),\text{ so that }a_{\nu}(u^{\nu},v)=-\int_{\Omega}f\,\overline{v},\quad\forall v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega).

(13)

Lemma 2.4
.

For each
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
,
\nu>0
, the problem (
3
) admits a unique solution
u^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
. Also,

\displaystyle\nu^{1/2}\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\sqrt{\|f\|\|u^{\nu}\|},

(14)

\displaystyle\|u^{\nu}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\lesssim\nu^{-1}\|f\|.

(15)

The solution
u^{\nu}
belongs to
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Omega)
for all
\nu>0
.

Proof.

Consider (
13
) and remark that, by Corollary
2.2
, for any
u\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}a_{\nu}(u,u)\geq c_{\mathbb{T}}\nu\|\nabla u\|^{2}\gtrsim\nu\|u\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\Omega)},

(16)

where the last bound follows by the Poincaré inequality in
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
(valid since functions from
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
vanish on
\Gamma_{p}\cup\Gamma_{n}
). The well-posedness of (
13
) in
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
follows by continuity of
a_{\nu}
and the Lax-Milgram lemma.

The stability estimate (
14
) is obtained by taking the imaginary part of both sides of (
13
) and using (
16
):

\displaystyle c_{\mathbb{T}}\nu\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|^{2}\leq\operatorname{Im}a_{\nu}(u^{\nu},u^{\nu})=-\operatorname{Im}(f,u^{\nu})\leq\|f\|\|u^{\nu}\|.

The bound (
15
) follows from the above, using the second inequality in (
16
) and the Poincaré inequality:

\displaystyle\nu\|u^{\nu}\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|f\|\|u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|f\|\|u^{\nu}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}.

The fact that
u^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Omega)
follows by elliptic regularity, cf. e.g. the proof of
[
30
, Theorem 4.18]
.
∎

While the above estimate shows the well-posedness of (
13
), it does not indicate any convergence properties of the sequence
(u^{\nu})_{\nu>0}
as
\nu\rightarrow 0
. The principal goal of this paper is to investigate this question in detail. We present the principal results of this paper in the following section.

2.2
Principal results for the simplified problem

Recall the definition of
\mathcal{V}_{sing}
in (
7
). The following holds true.

Theorem 2.5

(Limiting absorption principle)
.

Given
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, consider the family of solutions
(u^{\nu})_{\nu>0}\subset\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
to (
3
). Then, as
\nu\rightarrow 0+
,
u^{\nu}\rightarrow u^{+}\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}
strongly in
H^{1/2-}(\Omega)
.

Definition 2.6
.

The function
u^{+}
defined in Theorem
2.5
is called a ’limiting absorption solution’.

Next, it can be shown that in a weak sense
u^{+}
satisfies
\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{+})=f
. To state what we mean by this, we start with the following observation: a function
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)
necessarily satisfies
x\mathbb{A}\nabla u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, as argued in Proposition
B.6
. This enables us to introduce the following definition.

Definition 2.7
.

We will say that
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)
satisfies
\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)=f
,
f\in L^{2}(\Omega),
if and only if

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}x\mathbb{A}\nabla u\,\nabla\varphi=-\int_{\Omega}f\,\varphi,\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega).

The above is equivalent to requiring that

\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)=f
in
\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n}
and
[\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u]=0
.

Unfortunately, the weak solution to
\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)=f
, when considered in the space
\mathcal{V}_{sing}
and equipped with appropriate boundary conditions on
\partial\Omega
, appears to be non-unique. In particular, if the absorption in (
3
) is taken negative, i.e.
\nu<0
, then
u^{\nu}\rightarrow u^{-}
with
u^{-}\neq u^{+}
, and the limit
u^{-}
satisfies
\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)=f
.

This shows that to state a well-posed problem for
u^{+}
, we need to restrict the space of solutions. Such a space cannot be singled out by imposing the regularity constraints, as it is typical in elliptic PDEs. The reason for this is that the statement of Theorem
2.5
holds true for the limit from the left
\nu\rightarrow 0-
, and the two limits
u^{+}
and
u^{-}
have the same regularity but do not coincide, see the discussion after Theorem
2.11
. Thus, the restriction is done by introducing a radiation-like condition, similarly to how it is done for the Helmholtz equation in unbounded domains, which allows to distinguish between the two limits.

In order to state such a radiation-like condition, we take inspiration from
[
8
]
. We will define a Neumann and a Dirichlet trace of a singular solution
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)
, and, as we will see, it is a relation between these traces that will ensures uniqueness of the solution to our problem. To do so, we start with the following decomposition for functions from the subspace of (
8
),
\delta=0
, with
\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
-conormal derivatives:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)=\{v\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega):\,\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla v)\in L^{2}(\Omega),\,(\gamma_{n}^{\Gamma_{+}}+\gamma_{n}^{\Gamma_{-}})v=0,\,\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}v\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)\},

(17)

equipped with the norm
\|v\|_{\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)}^{2}:=\|v\|_{\mathcal{V}_{sing}}^{2}+\|\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}v\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)
.
Then
u
can be decomposed in a unique manner as follows:

\displaystyle u=u_{reg}+u_{sing},\quad u_{sing}=u_{h}\log|x|,

(18)

where
u_{reg}\in\mathcal{H}^{1-}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)
and
u_{h}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
is a piecewise-
\mathbb{A}
-harmonic function that satisfies the following decoupled boundary-value problem (see Assumption
2.1
for the definition of
a_{11}
):

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{h})=0\text{ in }\Omega\setminus\Sigma,

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{h}=a_{11}^{-1}\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u,

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}\cup\Gamma_{n}}u_{h}=0,\qquad\text{ periodic BCs at $\Gamma^{\pm}_{p}\cup\Gamma^{\pm}_{n}$}.

In the above decomposition,
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u=\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{sing}
.

The above proposition shows that, in general, solutions
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)
to
\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)\in L^{2}(\Omega)
are not regular in the vicinity of the interface, and posses a logarithmic singularity on
\Sigma
.

Remark 2.9
.

The appearance of the logarithmic term can be understood by studying the 1D counterpart of (
18
). For a more general case, we refer the interested reader to the work
[
29
]
, which introduces the calculus of elliptic edge operators, and, more generally, to
b
-calculus techniques
[
31
,
19
]
.

For
u
as in the above proposition, we can define two types of traces. The first one is a classical conormal trace
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u\in\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)
, which is ’carried’ by
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{sing}
. The second trace, namely the one-sided Dirichlet trace for
u
, taken from
\Omega_{p}
or
\Omega_{n}
, is not defined in a classical sense, since the singular term
u_{h}\log|x|
obviously blows up in the vicinity of
\Sigma
. Nonetheless, we can define it for the regular part of the decomposition (
18
). Since
u_{reg}
is only piecewise-regular, let us introduce the associated notation for its restrictions to
\Omega_{\lambda}
. Namely, for
v\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, we define

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}:=\left.v\right|_{\Omega_{\lambda}}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{\lambda}),\quad\lambda\in\{n,p\}.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
u
be like in Proposition
2.8
. We define the one-sided trace of
u
on
\Sigma
as a trace of its regular part:
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,\lambda}u:=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{reg,\lambda}\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2-}(\Sigma),\;\lambda\in\{n,p\}.
The jump of the traces is then defined via

[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u]:=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}u_{reg}-\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,n}u_{reg}.

The notion of trace enables us to reformulate the problem satisfied by the limiting absorption solution
u^{+}
essentially as a transmission problem between
\Omega_{p}
and
\Omega_{n}
. We will single out the limiting absorption solution among all the solutions to

\displaystyle\begin{split}&u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega),\qquad\text{s.t. }\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)=f\text{ in }\Omega.\end{split}

(19)

The following result is the second main result of this paper.

Theorem 2.11
.

Given
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, the limiting absorption solution
u^{+}
as defined in Theorem
2.5
is a unique solution to the following well-posed problem: find
u
that satisfies (
19
), and

\displaystyle[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u]=-i\pi a_{11}^{-1}\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u.

(20)

Remark 2.12
.

In the above, the well-posedness is meant in the sense of Hadamard: (
19
) combined with (
20
) admits a unique solution in
\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)
, and this solution satisfies the bound
\|u\|_{\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
, with
C>0
depending on
\Omega,\mathbb{A},\mathbb{T}
only.

The above theorem shows that the limiting absorption solution satisfies a very peculiar relation between the jump of its traces and the co-normal trace. This relation indeed resembles the Sommerfeld radiation condition: it appears that the family of solutions to (
3
)
(u^{\nu})_{\nu<0}
, as
\nu\rightarrow 0-
admits a limit
u^{-}
, which satisfies (
19
) and the condition (
20
) taken with the opposite sign
[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u^{-}]=i\pi a_{11}^{-1}\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u^{-}
. This is seen later in the paper, in the proof of Theorem
4.15
, cf. Remark
4.20
.

Proposition
2.8
is proven in the end of Section
3.2.1
, Theorems
2.5
and
2.11
are proven in Section
5
.

Remark 2.13
.

The constraint
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
embedded into the space
\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)
is of technical nature, since it allows us to define the notions of the Dirichlet and Neumann trace through the decomposition of Proposition
2.8
. We believe that similar results hold for
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u\in\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)
, but we postpone the development of the corresponding argument to future works.

Remark 2.14
.

Periodic boundary conditions at
\Gamma^{\pm}
are not essential for the analysis and can be replaced by homogeneous Dirichlet or Neumann boundary conditions.

2.3
A road-map to the proofs of the results of the paper

Let us explain how the paper is organized in more detail. First of all, we will discuss the question of well-posedness of the problem (
3
) without the absorption term. More precisely, we consider

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)=f\text{ in }\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n},\\
&u=0\text{ on }\Gamma_{p}\cup\Gamma_{n},\qquad\text{ periodic BCs at }\Gamma^{\pm}_{p}\cup\Gamma^{\pm}_{n}.\end{split}

(21)

Of course, we need to be precise on the definition of the spaces in which we will look for
u
. Because the above problem is sign-indefinite, we start by considering the above problem in one of the subdomains
\Omega_{p}
.
One sees that perhaps one lacks a boundary condition at
\Sigma
. It is more natural to start with the Neumann boundary condition, since its well-definiteness relies solely on the requirement that
x\mathbb{A}\nabla u\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
(contrary to the Dirichlet boundary condition which requires a regularity of
u
itself). Thus, we first study the homogeneous Neumann problem (Section
3.1
), and next the heterogeneous one (Section
3.2
). These studies lead us, on one hand, to Proposition
2.8
about the decomposition of the fields, and, on the other had, pave the way to the proof of Theorem
4.2
about the boundedness of
\|u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
uniformly in
\nu
.

Next, we get back to the original problem with the absorption (
3
). We will prove two facts.
First, it is the fact that the family
(u^{\nu})_{\nu>0}
is uniformly bounded in
\nu
in
\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)
; this will show that the sequence
(u^{\nu})_{\nu>0}
admits a weakly convergent subsequence. At this point there are potentially infinitely many such convergent subsequences. Therefore, we additionally need to prove that
u^{\nu}
has a single limit point
u^{+}
. This is done indirectly, by showing that the conormal trace on
\Sigma
is uniformly bounded in
\nu\rightarrow 0
:

\displaystyle\text{ for all }0<\nu<\nu_{0},\qquad\|\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},\qquad\text{ see Theorem \ref {theorem:gnubound_improved}. }

This will enable us to apply the decomposition of Proposition
2.8
to the weak
L^{2}
-limits
u
of subsequences of
(u^{\nu})_{\nu>0}
, and reveal that all such limits satisfy (
20
) (see Theorem
4.15
). To prove that this condition ensures the uniqueness of the problem, will follow the idea of
[
8
]
by exploiting a non-self-adjoint nature of the limiting operator, which is expressed through the suitable Green’s formula.

3
Problems in sub-domains

3.1
A homogeneous Neumann problem

First of all, we start by considering the following auxiliary homogeneous BVP:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)=f\text{ in }\Omega_{p},\\
&\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u=0,\\
&\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}}u=0,\text{ periodic BCs at }\Gamma^{\pm}.\end{split}

(22)

Assume that
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
. We single out two spatial frameworks:

\displaystyle\text{Find }u\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})\text{ that satisfies }\eqref{eq:main_problem}.

(RP)

\displaystyle\text{Find }u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})\text{ that satisfies }\eqref{eq:main_problem}.

(SP)

The first choice appears when considering the variational formulation associated to (
22
) and looking for the largest space in which the corresponding skew-symmetric bilinear form is continuous:

\displaystyle a_{r}(u,v):=\int_{\Omega_{p}}x\mathbb{A}\nabla u\,\cdot\overline{\nabla v}.

Interestingly, as it was shown in
[
33
, Section 1.1]
, see also Lemma
3.5
, the boundary condition on
\Sigma
holds automatically true for all functions from
\mathcal{V}_{reg}
satisfying (
22
) with
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
.

The second choice is motivated by remarking that, upon setting
\boldsymbol{v}:=x\mathbb{A}\nabla u
, the first equation in (
22
) implies that
\operatorname{div}\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
. Therefore, if, additionally,
\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, then the boundary condition on
\Sigma
can be understood in the sense of equality in
\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)
.

The key result of this section is that the problems (
RP
) and (
SP
) are both well-posed (and thus coincide).

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
. Then (
RP
) and (
SP
) both admit a unique (identical) solution. Moreover,
u\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
and satisfies the following bound:

\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
, with some
C>0
independent of
f
.

Proof.

See Theorem
3.3
, Theorem
3.7
and Theorem
3.8
.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

Remarkably, the problem (
RP
), well-posed in
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
, i.e. in the spaces of functions that do not have an
L^{2}(\Sigma)
-trace, admits a solution in the space
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
. This is reminiscent of the elliptic regularity results for the classical Laplacian.

We start with proving some facts about the problem (
RP
).

3.1.1
Regular problem (
RP
): well-posedness in
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})

The key result of this section is given below.

Theorem 3.3
.

The problem (
RP
) is well-posed, and, for all
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
,
\|u\|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
.

Auxiliary results needed to prove Theorem
3.3

We start by recalling some facts about the space
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
. First of all, the same argument as in
[
17
, Theorem 1.1]
, see Proposition
B.3
, shows

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})=\overline{\mathcal{C}_{comp}(\Omega_{p})}^{\|.\|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})}}.

(23)

We also have the Poincarè inequality: for all
u\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
, it holds that (Proposition
B.4
in Appendix
B
):

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C(\Omega_{p})|u|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})}.

(24)

Let us now state several auxiliary results for the proof of Theorem
3.3
. We will make use of the sign-definiteness of the problem (
22
) and write a corresponding variational formulation, which will appear to be coercive. We start with the following observation.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
. Assume that
u\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
satisfies (
22
). Then, necessarily,
u
satisfies the following variational formulation:

\displaystyle a_{r}(u,v)=-\int_{\Omega_{p}}f\overline{v},\quad\text{for all}\quad v\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p}).

(25)

And vice versa, if
u\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
satisfies the above variational formulation, it satisfies (
22
).

To prove this result, we need the following lemma, which is a generalization of a similar result in
[
36
, p.70]
or
[
33
, Section 1.1]
, and in Appendix
C.1
, see Proposition
C.1
. At the moment we need the result below for
\varepsilon=1/2
only, however, we will make use of its extended form later.

Lemma 3.5
.

Any function
u\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
, s.t., with some
\varepsilon>0
,
\mathbf{x}\mapsto x^{1/2-\varepsilon}\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}(\mathbf{x})\nabla u(\mathbf{x}))\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, satisfies
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u=0\text{ in }\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma).

The above lemma enables us to prove Proposition
3.4
.

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

The fact that
u
as in (
RP
) satisfies (
25
) is standard and follows from integration by parts and the density of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{comp}(\overline{\Omega_{p}})
in
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
, cf. (
23
).

The fact that
u
solving (
25
) satisfies (
22
) again follows immediately, by testing (
22
) with
v\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega_{p})
which shows that

\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)=f
in
\Omega_{p}
. Next we employ Lemma
3.5
to see that
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u=0
. Finally, testing with
v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)\subset\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega)
and using the variational definition of the co-normal trace on
\partial\Omega_{p}
yields the periodicity of the co-normal derivatives in
y
-direction.
∎

Thus, we have (classically) reduced the question of the well-posedness of (
RP
) to the question of the well-posedness of the variational formulation (
25
). We have the following result.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
f\in\left(\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})\right)^{\prime}
. Then the following problem: find
u\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
, s.t.

\displaystyle a_{r}(u_{reg},v)=-\langle f,\overline{v}\rangle_{(\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p}))^{\prime},\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})},\quad\text{for all}\quad v\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p}),

admits a unique solution in
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
, and, moreover,
\|u\|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{(\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p}))^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Evidently,
a_{r}:\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})\times\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is continuous. Next, using Assumption
2.1
on
\mathbb{A}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}a_{r}(u,u)=(x^{1/2}\mathbb{A}\nabla u,x^{1/2}\nabla u)_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\geq c_{\mathbb{A}}\|x^{1/2}\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}^{2}=c_{\mathbb{A}}|u|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})}^{2},\quad\forall u\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p}).

(26)

With (
24
), this shows that
\operatorname{Re}a_{r}(u,u)\gtrsim\|u\|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})}^{2}
, and we conclude using the Lax-Milgram lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.3

It is an immediate corollary of Proposition
3.4
and Theorem
3.6
.

3.1.2
Regular problem (
RP
): regularity estimates for regular data

When the right-hand side
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, it appears that the solution to (
RP
) possesses more regularity than predicted by Theorem
3.6
. This is reminiscent of the standard elliptic regularity for the Laplace equation. The key result of this section is Theorem
3.7
, given below.
For
\mathbb{A}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega_{p}};\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
, the corresponding result was proven in the work
[
4
]
, modulo the boundary conditions, using pseudo-differential calculus techniques. Their proof extends quite straightforwardly to the case of less regular coefficients, and therefore we omit it here; see
Appendix
D

for the details.

Theorem 3.7

(Theorem 1 in
[
4
]
)
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
. Then the unique solution
u
to (
RP
) belongs to
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
, and satisfies the following stability bound:
\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})}+\|xu\|_{H^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
.

If, moreover,
f\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
, then the unique solution to
u
to (
RP
) satisfies
u\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, and the following stability bound holds true:
\|u\|_{H^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})}
.

3.1.3
Singular problem (
SP
): proof of Theorem
3.1

Now we have all ingredients that enable us to work with (
SP
). Let us address first of all the question of the existence. Since
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})\subset\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})
, the existence follows from Theorem
3.3
. Unfortunately, we cannot conclude with the uniqueness by using Fredholm-type arguments, since the space
\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})
is not compactly embedded into
L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
.
Nonetheless, the following holds true.

Theorem 3.8
.

There exists a unique solution to (
SP
) (and it is also a unique solution to (
RP
)).

Before proving this theorem, we recall that, see Propositions
B.2
,
B.3
in Appendix
B
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})=\overline{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}_{p})}^{\|.\|_{\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})}}=\overline{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{comp}(\overline{\Omega}_{p})}^{\|.\|_{\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})}}.

(27)

Proof.

As argued before the statement of this theorem, we need to prove injectivity only. Assume that
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})
satisfies (
SP
) with
f=0
. Testing (
SP
) with
v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
, and integrating by parts yields

\displaystyle 0=-\int_{\Omega_{p}}x\mathbb{A}\nabla u(\mathbf{x})\cdot\overline{\nabla v(\mathbf{x})}d\mathbf{x},\quad\forall v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p}).

(28)

Next, we will choose
v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
that satisfies (see Theorems
3.3
and
3.7
for existence/uniqueness)

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla v)=u\text{ in }\Omega_{p},

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}v=0,

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}}v=0,\qquad\text{ periodic BCs at $\Gamma_{p}^{\pm}$. }

By Proposition
3.4
and using that
\mathbb{A}
is Hermitian,
v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
satisfies the following variational formulation:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}\nabla v\cdot\overline{x\mathbb{A}\nabla q}=\int_{\Omega_{p}}x\mathbb{A}\nabla v\cdot\,\overline{\nabla q}=-\int_{\Omega_{p}}u\,\overline{q},\quad\forall q\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}_{p}).

By density (
27
) we can replace
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}_{p})
by
\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})
, so that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}\nabla v\cdot\overline{x\mathbb{A}\nabla q}=-\int_{\Omega_{p}}u\,\overline{q},\quad\forall q\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p}).

This enables us to choose
q=u
, which gives

\int_{\Omega_{p}}\nabla v\cdot\overline{x\mathbb{A}\nabla u}=-\int_{\Omega_{p}}|u|^{2},

and comparing the above with (
28
) we conclude that
u=0
.
∎

3.2
Heterogeneous Neumann problem

In what follows, we will need to understand the behaviour of a variant of (
22
) when
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u
does not vanish. Evidently, in view of Lemma
3.5
, it does not make sense to consider
u\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
. In this section we are interested in the well-posedness of the following problem (the choice of the spaces for the data will be made clear later): given
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
,
g\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
, find
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})
, s.t.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)=f\text{ in }\Omega_{p},\\
&\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u=g,\\
&\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}}u=0,\qquad\text{ periodic BCs at }\Gamma_{p}^{\pm}.\end{split}

(N)

This problem is equivalent to the following problem: find
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})
, s.t.

\displaystyle(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u,\nabla\varphi)=-(f,\varphi)-\langle g,\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\overline{\varphi}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)},\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p}).

The uniqueness of a solution to (
N
) follows from Theorem
3.1
. To prove the existence, we will construct the solution using an appropriate lifting of the data
g
, which in this case will be treated like essential boundary conditions, similarly to the Dirichlet data for the non-weighted Laplacian.

3.2.1
Well-posedness

We have the following well-posedness result.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
,
g\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
. Then the problem (
N
) admits a unique solution
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})
. This solution admits the following decomposition:

\displaystyle u(x,y)={u}_{reg}(x,y)+u_{sing}(x,y),\quad u_{sing}(x,y)=u_{h}(x,y)\log|x|,

(29)

where
{u}_{reg}\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})\cap\bigcap\limits_{\varepsilon>0}\mathcal{H}^{1-\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})
, and
u_{h}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
is a piecewise-
\mathbb{A}
-harmonic function that satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}(\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{h})=0\text{ in }\Omega_{p},\\
&\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{h}=a_{11}^{-1}g,\\
&\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}}u_{h}=0,\qquad\text{ periodic BCs at }\Gamma^{\pm}_{p}.\end{split}

(30)

This decomposition satisfies additional properties:

1. Stability in fractional and weighted Sobolev spaces. For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
C_{\varepsilon}>0
, s.t.

\|u_{h}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})}+\|u_{reg}\|_{H^{1-\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\left(\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\right).

Moreover,
u_{reg}\in\bigcap\limits_{\varepsilon>0}\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})
and, for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
C_{\varepsilon}>0
, s.t.

\|u_{reg}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\left(\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\right).

2. Property of the conormal trace. It holds that
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u=\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{sing}
and
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{reg}=0
.

3. Direct sum. The decomposition (
29
) is unique, in other words, if
u=u_{reg}^{j}+u_{h}^{j}\log|x|
, for
j=1,2
,
u_{h}^{j}
solves (
30
) and
u_{reg}^{j}\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
, then, necessarily
u_{reg}^{1}=u_{reg}^{2}
and
u_{h}^{1}=u_{h}^{2}
.

To prove this result, we state the following auxiliary lemma.

Lemma 3.10

(Proposition
C.4
in Appendix
C.2
)
.

Let
0\leq\delta<1
, and let
f\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
.
Then the homogeneous Neumann problem (
RP
) admits a unique solution
u\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
. It satisfies

u\in\bigcap_{0<\varepsilon<1}\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta+\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})\subset\bigcap_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1-\frac{\delta}{2}}\mathcal{H}^{1-\delta/2-\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p}).

Additionally,
\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1-\delta/2-\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C_{\delta,\varepsilon}\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta+2\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})}\leq\tilde{C}_{\delta,\varepsilon}\|x^{\delta/2}f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})},
for all
0<\varepsilon<1-\delta/2
.

Proof of Theorem
3.9
.

In view of the linearity of the problem and of the well-posedness/regularity result of Theorem
3.1
, it suffices to prove the corresponding result for the case
f=0
. The uniqueness is a corollary of Theorem
3.1
, and we need to prove the existence only. This will be done by constructing the solution.

Definition of
u_{sing}
.

The main idea is to treat the boundary condition in (
N
) as an
essential
boundary condition, in other words we will construct an appropriate lifting of
g
. To explain how to do so, let us first consider a simplified case
\mathbb{A}=\operatorname{Id}
and a regular
g\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Sigma)
. The first obvious choice (cf.
[
33
]
) is
u_{sing}(x,y)=g(y)\log|x|
, which would satisfy
x\partial_{x}u_{sing}=g
in
\Omega_{p}
and in particular on
\Sigma
(thus
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{sing}=g
). Decomposing the solution
u
into the regular and the singular part
u=u_{sing}+u_{reg}
, we see that

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(x\nabla u_{reg})=-\operatorname{div}(x\nabla u_{sing})=-x\log|x|\partial_{y}^{2}g,

(31)

\displaystyle\left.x\partial_{x}u_{reg}\right|_{\Sigma}=0.

It is possible to verify that the right-hand side in (
31
), since
g\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Sigma)
, defines an element of
\left(\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})\right)^{\prime}
; in this case (
31
) admits a unique solution in
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
, cf. Theorem
3.6
. When the regularity of
g
is only
\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
, it is unclear whether the right-hand side is still of regularity
\left(\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})\right)^{\prime}
, and thus the theory developed in Section
3.1
does not seem to be applicable.
To overcome this technical difficulty and consider the case of arbitrary
\mathbb{A}
, instead of
g(y)\log|x|
we take

u_{sing}(x,y)=v_{g}(x,y)\log|x|,

where
v_{g}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
is a well-chosen lifting of
a_{11}^{-1}(y)g(y)\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
. The first (natural) choice is taking a piecewise
\operatorname{div}(\mathbb{A}\nabla.)
-harmonic lifting
v_{g}=u_{h}
, with
u_{h}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)
being a unique solution to (
30
). Before proceeding, we need to verify that
u_{sing}
defined as above indeed belongs to the space
\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})
. For this we use the fact that
u_{h}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
, and

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\mapsto u_{h}(\mathbf{x})\log|x|\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})\text{ by the Hardy-type inequality, cf. Proposition \ref {prop:hardy} in Appendix \ref {appendix:weighted}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\mapsto x\nabla(u_{h}(\mathbf{x})\log|x|)=x\log|x|\nabla u_{h}+\partial_{x}u_{h}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})\text{ by $\|x\log|x|\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{p})}<\infty$.}

The stated bound
\|u_{sing}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}
follows from the above and (
30
).

We will verify a posteriori that
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u=\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{sing}
,and for the moment will concentrate on constructing
u_{reg}
.

Definition of
u_{reg}
.
Let us write the problem to be satisfied by
u_{reg}\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})
, which we will equip additionally with
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{reg}=0
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{reg})&=-\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{sing})=-\operatorname{div}(x\log|x|\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{h})-\operatorname{div}(u_{h}\mathbb{A}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{x})\\
&=-(1+\log|x|)\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{h}-\operatorname{div}(u_{h}\mathbb{A}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{x})=:f_{h},\\
&\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}}u=0,\text{ periodic BCs at }\Gamma^{\pm}_{p}.\end{split}

(32)

With Lemma
3.10
we fix
u_{reg}
as a unique solution from
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
to the above problem. Indeed, since
\nabla u_{h}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, the right-hand side
f_{h}\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
for all
\delta>0
. Hence
u_{reg}\in\bigcap_{0<\varepsilon<1}\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})\subset\mathcal{H}^{1-}(\Omega_{p})
, and the control on the norms of
u_{reg}
follows from the statement of the same lemma.

Verification that
u_{reg}+u_{sing}
is indeed a solution.
We need to verify that
u=u_{reg}+u_{sing}
as defined above satisfies (
30
) with
f=0
. By construction of
u_{reg}
and
u_{sing}
it is sufficient to check that
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u=g
only. By Lemma
3.5
, it remains to verify that
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{sing}=g
. With the variational definition of the conormal derivative, using the second line (
32
), the fact that
f_{h}\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
and Lemma
C.2
to justify that the below integrals are defined as Lebesgue’s integrals, we arrive at the following identity for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
:

\displaystyle\langle\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{sing},\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\varphi\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega_{p}}\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{sing})\varphi-\int_{\Omega_{p}}x\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{sing}\cdot\nabla\varphi

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega_{p}}(1+\log|x|)\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{h}\,\varphi-\int_{\Omega_{p}}\operatorname{div}(u_{h}\mathbb{A}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{x})\,\varphi-\int_{\Omega_{p}}x\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{sing}\cdot\nabla\varphi.

Integrating by parts the second integral and replacing
x\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{sing}
by

\displaystyle x\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{sing}=x\mathbb{A}\left(\begin{matrix}x^{-1}u_{h}+\log|x|\partial_{x}u_{h}\\
\log|x|\partial_{y}u_{h}\end{matrix}\right)=\mathbb{A}\left(\begin{matrix}u_{h}+x\log|x|\partial_{x}u_{h}\\
x\log|x|\partial_{y}u_{h}\end{matrix}\right)=\mathbb{A}\left(\begin{matrix}u_{h}\\
0\end{matrix}\right)+x\log|x|\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{h},

we conclude that

\displaystyle\langle\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{sing},\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\varphi\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}-\int_{\Sigma}a_{11}u_{h}\varphi

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega_{p}}(1+\log|x|)\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{h}\,\varphi-\int_{\Omega_{p}}x\log|x|\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{h}\,\cdot\nabla\varphi

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{h}\nabla(x\log|x|\varphi).

Since
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
,
\mathbf{x}\mapsto x\log|x|\varphi(\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
; in particular,
\nabla(x\log|x|\varphi)=(1+\log|x|)\varphi+x\log|x|\nabla\varphi\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
by the Hardy-type inequality of Proposition
B.4
. We also have that
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}(x\log|x|\varphi)=0
. Therefore, by definition of
u_{h}
, the right-hand side vanishes, and it follows that
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{sing}=a_{11}\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{h}=g=\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u
.

This also proves the property of the conormal derivative stated in the theorem.

Uniqueness of the decomposition (
29
).
Uniqueness of the decomposition follows by uniqueness of the solution to (
30
) and next to (
32
) in
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
as argued in Lemma
3.5
.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

We require that
g\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
in (
N
) in order to be able to construct its lifting in a simple manner. We believe that the well-posedness result of Theorem
3.3
holds true also for less regular data
g\in\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)
, however, the decomposition (
29
) is no longer explicit. This is postponed to the future work.

Theorem
3.9
shows that the singular part of the solution to (
N
) has a very peculiar behaviour in the vicinity of
\Sigma
: the singularity is necessarily of a logarithmic type, while of course the space
\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})
contains functions with stronger singularities in the vicinity of
x=0
.

Remark 3.12
.

As seen from the proof of Theorem
3.9
, the decomposition in (
29
) is not stable in the following sense:
\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{reg}),\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{sing})\notin L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, but rather in a larger space
\bigcap_{\delta>0}L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
. This may seem not entirely satisfactory. One way to avoid this is to change the definition of the singular term in (
29
), by incorporating a well-chosen weight into the PDE satisfied by
u_{h}
. However, the resulting decomposition appeared to be more difficult to work with, and that is why we abandoned this idea.

Theorem
3.9
also allows to prove Proposition
2.8
, announced in Section
2.2
.

Proof of Proposition
2.8
.

The function
u
as in Proposition
2.8
satisfies (
N
) for some
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, separately in
\Omega_{p}
and in
\Omega_{n}
. Also,
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma,p}u=\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma,n}u=\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
. The result is immediate with Theorem
3.9
.
∎

3.2.2
The third Green’s formula

This section is dedicated to the derivation of the third Green’s formula for functions satisfying (
N
). This construction is inspired by
[
8
]
, where the Green’s formula was used to define a weak jump of the Dirichlet trace of a regular part of the limiting absorption solution.

While the notion of the Neumann trace for (
N
) is inherited from the definition of the normal trace of functions from
{H}(\operatorname{div};\Omega_{p})
, it seems impossible to define the Dirichlet trace for solutions of (
N
), due to the presence of the logarithmic singularity, see Theorem
3.3
. Nonetheless, it appears that the third Green’s formula holds true, provided a new definition of the trace (Definition
2.10
), re-stated below. Let (cf. (
8
))

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\{v\in\mathcal{H}_{0}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega_{\lambda}):\,\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}v\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)\},\quad\lambda\in\{n,p\},

equipped with the norm
(\|.\|_{\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{\lambda})}^{2}+\|\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.)\|_{\Omega_{\lambda}}^{2}+\|\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}.\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}^{2})^{1/2}
.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega_{\lambda})
. Given the decomposition (
29
) of
u
, we define its trace as

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u:=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{reg}\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2-}(\Sigma).

The above definition of the trace takes into account the regular part of
u
only, and the trace is well-defined, due to the uniqueness of the decomposition in Theorem
3.9
. It appears in the third Green’s formula, as made precise below.

Theorem 3.14
.

Let
u,\,v\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega_{p})
, and
u_{reg},u_{h}
,
v_{reg},v_{h}
be defined in Theorem
3.9
, so that

\displaystyle u=u_{reg}+u_{h}\log|x|,\quad v=v_{reg}+v_{h}\log|x|.

Then the following integration by parts formula holds true:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Omega_{p}}\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)\overline{v}-\int_{\Omega_{p}}\overline{\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla v)}{u}&=-\langle a_{11}\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{h},\overline{\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}v_{reg}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}+\overline{\langle a_{11}\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}v_{h},\overline{\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{reg}}\rangle}_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\\
&=-\langle\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u,\,\overline{\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}v}\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}+\overline{\langle\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}v,\,\overline{\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u}\rangle}_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\end{split}

(33)

Proof.

See Appendix
E
.
∎

3.2.3
On the Dirichlet trace of singular functions

As introduced in Definition
3.13
, the notion of the Dirichlet trace
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}
depends on
\Omega_{p}
through the chosen lifting of the Neumann trace, which we fixed to be
\mathbb{A}-
harmonic. More precisely, it is a priori unclear whether
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}u=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}(\varphi_{\varepsilon}u)
,
0<\varepsilon<1
, where
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
is a cut-off function localized in the vicinity of the interface, as defined in (
2.1.2
). The answer to this question appears to be positive, see Lemma
3.15
. Another interesting question is whether the trace is preserved under a change of coordinates (the answer appears to be negative). We present however a related result, which will be used in a sequel.

Lemma 3.15

(Definition of traces)
.

Assume that
u\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
writes, for some
0<\delta<1
,

\displaystyle u=u_{s,1}\log|x|+u_{r,1}=u_{s,2}\log|x|+u_{r,2},\text{ where }u_{s,j}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p}),\;u_{r,j}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p}),\,j=1,p.

Then
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{s,1}=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{s,2}
and
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{r,1}=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{r,2}.

If, additionally,
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.),\Omega_{p})
, then
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{r,j}
and
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u=a_{11}\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{h,j}
.

Proof.

Let us start by proving that
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{s,1}=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{s,2}
. To see this, we rewrite

\displaystyle u_{r,1}-u_{r,2}=d_{s}\log|x|,\quad d_{s}:=u_{s,2}-u_{s,1},\text{ and }\nabla(u_{r,1}-u_{r,2})=\frac{d_{s}}{x}+\nabla d_{s}\log|x|.

(34)

Since
u_{r,j}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
, and
d_{s}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
, it holds that
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\frac{d_{s}(\mathbf{x})}{x}\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
, in other words,
d_{s}\in L^{2}_{\delta-2}(\Omega_{p})
. In particular,
d_{s}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})\cap L^{2}_{\delta-2}(\Omega_{p})
. By
[
17
, Theorem 1.2, Proposition 1.2]
and (
117
) (which accounts for different conventions in the definition of spaces in this article and in
[
17
]
), we conclude that
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}d_{s}=0
.

It remains to prove the equality of traces
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{r,j}
. We proceed in a similar manner, by using now that
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}d_{s}=0
and
d_{s}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
.

In particular, by Hardy’s inequality
[
5
, p.313]
, the operator
M:\,\varphi(\mathbf{x})\mapsto\frac{\varphi(\mathbf{x})}{x}

is a continuous operator

\displaystyle M\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega_{p}),L^{2}(\Omega_{p})),\quad\text{ where }\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega_{p})=\{u\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p}):\,\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u=0\}.

(35)

Therefore,
\mathbf{x}\mapsto d_{s}(\mathbf{x})/x\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, thus
d_{r}:=(u_{r,1}-u_{r,2})\in L^{2}_{-2+\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})
, for all
\varepsilon>0
. Therefore,
d_{r}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})\cap L^{2}_{\delta-2}(\Omega_{p})
, and the conclusion
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}d_{r}=0
follows using the same argument as before.

The final assertion of the lemma for
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega_{p})
is an immediate corollary of the previous statements, the decomposition defined in Theorem
3.9
and Definition
3.13
.
∎

An immediate corollary of the above lemma and Theorem
3.14
reads.

Corollary 3.16
.

Let
u,v\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega_{p})
, be s.t.
u=u_{s}\log|x|+u_{r}
and
v=v_{s}\log|x|+v_{r}
, where

\displaystyle\begin{split}&u_{s},v_{s}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p}),\quad u_{r},\,v_{r}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p}),\qquad\text{ for some }0<\delta<1.\end{split}

(36)

Then
\int_{\Omega_{p}}\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)\overline{v}-\int_{\Omega_{p}}\overline{\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla v)}{u}=-\langle a_{11}\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{s},\overline{\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}v_{r}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}+\overline{\langle a_{11}\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}v_{s},\overline{\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{r}}\rangle}_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}.

4
The limiting absorption principle and properties of solution to (
3
)

Recall the family of problems (
3
): given
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
,
\nu>0
, find
u^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
that satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu})=f\text{ in }\Omega,\\
&\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}\cup\Gamma_{n}}u^{\nu}=0,\text{ periodic BCs at }\Gamma^{\pm}.\end{split}

(P
\nu
)

As argued in Lemma
2.4
, the above problem is well-posed for all
\nu>0
. Let us discuss a strategy to prove the limiting absorption principle for (
P

ν

). First of all, we see that if we are able to prove that
g^{\nu}:=\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}
is bounded uniformly in
\nu
, namely

\displaystyle\|g^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\|f\|,

(37)

we can argue that
g^{\nu}
admits a weakly convergent subsequence (again denoted by
g^{\nu}
) in
\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
that would converge to some
g\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
. This allows to decouple the problem (
P

ν

) into two independent, sign-definite subproblems with the Neumann boundary condition on
\Sigma
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu})=f\text{ in }\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n},\\
&\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}=g^{\nu},\\
&\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}\cup\Gamma_{n}}u^{\nu}=0,\text{ periodic BCs at }\Gamma^{\pm}_{p}\cup\Gamma^{\pm}_{n}.\end{split}

(38)

and conclude about the convergence of
u^{\nu}
, using the same argument as in Theorem
3.9
. However, derivation of the estimate (
37
) is quite technical; thus we start with a simpler bound (even its weaker version (
39
))

\|g^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\|f\|.

It is then used to prove that
\|u^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{sing}}
is uniformly bounded as
\nu\rightarrow 0+
, see Theorem
4.2
. This result is further used in the proof of the bound (
37
), see Theorem
4.6
. Once the bound (
37
) is established, we proceed to proving the first part of Theorem
2.5
: more precisely, we will argue that the statement of this theorem is valid up to a subsequence, see Theorem
4.15
. These results will be used in Section
5
to prove the statements of Theorem
2.5
, Theorem
2.11

Remark 4.1
.

All stability results of this section hold true when
f
in (
P

ν

) is replaced by
f^{\nu}
, s.t. there exist
C,\nu_{0}>0
, s.t. for
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
,
\|f^{\nu}\|\leq C\|f\|
.

4.1
The key stability estimate

Theorem 4.2

(The first stability estimate)
.

There exist
C,\,\nu_{0}>0
, s.t. for all
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
, the solution to (
P

ν

) satisfies the following stability bound:
\|u^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

To prove this theorem, we start with the following estimate, which shows that the norm of the conormal trace of
u^{\nu}
is well-controlled in the space
\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Given
u^{\nu}
as in (
P

ν

), let the co-normal derivative at the interface
\Sigma
be denoted by

g^{\nu}:=\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}=\left.(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu}\cdot\mathbf{n}\right|_{\Sigma}.

There exist
C,\,\nu_{0}>0
, s.t. for all
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|g^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}\leq C\left(\nu^{1/2}\|f\|+\sqrt{\|f\|\|u^{\nu}\|}\right).

(39)

Remark 4.4
.

It is quite easy to obtain a rougher version of this estimate. Indeed, since
\mathbf{v}:=(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}(\operatorname{div};\Omega_{p})
, it holds that
\|\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\|\operatorname{div}\mathbf{v}\|+\|\mathbf{v}\|\lesssim\|f\|+\|x\nabla u^{\nu}\|
. By integration by parts one sees that
\|x\nabla u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|u^{\nu}\|
, see the proof of Theorem
4.2
. Thus
\|g^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\|f\|+\|u^{\nu}\|
. Unfortunately, this rougher bound does suffice for our proof of Theorem
4.2
.

The proof of this proposition relies on the following lifting lemma. To state it, let us repeat the definition (
9
) for the convenience of the reader:
\Omega^{\delta}_{\Sigma}:=\{\mathbf{x}=(x,y)\in\Omega:\,|x|<\delta\},\quad\delta>0.

Lemma 4.5
.

For all
\phi\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
, all
0<\delta<\ell
, there exists
\Phi^{\delta}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
, s.t.

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\Phi^{\delta}=\phi,\qquad\operatorname{supp}\operatorname{\Phi}^{\delta}\subseteq\overline{\Omega_{p}\cap\Omega^{\delta}_{\Sigma}},\qquad\Phi^{\delta}(\delta,\mathbf{y})=0,\text{ and }

\displaystyle\|\Phi^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\delta\|\nabla\Phi^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C\delta^{1/2}\|\phi\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)},\quad

(40)

where
C>0
is independent of
\delta
,
\phi
.

Proof.

See Lemma
C.3
in Appendix
C.1
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Given
\phi\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
and
\delta>0
, let
\Phi^{\delta}
be its lifting from Lemma
4.5
. Recall

\displaystyle\|g^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{\phi\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma):\,\|\phi\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}=1}\left|\langle g^{\nu},\phi\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}\right|.

With the variational definition of the conormal derivative, it holds that

\displaystyle-\langle g^{\nu},\phi\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}=\int_{\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\delta}\bigcap\Omega_{p}}\operatorname{div}\left((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu}\right)\,{\Phi^{\delta}}+\int_{\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\delta}\bigcap\Omega_{p}}(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu}\cdot\nabla{\Phi}^{\delta}.

The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, Assumption
2.1
on
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{T}
and the inequality
|x|<\delta
in
\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\delta}
yield

\displaystyle|\langle g^{\nu},\phi\rangle_{\Sigma}|\leq\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\|\Phi^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+(\delta+\nu)\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\|\nabla\Phi^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}.

Next, we use (
40
) for
\Phi^{\delta}
and (
14
) for
u^{\nu}
, more precisely
\nu^{1/2}\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|\leq c\sqrt{\|f\|\|u^{\nu}\|}
:

\displaystyle|\langle g^{\nu},\phi\rangle_{\Sigma}|

\displaystyle\leq C\delta^{1/2}\|f\|\|\phi\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}+C\delta^{-1/2}(\delta+\nu)\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|\|\phi\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|f\|\delta^{1/2}+c\delta^{-1/2}(\delta+\nu)\nu^{-1/2}\sqrt{\|f\|\|u^{\nu}\|}\right)\|\phi\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}.

Choosing
\delta=\nu
proves the bound in the statement of the proposition.
∎

Once we have the result of Proposition
4.3
, we can prove Theorem
4.2
.
We use duality-type techniques.

Proof.

Proof that
\|u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|f\|
.

Let
p^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)
be a unique solution to

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left(x\mathbb{A}\nabla p^{\nu}\right)=u^{\nu}\text{ in }\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n},

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}p^{\nu}=0,

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}\cup\Gamma_{n}}p^{\nu}=0,\qquad\text{ periodic BCs at }\Gamma_{p}^{\pm}\cup\Gamma_{n}^{\pm},

namely, the homogeneous decoupled Neumann problem,
cf. Theorem
3.1
for the well-posedness. Thus defined
p^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)
satisfies the following variational formulation:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}\nabla p^{\nu}\cdot\overline{x\mathbb{A}\nabla v^{\nu}}+\int_{\Omega_{n}}\nabla p^{\nu}\cdot\overline{x\mathbb{A}\nabla v^{\nu}}=-\int_{\Omega}u^{\nu}\overline{v^{\nu}},\quad\forall v^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma).

(41)

On the other hand, testing (
P

ν

) with
p^{\nu}
yields the following identity:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu}\cdot\overline{\nabla p^{\nu}}+\int_{\Omega_{n}}(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu}\cdot\overline{\nabla p^{\nu}}+\int_{\Sigma}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}\,\overline{\left(\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}p^{\nu}-\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,n}p^{\nu}\right)}=-\int_{\Omega}f\overline{p^{\nu}}.

Replacing the terms that involve
x\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}\cdot\overline{\nabla p^{\nu}}
in the first two integrals in the above by the conjugated right-hand-side of (
41
) where
v^{\nu}=u^{\nu}
yields the identity:

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}|u^{\nu}|^{2}=-\int_{\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n}}i\nu\mathbb{T}\nabla u^{\nu}\cdot\overline{\nabla p^{\nu}}-\int_{\Omega}f\overline{p}^{\nu}-\int_{\Sigma}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}\,\overline{[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}p^{\nu}]}.

Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we obtain the following bound:

\displaystyle\|u^{\nu}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\nu\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|(\|\nabla p^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\|\nabla p^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{n})})+\|\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}(\|\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}p^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}+\|\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,n}p^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)})

\displaystyle\lesssim\nu\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|\|p^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)}+(\nu^{1/2}\|f\|+\sqrt{\|f\|\|u^{\nu}\|})\|p^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)},

where in the last inequality we used the continuity of the trace operator in
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
(resp.
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{n})
and the bound on the conormal trace (
39
). Next, we apply Theorem
3.7
to bound
\|p^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)}\lesssim\|u^{\nu}\|
in the right-hand side of the above, and use the bound
\nu\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|f\|
from (
15
). Dividing both sides of the obtained expression by
\|u^{\nu}\|
leads to the following bound:

\displaystyle\|u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|f\|+(\nu^{1/2}\|f\|+\sqrt{\|f\|\|u^{\nu}\|}).

Using the Young inequality
\sqrt{\|f\|\|u^{\nu}\|}\leq\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon^{-1}\|f\|+\varepsilon\|u^{\nu}\|)
with
\varepsilon
sufficiently small independent of
\nu
yields

\displaystyle\|u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|f\|.

(42)

Proof that
\|xu^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|f\|
.

We test (
P

ν

) with
xu^{\nu}
. This yields

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}x(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu}\cdot\overline{\nabla u^{\nu}}-\int_{\Omega}\overline{u^{\nu}}(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{x}=\int_{\Omega}f\overline{xu^{\nu}}.

Taking the real part of the above and using that
\mathbb{A},\,\mathbb{T}
are Hermitian (cf. Corollary
2.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}x^{2}\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}\cdot\overline{\nabla u^{\nu}}-\operatorname{Re}\int_{\Omega}\overline{u^{\nu}}(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{x}=\operatorname{Re}\int_{\Omega}f\overline{xu^{\nu}}.

Together with the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality this shows that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}x^{2}|\nabla u^{\nu}|^{2}\lesssim\left(\|x\nabla u^{\nu}\|+\nu\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|\right)\|u^{\nu}\|+\|f\|\|u^{\nu}\|.

With (
42
) and (
15
) (namely
\nu\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|f\|
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\|x\nabla u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|f\|\|x\nabla u^{\nu}\|+\|f\|^{2}.

Using the Young’s inequality, we prove that
\|xu^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|f\|^{2}
.
∎

Quite a rough result of Theorem
4.2
paves the way to proving a series of regularity estimates. We will present the corresponding results step-by-step in the list of propositions below. Before continuing, we state an immediate corollary of Theorem
4.2
and Lemma
2.4
, in particular of (
14
): for all
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
,

\displaystyle\|u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|x\nabla u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\nu^{1/2}\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

(43)

4.2
Refined stability estimates on the conormal trace

The key result of this section is an improved regularity of the conormal derivative of
u^{\nu}
. As we will see further, this result will play a crucial role in constructing the limiting absorption solution.

Theorem 4.6
.

Given
u^{\nu}
as in (
P

ν

), let
g^{\nu}:=\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}
. Then
g^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
, and there exist
C,\nu_{0}>0
, s.t.

\displaystyle\|g^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}\leq C\|f\|,\quad\text{ for all }0<\nu<\nu_{0}.

(44)

From Proposition
4.3
combined with Theorem
4.2
we already know that
\|g^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}\leq C\|f\|.

Thus the result of Theorem
4.6
improves this regularity by one order. The proof of Theorem
4.6
relies on two auxiliary results. The first one is a counterpart of Proposition
4.3
, and is given below.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
g^{\nu}
be like in Theorem
4.6
. There exist
C_{1},C_{2},\,\nu_{0}>0
, s.t. for all
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
,

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}g^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}\leq C_{1}\|g^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}\leq C_{2}\left(\|f\|+\sqrt{\|f\|\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|}\right).

(45)

The second results indicates an improved regularity of
u^{\nu}
in the direction tangential to the interface.

Proposition 4.8
.

There exist
C,\,\nu_{0}>0
, s.t. for all
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
, the solution to (
P

ν

) satisfies:

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|\leq C\|f\|.

Proof.

Once Proposition
4.7
is proven, the result follows by an argument resembling the proof of Theorem
4.2
, thus can be found in Appendix
H
.
∎

As soon as we have these two Propositions proven, we can prove Theorem
4.6
.

Proof of Theorem
4.6
.

Follows at once from Propositions
4.7
and
4.8
.
∎
