The remainder of the section is dedicated to the proof of Proposition
4.7
.
Since this proposition is an improvement over the earlier result of Proposition
4.3
, we pursue a similar strategy in its proof. Looking through the proof of Proposition
4.3
, we see that it relies on the following two components:

•

the bound (
14
) for
\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|
, namely
\nu^{1/2}\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\sqrt{\|f\|\|u^{\nu}\|}
;

•

a lifting Lemma
4.5
with well-tuned parameters.

To prove Proposition
4.7
, it would be natural to require a bound of the type (
14
) with
u^{\nu}
replaced by
\partial_{y}u^{\nu}
; however, such a bound does not seem to hold true. The key observation is the following: if we split the solution into high- and low-frequency components (with respect to the variable
y
, tangential to the interface
\Sigma
), with a well-chosen cutoff frequency, a necessary bound can be shown to hold true for the high-frequency component. On the other hand, low-frequency components have a high regularity, and the control on the corresponding conormal derivative can be obtained easier. This is the starting idea of the approach currently hidden in technical details. The suitable definition of a low- and high- frequency appears to be
\nu
-dependent, and thus more care should be given to the construction of an appropriate lifting, as well as obtaining low-frequency bounds. In particular, we will require fine regularity estimates on the solution in fractional Sobolev spaces; this is inspired by the earlier paper of Baouendi and Goulaouic
[
4
]
.

4.2.1
Extension operators and Bessel potentials

In what follows, we will use Fourier analysis/pseudo-differential calculus techniques, in the spirit of
[
4
]
, to relate weighted and fractional Sobolev norms through appropriate embeddings.

We start by rewriting the problem (
P

ν

) in the strip
(-a,a)\times\mathbb{R}
, by using periodic boundary conditions, next localize the new extended solution around the strip
\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\varepsilon}
, and, finally, will make use of the Fourier transform in the
y
-direction. To do so, we need to introduce extension, localization and restriction operators. In the case of periodic functions on
\Omega
those operators take an extremely simple form.

Extension operators.
Let us fix
0<\delta<\ell/2
(recall that
\Omega=(-a,a)\times(-\ell,\ell)
), and define a truncation function in the tangential (i.e.
y-
) direction
\chi_{\ell,\delta}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni\mathbf{x}=(x,y)\mapsto\chi_{\ell,\delta}(x,y):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,&|y|\leq\ell+\delta/2,\\
0,&|y|>\ell+\delta,\\
\in(0,1),&\text{ otherwise. }\end{array}\right.

Sometimes we will write
\chi_{\ell,\delta}(y)
instead of
\chi_{\ell,\delta}(x,y)
, to underline that
\chi_{\ell,\delta}
is independent of
x
.

Let us set
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\,x\in(-a,a)\}
. Then we define the operator
\mathcal{E}_{\delta}
as a product of a truncation and the periodic extension operator:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\delta}

\displaystyle:\,L^{2}(\Omega)\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}),\quad\mathcal{E}_{\delta}u(x,y)=\chi_{\ell,\delta}(x,y)\mathcal{E}u(x,y),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle:\,L^{2}(\Omega)\rightarrow L^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}),\quad\mathcal{E}u(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}u(x,y),&|y|<\ell,\\[5.0pt]
u(x,y-2k\ell),&y\in(\ell(2k-1),\,\ell(2k+1)).\end{array}\right.

The following observations will be of use: there exists
C_{\delta}>0
, s.t. for all
u
sufficiently regular,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|u\|_{\Omega}\leq\|\mathcal{E}_{\delta}u\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}\leq C_{\delta}\|u\|_{\Omega},\quad\|\partial_{y}u\|_{\Omega}\leq\|\partial_{y}\mathcal{E}_{\delta}u\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}\leq C_{\delta}(\|\partial_{y}u\|_{\Omega}+\|u\|_{\Omega}),\\
&\|\partial_{y}(\mathbb{P}\nabla u)\|_{\Omega}\leq\|\partial_{y}(\mathbb{P}\nabla\mathcal{E}_{\delta}u)\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}\leq C_{\delta,\mathbb{P}}(\|\mathbb{P}\partial_{y}\nabla u\|_{\Omega}+\|\partial_{y}u\|_{\Omega}+\|u\|_{\Omega}),\quad\mathbb{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2},\\
&\|x\nabla\mathcal{E}_{\delta}u\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}\leq C_{\delta}\|u\|_{\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)}.\end{split}

(49)

Definition of
\mathcal{J}
.
Next, let us define a Bessel potential in the direction
y
. Let us introduce the space of functions compactly supported in the direction
y
:

{C}^{\infty}_{0,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}):=\{v\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}):\,\operatorname{supp}v\subseteq[-a,a]\times[-L,L],\text{ for some }L>0\}.

Given
u\in{C}_{0,y}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
, we define the (uni-directional) Bessel potential
\mathcal{J}:\,{C}_{0,y}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})\rightarrow{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}u:=\mathcal{F}_{y}^{-1}\left((1+\xi_{y}^{2})^{1/4}\mathcal{F}_{y}u\right),\quad\mathcal{F}_{y}v(x,\xi_{y}):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{e}^{-i\xi_{y}y}v(x,y)dy.

The operator
\mathcal{J}
extends by density to a bounded linear operator from
H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
into
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
.

By the Plancherel identity together with the Fubini theorem, we have that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{J}^{2}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}=\int_{-a}^{a}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+\xi_{y}^{2})|\mathcal{F}_{y}u(x,\xi_{y})|^{2}d\xi_{y}\,dx=\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\|\partial_{y}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})},\quad\forall u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}).

(50)

4.2.2
Extending the problem (
P

ν

) to
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}
and relating the conormal derivatives.

Let us introduce

\displaystyle U^{\nu}(x,y):=\mathcal{E}u^{\nu}(x,y),\quad U^{\nu}_{\delta}(x,y):=\mathcal{E}_{\delta}u^{\nu}(x,y),

and extend
\mathbb{A},\mathbb{T}
by periodicity to the full strip
\mathbb{R}_{a}^{2}
preserving the notation. The truncated function
U^{\nu}_{\delta}=\chi_{\ell,\delta}U^{\nu}
satisfies the new problem:

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\right)

\displaystyle=F^{\nu}_{\delta}\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a},

(51)

\displaystyle F^{\nu}_{\delta}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{\delta}f+U^{\nu}\operatorname{div}((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla\chi_{\ell,\delta})+\nabla U^{\nu}\cdot(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla\chi_{\ell,\delta}+\nabla\chi_{\ell,\delta}\cdot(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla U^{\nu}.

Remark that
F^{\nu}_{\delta}
is supported on
|y|\leq\ell+\delta
, and
U^{\nu}=\chi_{\ell,2\delta}U^{\nu}
on
|y|\leq\ell+\delta
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\|F^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\leq\|\mathcal{E}_{\delta}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+c_{1,\delta}\left(\|U^{\nu}_{2\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\|x\nabla U^{\nu}_{2\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\nu\|\nabla U^{\nu}_{2\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\right),

(52)

for some
c_{1,\delta}>0
.
The following stability bound follows from (
49
) and (
43
) (remark that the bound below is stronger than needed, cf. the power of
\nu
):

\displaystyle\|U^{\nu}_{2\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\|x\nabla U^{\nu}_{2\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\nu^{1/2}\|\nabla U^{\nu}_{2\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2,\delta}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

(53)

with
c_{2,\delta}>0
. Since
\|\mathcal{E}_{\delta}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\leq C_{\delta}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
(see (
49
)), we conclude that there exists
c_{3,\delta}>0
, s.t.

\displaystyle\|F^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\leq c_{3,\delta}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},\quad\forall 0<\nu<1.

(54)

In what follows, we will also need

Lemma 4.9
.

For all
\nu>0
,
U^{\nu}_{\delta}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
; also
\gamma_{0}^{\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}\left(\partial_{y}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\right)=0
,
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
.

Proof.

Follows by elliptic regularity (
u^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Omega)
by Lemma
2.4
) and definition of
\mathcal{E}_{\delta}
.
∎

The rewriting (
51
) of the problem (
3
) will enable us to apply the Fourier transform in the direction
y
and obtain more precise regularity estimates. To prove Proposition
4.7
we will resort to proving an analogous result for
U^{\nu}_{\delta}
. For this, let us introduce

\Sigma_{\infty}:=\{(0,y),\,y\in\mathbb{R}\}.

(55)

The result below is standard and links norms of
\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}
and
\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta}:=\left.(\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\cdot\mathbf{n}\right|_{\Sigma^{\infty}}
(see in particular
[
21
, Theorem 4.2.1]
on equivalence of different
\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
-norms, and use e.g. a standard trace norm based on parametrization of
\Gamma
, cf.
[
21
, p.176]
).

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
\delta>0
. There exists
C_{\delta},\nu_{0}>0
, s.t. for all
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
,

\displaystyle\|\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}\leq C_{\delta}\|\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}.

Remark 4.11
.

In what follows, we will never need to make
\delta\rightarrow 0
or
\delta\rightarrow+\infty
. Therefore, where appropriate, we will not indicate the dependence of the bounds on
\delta
, but rather consider that we fix
\delta>0
.

4.2.3
Auxiliary regularity bounds

In what follows we will need two stability bounds. The first one is a counterpart of the bound (
14
), namely
\nu\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|^{2}\lesssim\|f\|\|u^{\nu}\|
. Informally, the result below says that
\nu^{1/2}\|(-\partial_{y}^{2})^{1/4}\nabla u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|f\|+\sqrt{\|f\|\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|}
.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
(u^{\nu})_{\nu>0}
solve (
P

ν

),
U^{\nu}_{\delta}=\mathcal{E}_{\delta}u^{\nu}
satisfy (
51
), and let the matrix-valued function
\mathbb{B}\in C^{1,1}([-a,a]\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
. Then there exist
C,\,\nu_{0}>0
, s.t. for all
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
,

\displaystyle\nu\|\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{B}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\leq C\left(\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

Proof.

See Appendix
E.1.2
.
∎

The second bound is a standard elliptic regularity bound, explicit in
\nu>0
.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
U^{\nu}_{\delta}
and
\mathbb{B}\in C^{0,1}([-a,a]\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
. Then there exist
C,\,\nu_{0}>0
, s.t.

\displaystyle\nu\|\partial_{y}(\mathbb{B}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},\text{ for all }0<\nu<\nu_{0}.

(56)

Proof.

See Appendix
E.1.3
.
∎

4.2.4
Frequency filters and corresponding results

As discussed before, our proof of Proposition
4.7
relies on the decomposition of the solution in high- and low-frequency components in the direction tangential to the interface, and working with them in a separate manner. The originality of the approach is that the definition of the ’low-’ and ’high-’ frequency components is now
\nu
-dependent; as we will see later, we will need more sophisticated stability estimates for the low-frequency components of the solution, which make use of an improved regularity in the tangential direction. This is due to the definition of the lifting operator, which allows to control well high frequencies, but is less efficient at low frequencies, cf. Lemma
4.14
.

Interface filters.
Let us introduce high- and low-frequency filters in the
y
-direction on the line
{\Sigma}_{\infty}
, cf. (
55
), and next on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}
. For a fixed
w>0
, we define (where
\mathcal{F}
is the Fourier transform):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{L}_{w}:\,L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\quad\mathcal{L}_{w}q:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\mathbb{1}_{|\xi|<w}\mathcal{F}q(\xi)\right),\quad\mathcal{H}_{w}:=\operatorname{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{w}.\end{split}

(57)

It is straightforward to verify that a priori
\|\mathcal{L}_{w}u\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C\|u\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R})}
, for all
u\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
, thus both filters are continuous on the space
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, we also have that
\|\mathcal{L}_{w}u\|_{H^{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C_{s,p}\|u\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R})}
, for all
p\in\mathbb{R}
. Additionally, these operators commute with
\partial_{y}
, i.e.
[\partial_{y},\mathcal{L}_{w}]=0
, and similarly for
\mathcal{H}_{w}
.

Volume filters.
In a similar manner, we define the filter operators on the strip
\mathbb{R}_{a}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{w}:\,L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}),\quad\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{w}q=\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{y}\left(\mathbb{1}_{|\xi_{y}|<w}\mathcal{F}_{y}q(.,\xi_{y})\right),\\
&\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}_{w}=\operatorname{Id}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{w},\quad w>0.\end{split}

(58)

From the Plancherel identity, it follows that these operators are
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
-orthogonal projectors. Again, we have the following commutation relations:
\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{w}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{w}\nabla
, and, of course,
f(x)\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{w}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{w}(f(x)\cdot)
, for any
f\in L^{\infty}(-a,a)
(i.e.
[\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{w},f(x)]=0
).

4.2.5
Lifting lemmas

Another important component of our approach are lifting lemmas, cf. the proof of the less precise counterpart of Proposition
4.7
, namely Proposition
4.3
. Below we will present a lifting lemma for functions defined on a real line
\Sigma_{\infty}
.
Let us also denote by
\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}:=(0,a)\times\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
0<\nu<a
. There exists a bounded linear operator

\displaystyle L^{\nu}:\,H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}),\text{ s.t. }\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}L^{\nu}\psi=\psi,\text{ and }\operatorname{supp}L^{\nu}\psi\subseteq[0,\nu]\times\mathbb{R}.

This operator commutes with
\partial_{y}
: for all
\psi\in H^{3/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})
,
\partial_{y}L^{\nu}\psi=L^{\nu}\partial_{y}\psi
, and satisfies the following bounds, valid for all
0<\varepsilon<1/2
,
\psi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})
, with
C_{\varepsilon}>0
independent of
\nu
, but depending on
\varepsilon
:

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}L^{\nu}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|\psi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})},

(59)

\displaystyle\nu^{1/2}\|\mathcal{J}\partial_{x}L^{\nu}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\nu^{-1}}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|\psi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})},

(60)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{x}L^{\nu}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon\nu^{-1}}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|\psi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}.

(61)

Proof.

See Appendix
F
.
∎

We see that the above lifting lemma distinguishes between the low- and high-frequency cases. We are able to construct a lifting that is supported on
(0,\nu)
, and whose
H^{1}
-seminorm is controlled for ’high’ frequencies, however, we are not able to do this for the
’low’ frequency case, where only derivatives in the tangential to
\Sigma
direction are bounded uniformly in
\nu
.

4.2.6
Proof of Proposition
4.7

We start by remarking that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}\leq C_{1}\|\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)},

(62)

which follows by using the Fourier characterization of the norms on closed regular curves
[
21
, Section 4.2.1]
. To bound the above further, we use Proposition
4.10
, and thus it remains to establish the corresponding bound for
\|\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}
. The Fourier definition of the
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
-norm and
(1+\xi^{2})^{1/2}=(1+\xi^{2})^{-1/2}+\xi^{2}(1+\xi^{2})^{-1/2}
yield

\displaystyle\|\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}^{2}=\|\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}^{2}+\|\partial_{y}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}^{2}.

(63)

The
H^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})
-part can be controlled using

\displaystyle\|\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}\lesssim\|\operatorname{div}((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu)\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\|(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu)\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

see (
53
) and (
54
).The estimate (
62
), Proposition
4.10
, (
63
) and the estimate above yield

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}\leq C_{1}\|\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}\leq C(\|f\|+\|\partial_{y}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}),

(64)

with some
C>0
. It remains to control the following quantity:

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}=\sup\limits_{0\neq\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\infty})}\frac{\left|\langle\partial_{y}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta},\overline{\varphi}\rangle_{H^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty}),H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}\right|}{\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}},

(65)

where we used the density of
C_{0}^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\infty})
in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})
. By definition of the distributional derivative

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{y}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta},\overline{\varphi}\rangle_{H^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty}),H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}=-\langle\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta},\partial_{y}\overline{\varphi}\rangle_{H^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty}),H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}.

To estimate the above, we use the variational definition of the conormal trace, with
\Phi^{\nu}:=L^{\nu}\varphi
, see Lemma
4.14
, and the fact that
[\partial_{y},L^{\nu}]=0
:

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{y}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta},\overline{\varphi}\rangle_{H^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty}),H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu}}\operatorname{div}((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})\overline{\partial_{y}\Phi^{\nu}}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu}}(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\,\overline{\partial_{y}\nabla\Phi^{\nu}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}}F^{\nu}_{\delta}\overline{\partial_{y}\Phi^{\nu}}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu}}\partial_{y}\left((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\right)\cdot\overline{\nabla\Phi^{\nu}},

where we also used in the integration by parts that
U^{\nu}_{\delta}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
, cf. Lemma
4.9
. To obtain the required estimates, we will exploit the fact that
\|\partial_{y}\Phi^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}/\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}
is uniformly bounded in
\nu
, cf. (
59
), and single out the respective terms:

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{y}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta},\overline{\varphi}\rangle_{H^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty}),H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\mathcal{I}_{j},\quad\text{ where }\mathcal{I}_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}}F^{\nu}_{\delta}\overline{\partial_{y}\Phi^{\nu}},

(66)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu}}\mathbf{e}_{y}\cdot\partial_{y}\left((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\right)\overline{\partial_{y}\Phi^{\nu}},\qquad\mathcal{I}_{3}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu}}\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\partial_{y}\left((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\right)\overline{\partial_{x}\Phi^{\nu}}.

A bound on
\mathcal{I}_{1}
.

With the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}_{1}\right|\leq\|F_{\delta}^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\nu}^{2,+})}\|\partial_{y}\Phi^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\nu}^{2,+})}\leq C_{\delta}\|f\|\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})},\quad C_{\delta}>0,

(67)

where the last bound follows from the bound (
54
) on
\|F_{\delta}^{\nu}\|
and (
59
).

A bound on
\mathcal{I}_{2}
.
By the same argument as above, it holds that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{e}_{y}\cdot\partial_{y}\left((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\right)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\|(x\partial_{y}\mathbb{A}+i\nu\partial_{y}\mathbb{T})\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}+\|(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\partial_{y}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}\right)\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}.

We bound
\|(x\partial_{y}\mathbb{A}+i\nu\partial_{y}\mathbb{T})\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}\lesssim\|f\|
, cf. (
53
); as for the second term, we use
|x|<\nu
:

\displaystyle\|(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\partial_{y}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}\lesssim\nu\|\partial_{y}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

as argued in (
56
). This proves that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{2}|\lesssim\|f\|\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}.

(68)

A bound on
\mathcal{I}_{3}
.
We split the term
\partial_{x}\Phi^{\nu}
into low- and high- frequencies:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{3,l}+\mathcal{I}_{3,h},\qquad\mathcal{I}_{3,l}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu}}\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot(x\partial_{y}(\mathbb{A}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})+i\nu\partial_{y}(\mathbb{T}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}))\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}L^{\nu}{\mathcal{L}}_{(2\nu)^{-1}}\varphi},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{3,h}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu}}\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot(x\partial_{y}(\mathbb{A}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})+i\nu\partial_{y}(\mathbb{T}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}))\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}L^{\nu}{\mathcal{H}}_{(2\nu)^{-1}}{\varphi}}.

We start by bounding
\mathcal{I}_{3,h}
. Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and
|x|<\nu
we obtain

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{3,h}|\leq\nu(\|\partial_{y}(\mathbb{A}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}+\|\partial_{y}(\mathbb{T}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})})\|\partial_{x}L^{\nu}\mathcal{H}_{(2\nu)^{-1}}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}\leq C\|f\|\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})},

(69)

where the desired bound follows by combining (
56
) and (
61
) with
\varepsilon=1/2
.

The quantity
\mathcal{I}_{3,l}
is further rewritten using the Plancherel identity (
\xi
is the Fourier variable in
y
-direction):

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{3,l}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu}}i\xi\left(x\mathcal{F}_{y}(\mathbb{A}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})+i\nu\mathcal{F}_{y}(\mathbb{T}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})\right)\,\overline{\mathcal{F}_{y}(\partial_{x}L^{\nu}\mathcal{L}_{(2\nu)^{-1}}\varphi)}dxd\xi,

and with the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and
|\xi|\leq(1+\xi^{2})^{1/2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{3,l}|\leq\|(1+\xi^{2})^{1/4}\left(x\mathcal{F}_{y}(\mathbb{A}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})+i\nu\mathcal{F}_{y}(\mathbb{T}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})\right)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}\|(1+\xi^{2})^{1/4}{\mathcal{F}_{y}(\partial_{x}L^{\nu}\mathcal{L}_{(2\nu)^{-1}}\varphi)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}.

With the definition of
\mathcal{J}
(see p.
50
), the Plancherel identity, and the bound
|x|<\nu
, we conclude that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{3,l}|\leq\nu(\|\mathcal{J}\mathbb{A}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}+\|\mathcal{J}\mathbb{T}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})})\,\|\mathcal{J}\partial_{x}L^{\nu}\mathcal{L}_{(2\nu)^{-1}}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}.

We use Proposition
4.12
to bound the terms involving
U^{\nu}_{\delta}
and Lemma
4.14
, more precisely, (
60
) with
\varepsilon=1/2
, for the term involving
\varphi
. This gives the upper bound

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{3,l}|\leq C(\|f\|+\sqrt{\|f\|\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|})\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})},

(70)

with
C>0
independent of
\nu
.
Combining (
69
) and (
70
) we obtain

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{3}|\lesssim(\|f\|+\sqrt{\|f\|\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|})\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}.

(71)

The final bound.
Combining the bounds (
71
), (
68
), (
67
) into (
66
) and next (
65
) yields

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}\partial_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}\lesssim\|f\|+\sqrt{\|f\|\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|}.

The desired bound in the statement of the proposition is immediate from the above and (
64
). ∎

4.3
An important jump property of the solutions to (
P

ν

): a weakened form of Theorem
2.5

Now that we have the result of Theorem
4.6
on the regularity of the Neumann trace of
u^{\nu}
on
\Sigma
, we can consider separately the problem satisfied by
u^{\nu}
in
\Omega_{p}
and
\Omega_{n}
, with the boundary condition
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}=g^{\nu}
. As
\nu\rightarrow 0+
, the couple
(u^{\nu},g^{\nu})
admits, up to a subsequence, a weak limit in the topology
\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)\times\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
. At this point we are not able to conclude about the uniqueness of the limit.
Nonetheless, we will be able apply the result of Theorem
3.9
to the
u^{+}_{k}
, which would yield the decomposition of
u^{+}_{k}
into a regular and singular parts. This approach will reveal one important property of the limiting absorption solution, namely, the relation between the jump across
\Sigma
of the regular part and the conormal trace. Recall (
17
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)=\{{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega):\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla v)\in L^{2}(\Omega),\,\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}v\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma),\,(\gamma_{n}^{\Gamma_{+}}+\gamma_{n}^{\Gamma_{-}})v=0\},

(72)

and Definition
2.10
of the trace of functions from the above space.

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
(u^{\nu})_{\nu>0}\subset\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
be a sequence of solutions to (
P

ν

). Then there exists a weakly convergent, as
\nu\rightarrow 0+
, subsequence in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. For any such subsequence
(u^{\nu_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, the following holds true.

As
\nu_{k}\rightarrow 0
, it converges strongly in
{H}^{1/2-\varepsilon}(\Omega)
for all
\varepsilon>0
to a limit
\widetilde{u}\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)
, which satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla\widetilde{u})=f\text{ in }\Omega,\\
&[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{u}]=-i\pi a_{11}^{-1}\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{u}.\end{split}

(73)

The above convergence statement is a weakened version of Theorem
2.5
, since it is valid up to a subsequence. The key, unusual property, is of course
[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{u}]=-i\pi a_{11}^{-1}\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{u}
, which, as we will see later, ensures the uniqueness of the limit of
(u^{\nu})_{\nu>0}
.

Remark 4.16
.

Up to our knowledge, this property was first observed in the work
[
8
]
; moreover, it was proven to hold true under the assumption that, as
\nu\rightarrow 0+
,
u^{\nu}\rightarrow u^{+}
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, where
u^{+}(x,y)=g(y)(\log|x|+i\pi\mathbb{1}_{x<0})+u^{+}_{cont}(x,y)
, with
g\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Sigma)
and
u^{+}_{cont}\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega)
. Unlike in quite an implicit proof of the respective result in
[
8
]
, in the approach of the present work this property of the traces comes out naturally, as a corollary of the fact that the solution with the absorption can be split as
u^{\nu}=u^{\nu}_{s}+u^{\nu}_{cont}
, with
u^{\nu}_{s}
converging to a function with in particular a logarithmic and jump singularities, and
u^{\nu}_{cont}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
to a regular function in the topology of
\mathcal{H}^{1-}(\Omega)
. See the proof of Theorem
4.15
for more details.

The proof of Theorem
4.15
is based on two auxiliary results, summarized in the following sesction.

4.3.1
Auxiliary results

The first result is a counterpart of Proposition
2.8
for the problem with absorption (
22
).

Proposition 4.17
.

There exists
\nu_{0}>0
, s.t. for any
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
, the solution to (
P

ν

) writes

\displaystyle u^{\nu}=u^{\nu}_{h}\log\left(x+i\nu r\right)+u^{\nu}_{cont},\qquad r:=\mathbb{T}_{11}(\mathbb{A}_{11})^{-1},

where, for all
0<\varepsilon<1/2
there exists
C_{\varepsilon}>0
independent of
\nu
, s.t.
\|u^{\nu}_{cont}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1-\varepsilon}(\Omega)}+\|u^{\nu}_{cont}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega)}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|f\|
, and there exists
C>0
independent of
\nu,\varepsilon,
s.t.
\|u^{\nu}_{h}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|
.

This result, in turn, relies on the following limiting absorption result, proven in Appendix
G
.

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
f\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
for some
0<\delta<1
, and let
(v^{\nu})_{\nu>0}\subset\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
be a family of the solutions to the well-posed problems parametrized by
\nu>0
: find
v^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
, s.t.

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A})\nabla v^{\nu})=f\text{ in }\Omega_{p},\qquad r=\mathbb{T}_{11}(\mathbb{A}_{11})^{-1},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}v^{\nu}:=\left.(x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla v^{\nu}\cdot\mathbf{n}\right|_{\Sigma}=0,

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}}v=0,\text{ periodic BCs at }\Gamma^{\pm}_{p}.

Then there exists
\nu_{0}>0
, s.t. for all
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
, all
0<\varepsilon<1
,
\|v^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta+\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})}
, with
C_{\varepsilon}>0
independent of
\nu
.

Proof of Proposition
4.17
.

Rewriting of the problem.
Before proving the desired result, we will rewrite the problem satisfied by
u^{\nu}
in a more convenient for us form. For this we will alter the original formulation:

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu})=\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu})+i\nu\operatorname{div}((\mathbb{T}-r\mathbb{A})\nabla u^{\nu}).

We set
f_{1}^{\nu}:=\operatorname{div}((\mathbb{T}-r\mathbb{A})\nabla u^{\nu})
, and remark that
(\mathbb{T}-r\mathbb{A})_{11}=0
, therefore

\displaystyle\|f_{1}^{\nu}\|\leq C\nu(\|\partial_{y}\nabla u^{\nu}\|+\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|+\|u^{\nu}\|)\lesssim\|f\|,

(74)

as follows from Proposition
4.13
and (
43
). This shows that

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu})=f-f_{1}^{\nu},\quad\|f-f_{1}^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|.

(75)

Then all the previous results apply to this new problem, see also Remark
4.1
, with the adapted conormal derivative
\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}:=\left.(x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}\cdot\mathbf{n}\right|_{\Sigma}=:\widetilde{g}^{\nu}
.

Decomposition.
We make an ansatz (cf. the decomposition of Proposition
2.8
):

\displaystyle u^{\nu}(x,y):=u^{\nu}_{s}+u^{\nu}_{cont},\qquad u^{\nu}_{s}(x,y):=u^{\nu}_{h}(x,y)\log\left(x+i\nu r(x,y)\right),

with
u^{\nu}_{h}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
being a unique solution to the decoupled boundary-value problem:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}(\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{h})=0\text{ in }\Omega\setminus\Sigma,\\
&\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}=a_{11}^{-1}\widetilde{g}^{\nu},\\
&\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}\cup\Gamma_{n}}u^{\nu}_{h}=0,\text{ periodic BCs at }\Gamma^{\pm}_{p}\cup\Gamma^{\pm}_{n},\end{split}

(76)

and
u^{\nu}_{cont}:=u^{\nu}-u^{\nu}_{h}
. The stated upper bound on
u^{\nu}_{h}
is a corollary of Theorem
4.6
applied to (
75
).

It remains to prove the bound on
u^{\nu}_{cont}
. For this we will write the problem satisfied by
u^{\nu}_{cont}
. To do so, we will need the one-sided conormal trace of
u^{\nu}_{h}
, associated to the problem (
76
), and defined for sufficiently regular functions via
\partial_{n}^{\Sigma,\lambda}v:=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,\lambda}(\mathbb{A}\nabla v)\cdot\mathbf{n}
,
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
. Remark that from the stated upper bound on
\|u^{\nu}_{h}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)}
it follows that

\displaystyle q^{\nu}_{\lambda}:=\partial_{n}^{\Sigma,\lambda}u^{\nu}_{h}\quad\text{ satisfies }\quad\|q^{\nu}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\|f\|,\quad\lambda\in\{n,p\}.

(77)

Let us now study the
problem satisfied by
u^{\nu}_{cont}
.
A priori,
\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{cont})\notin L^{2}(\Omega)
, since the jump of the conormal trace of
u^{\nu}_{cont}
across
\Sigma
may not vanish. Therefore, we will write a decoupled problem satisfied by
u^{\nu}_{cont}
, on
\Omega_{p}
and
\Omega_{n}
separately. In particular, on
\Omega\setminus\Sigma
, using that
\operatorname{div}(\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{h})=0
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{cont})

\displaystyle=f-f^{\nu}_{1}-\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{s})

\displaystyle=f-f^{\nu}_{1}-\operatorname{div}(\mathbb{A}(\mathbf{e}_{x}+i\nu\nabla r)u^{\nu}_{h})-\nabla((x+i\nu r)\log(x+i\nu r))\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{h}=:f^{\nu}.

(78)

By a straightforward computation, with
\|u^{\nu}_{h}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
and (
74
), it follows that for all
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\|f^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{\lambda})}\leq C_{\delta}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},\quad\lambda\in\{n,p\}.

(79)

Next, since
u^{\nu}_{cont}
satisfies the decoupled problem (
78
) in
\Omega\setminus\Sigma
, we need to provide it with the boundary condition on
\Sigma
, which would render it well-posed. We choose the Neumann boundary condition, adapted to (
78
). Remark that

\displaystyle(x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{s}=\mathbb{A}(\mathbf{e}_{x}+i\nu\nabla r)u^{\nu}_{h}+(x+i\nu r)\log(x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{h},

hence, using the fact that
\partial_{n}^{\Sigma,\lambda}u^{\nu}_{h}\in\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)
, one can check that the following identity holds in
\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma,\lambda}u^{\nu}_{s}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{A}(\mathbf{e}_{x}+i\nu\nabla r)\cdot\mathbf{e}_{x}\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,\lambda}u^{\nu}_{h}+i\nu r\log(i\nu r)\partial_{n}^{\Sigma,\lambda}u^{\nu}_{h}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{g}^{\nu}(1+i\nu\gamma)+i\nu r\log(i\nu r)q^{\nu}_{\lambda},\quad\gamma:=a_{11}^{-1}\left.\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla r\right|_{\Sigma}.

Since
u^{\nu}_{cont}=u^{\nu}-u^{\nu}_{s}
, we conclude that
u^{\nu}_{cont}
satisfies the following two-sided Neumann BC on
\Sigma
,
0<\nu<1/2
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma,\lambda}u^{\nu}_{cont}=\nu|\log\nu|e^{\nu}_{\lambda},\text{ where }e^{\nu}_{\lambda}=-i|\log\nu|^{-1}\left(\gamma\widetilde{g}^{\nu}-ir\log(i\nu r)q^{\nu}_{\lambda}\right)\in\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\quad\lambda\in\{n,p\}.

(80)

Crucially, from Theorem
4.6
and (
77
), it follows that for all
\nu<1/2
,

\displaystyle\|e^{\nu}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\|f\|.

(81)

Thus, we have shown that
u^{\nu}_{cont}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)
solves the following decoupled Neumann problem: with
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}u^{\nu}_{cont})=f^{\nu}\text{ in }\Omega_{\lambda},

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma,\lambda}u^{\nu}_{cont}=\nu|\log\nu|e^{\nu}_{\lambda},

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{\lambda}}u=0,\quad\text{periodic BCs at }\Gamma^{\pm}_{\lambda}.

The above problem is well-posed in
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)
, by the same argument as used in Lemma
2.4
.

The bound on
u^{\nu}_{cont}
: splitting.

We split
u^{\nu}_{cont}=u^{\nu}_{cont,0}+s^{\nu}
, and bound the two terms separately. Here
u^{\nu}_{cont,0},\,s^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)
are unique solutions to the problems: for
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{cont,0})=f^{\nu}\text{ in }\Omega_{\lambda},\\
&\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma,\lambda}u^{\nu}_{cont}=0,\qquad{\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{\lambda}}u^{\nu}_{cont,0}=0,}\text{ periodic BCs at }\Gamma^{\pm}_{\lambda},\end{split}

(82)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla s^{\nu})=0\text{ in }\Omega_{\lambda},\\
&\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma,\lambda}s^{\nu}=\nu|\log\nu|e^{\nu}_{\lambda},\qquad{\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{\lambda}}s^{\nu}=0,}\text{ periodic BCs at }\Gamma^{\pm}_{\lambda}.\end{split}

(83)

We will obtain the bounds on
u^{\nu}_{cont,0}
and
s^{\nu}
separately in
\Omega_{n}
and
\Omega_{p}
, and next argue that they imply the global bound on
u^{\nu}_{cont}
.

A bound on
u^{\nu}_{cont,0}
.
From Proposition
4.18
and (
79
), it follows that for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
C_{\varepsilon}>0
,

\displaystyle\|u^{\nu}_{cont,0}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})}+\|u^{\nu}_{cont,0}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega_{n})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|f\|.

(84)

A bound on
s^{\nu}
.
We start by proving the following two auxiliary statements, and next proceed by a very simple, interpolation-like argument:

\displaystyle(a)\;\|\nabla s^{\nu}\|_{\Omega_{\lambda}}\lesssim\log\nu^{-1}\|f\|_{\Omega},\quad(b)\;\||x|^{1/2}\nabla s^{\nu}\|_{\Omega_{\lambda}}\lesssim\nu^{1/2}\log\nu^{-1}\|f\|_{\Omega},\qquad\lambda\in\{n,p\}.

(85)

It suffices to prove these estimates in
\Omega_{p}
. We test (
83
) with
s^{\nu}
and integrate by parts. This yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Omega_{p}}(x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla s^{\nu}\cdot\overline{\nabla s^{\nu}}=-\nu|\log\nu|\langle e^{\nu}_{p},\overline{\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}s^{\nu}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}.\end{split}

(86)

Taking the imaginary part of the above shows that

\displaystyle\nu\|\nabla s^{\nu}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\nu|\log\nu|\|e^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}\|\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}s^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\nu|\log\nu|\|f\|\|\nabla s^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})},

where the last bound follows from (
81
), continuity of
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}
on
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
and the Poincaré inequality in
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
. This yields (
85
)(a). The bound (
85
)(b) is obtained by taking the real part of (
86
) and proceeding like before:

\displaystyle\|x^{1/2}\nabla s^{\nu}\|^{2}\lesssim\nu|\log\nu|\|e^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}\|\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}s^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\nu|\log\nu|\|f\|\|\nabla s^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\nu\log^{2}\nu\|f\|^{2},

where the last bound follows from (
85
)(a).
The bounds (
85
)(a), (b) imply similar bounds in the weighted space
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})
. Indeed, for
0<\delta<1
, with
\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\nu}=\{(x,y)\in\Omega:\,|x|<\nu\}
, and for
\nu<1/2
,

\displaystyle\|x^{\delta/2}\nabla s^{\nu}\|^{2}_{\Omega_{p}}=\int_{\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\nu}\cap\Omega_{p}}x^{\delta}|\nabla s^{\nu}|^{2}+\int_{\Omega_{p}\setminus\overline{\Omega}_{\Sigma}^{\nu}}x^{\delta-1}x|\nabla s^{\nu}|^{2}\leq\nu^{\delta}\|\nabla s^{\nu}\|^{2}_{\Omega_{p}}+\nu^{\delta-1}\|x^{1/2}\nabla s^{\nu}\|^{2}_{\Omega_{p}}\lesssim\nu^{\delta}\log^{2}\nu\|f\|^{2},

as follows from (
85
)(a), (b). By the Poincaré inequality in weighted spaces, cf. e.g. Proposition
B.4
, and with the above bound, we conclude that

\displaystyle\|s^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\delta}^{1}(\Omega_{p})}+\|s^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\delta}^{1}(\Omega_{n})}\leq C_{\delta}\nu^{\delta/2}\log\nu^{-1}\|f\|.

(87)

Final bound on
u^{\nu}_{cont}
.
We combine the bounds (
84
) and (
87
) to conclude that, since, in particular, for all
\nu>0
,
u^{\nu}_{cont}=u^{\nu}-u^{\nu}_{h}\log(x+i\nu r)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
(as
u^{\nu}_{h}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
), it holds that

\displaystyle\|u^{\nu}_{cont}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega)}\leq\|u^{\nu}_{cont}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})}+\|u^{\nu}_{cont}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega_{n})}\leq\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\{n,p\}}(\|u^{\nu}_{cont,0}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega_{\lambda})}+\|s^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega_{\lambda})})\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

for all
\varepsilon>0
, with some
C_{\varepsilon}>0
independent of
\nu
but depending on
\varepsilon
. This proves one part of the bound. To prove the fractional space bound, we resort
to Lemma
B.7
; for all
0<\varepsilon<1/2
, there exists
c_{\varepsilon}>0
, s.t.

\displaystyle\|u^{\nu}_{cont}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1-\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})}+\|u^{\nu}_{cont}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1-\varepsilon}(\Omega_{n})}\leq c_{\varepsilon}\|f\|.

Because additionally
u^{\nu}_{cont}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
, we have that
[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}_{cont}]=0
. By
[
18
, Lemma 1.5.1.8]
or adapting the proof of a more detailed result
[
23
, Theorem 2.4]
, we conclude that
\|u^{\nu}_{cont}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1-\varepsilon}(\Omega)}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|f\|,

with some
C_{\varepsilon}>0
depending on
\varepsilon>0
but independent of
\nu
.
∎

A rather immediate corollary of the bounds of Proposition
4.17
is

Corollary 4.19
.

There exists
\nu_{0}>0
, s.t. for all
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
,

\displaystyle\|u^{\nu}\|_{H^{1/2-\varepsilon/2}(\Omega)}\leq c_{\varepsilon}\|u^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{1+\varepsilon}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},\quad\text{ for all }0<\varepsilon<1,

where
c_{\varepsilon},\,C_{\varepsilon}>0
depend on
\varepsilon>0
but are independent of
\nu
.

Proof.

By
[
23
, Theorem 2.4]
it is sufficient to prove the corresponding fractional Sobolev estimates separately in subdomains
\Omega_{p}
and
\Omega_{n}
, and by Lemma
B.7
, it is sufficient to show the stated estimates in the weighted Sobolev spaces only. In
\Omega_{p}
, using the decomposition of Proposition
4.17
,

\displaystyle x^{\frac{1+\varepsilon}{2}}\nabla u^{\nu}=\frac{x^{\frac{1+\varepsilon}{2}}}{x+i\nu r}(\mathbf{e}_{x}+i\nu\nabla r)u^{\nu}_{h}+x^{\frac{1+\varepsilon}{2}}\log(x+i\nu)\nabla u^{\nu}_{h}+x^{\frac{1+\varepsilon}{2}}\nabla u^{\nu}_{cont}.

By the same result, the last two terms in the right-hand side are bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
uniformly in
\nu
. The first term is bounded pointwise by
Cx^{-(1-\varepsilon)/2}|u^{\nu}_{h}|
, with
C
independent of
\nu
,
and the desired inequality follows by Proposition
B.4
.
∎

4.3.2
Proof of Theorem
4.15

Convergence.
The existence of the weakly convergent in
L^{2}(\Omega)
subsequence follows from Theorem
4.2
. Assume now that
(u^{\nu_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
to the limit
\widetilde{u}
. Its weak convergence in
\mathcal{H}^{1/2-\varepsilon}(\Omega)
for any
\varepsilon>0
is immediate from Corollary
4.19
and the fact that
u^{\nu_{k}}
admits a single limit point
\widetilde{u}
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

The stated strong convergence in
\mathcal{H}^{1/2-\varepsilon}(\Omega)
for any
\varepsilon>0
follows by the compact embedding of Sobolev spaces
\mathcal{H}^{1/2-\varepsilon}(\Omega)
into
\mathcal{H}^{1/2-\varepsilon/2}(\Omega)
.

Let us now prove that
\widetilde{u}\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)
, cf. (
72
). We will prove this alongside with showing that
\widetilde{u}
satisfies the first identity in (
73
). In virtue of (
38
), we also have that
u^{\nu}\rightharpoonup\widetilde{u}
in
\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)
. The standard argument about convergence of distributional derivatives, separately in
\Omega_{p}
and
\Omega_{n}
, yields

x\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu_{k}}\rightharpoonup x\mathbb{A}\nabla\widetilde{u}\quad\text{in}\quad L^{2}(\Omega_{p})\text{ and }L^{2}(\Omega_{n}).

Since
\tilde{u}\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)
, by Proposition
B.6
,
x\mathbb{A}\nabla\widetilde{u}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
. Therefore, using (
38
), as
\nu_{k}\rightarrow 0+
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{\nu_{k}}:=(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu_{k}}\overset{L^{2}(\Omega)}{\rightharpoonup}\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}:=x\mathbb{A}\nabla\widetilde{u}.

(88)

Since
\operatorname{div}\mathbf{v}^{\nu_{k}}=f
in
\Omega
, and using (
196
), necessarily,
\operatorname{div}\mathbf{v}^{\nu_{k}}=\operatorname{div}\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}.

Next, by continuity of the normal trace mapping
\mathcal{H}(\operatorname{div};\Omega)\ni\mathbf{h}\mapsto\left.\mathbf{h}\cdot\mathbf{n}\right|_{\Sigma}\in\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)
, the above weak convergence results, and Theorem
4.6
we conclude
\gamma_{n,\nu_{k}}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu_{k}}\rightharpoonup\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{u}
in
\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
.

The above considerations and continuity of the normal trace prove that
\widetilde{u}\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)
satisfies

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla\widetilde{u})=f\text{ in }\Omega,\qquad\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{u}\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma),

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}\cup\Gamma_{n}}\widetilde{u}=0,\qquad\text{periodic BCs at }\Gamma^{\pm}.

This shows that
\widetilde{u}\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)
.

The jump property of
\widetilde{u}
.
It remains to argue that
[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{u}]=-i\pi a_{11}^{-1}\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{u}
. Recalling Definition
3.13
of the Dirichlet trace, to prove this property we need to construct the decomposition of
\widetilde{u}
into the singular and regular parts, as defined in Theorem
3.9
. On the other hand, we have at hand the decomposition of Proposition
4.17
, which was constructed in a slightly different manner compared to (
29
), see the proofs of the corresponding results. Nonetheless, Lemma
3.15
enables us to make use of this modified decomposition.

We remark that, with notation of Proposition
4.17
,
\|u^{\nu_{k}}_{h}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)}
, and
\|u^{\nu_{k}}_{cont}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega)}
are uniformly bounded in
\nu_{k}\rightarrow 0
(for all
0<\varepsilon<1/2
). This shows that, up to a subsequence, as
\nu_{k}\rightarrow 0
, for all
0<\varepsilon<1/2
,

\displaystyle u^{\nu_{k}}_{h}\overset{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)}{\rightharpoonup}\widetilde{u}_{h},\qquad u^{\nu_{k}}_{cont}\overset{\mathcal{H}^{1-\varepsilon}(\Omega)}{\rightharpoonup}\widetilde{u}_{cont},\qquad u^{\nu_{k}}_{h}\overset{\mathcal{H}^{1-\varepsilon}(\Omega)}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{u}_{h},\qquad u^{\nu_{k}}_{cont}\overset{\mathcal{H}^{1-\varepsilon}(\Omega)}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{u}_{cont},

(89)

where we used in the last statements the compactness of the embeddings
H^{s}(\Omega)\subset H^{s+\varepsilon}(\Omega)
, for
\varepsilon>0
.

Since, additionally,
u^{\nu_{k}}
,
u^{\nu_{k}}_{h}
and
u_{h}^{\nu_{k}}
admit subsequences that converge pointwise (see
[
5
, Theorem 4.9]
) to their
L^{2}(\Omega)
-strong limits, we pass to the pointwise limit in the decomposition of Proposition
4.17
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}=\widetilde{u}_{h}\lim\limits_{\nu\rightarrow 0+}\log(x+i\nu r)+\widetilde{u}_{cont}=\widetilde{u}_{h}(\log|x|+i\pi\mathbb{1}_{x<0})+\widetilde{u}_{cont}.

(90)

By (
89
) and uniqueness of the limits, we have obtained the decomposition of
\widetilde{u}
whose restrictions to
\Omega_{p}
(resp.
\Omega_{n}
) satisfy conditions of Lemma
3.15
. Application of Lemma
3.15
shows that

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma,\lambda}\widetilde{u}=a_{11}\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,\lambda}\widetilde{u}_{h},\quad\lambda\in\{n,p\},\quad\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}\widetilde{u}=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}\widetilde{u}_{cont},\qquad\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,n}\widetilde{u}=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,n}\widetilde{u}_{cont}+i\pi\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,n}\widetilde{u}_{h}.

The regularity of the limits (
89
) implies that, cf. Lemma 1.5.1.8 of
[
18
]
for fractional Sobolev spaces,

[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{u}_{h}]=0,\,[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{u}_{cont}]=0,

and hence the assertion of the theorem.

Remark 4.20
.

Remark that (
90
) written for
\nu\rightarrow 0-
yields the decomposition
\widetilde{u}_{h}(\log|x|-i\pi\mathbb{1}_{x<0})+\widetilde{u}_{cont}
, and the jump condition
[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\tilde{u}]=i\pi\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}\tilde{u}
.

5
The limiting problem. Proofs of Theorems
2.5
and
2.11

We start by defining a well-posed problem satisfied by the limiting absorption solution. To state it, we will make use of the trace jump property observed in Theorem
4.15
. The first result states that the jump property ensures the uniqueness of the associated transmission problem.

Proposition 5.1

(Uniqueness)
.

Assume that
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)=0\text{ in }\Omega,\\
&[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u]=-i\pi a_{11}^{-1}\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u.\end{split}

(91)

Then
u=0
.

Remark 5.2
.

In the above, we require that
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
(cf. the definition of the space (
72
)), since the notion of
[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}.]
introduced in Definition
2.10
relies on the decomposition of Proposition
2.8
, which we have proven only in the case when
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
.

The results of Theorems
2.5
and
2.11
follow from Proposition
5.1
and Theorem
4.15
at once.

Proof of Theorem
2.5
.

By Theorem
4.15

(u^{\nu})_{\nu>0}
admits a subsequence that converges
\mathcal{H}^{1/2-\varepsilon}(\Omega)
strongly, as
\nu\rightarrow 0+
, to some
u^{+}\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)
.

Assume now that
(u^{\nu})_{\nu>0}
admits two
L^{2}(\Omega)
-weakly convergent as
\nu\rightarrow 0
subsequences that converge to different limits
u_{1}\neq u_{2}
. In virtue of Theorem
4.15
, we see that
u_{j}\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A});\Omega)
satisfies

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{j})=f,\qquad[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{j}]=-i\pi a_{11}^{-1}\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{j},\quad j=1,2.

Therefore,
d=u_{1}-u_{2}
solves the problem (
91
), and by Proposition
5.1
,
d=0
. This shows that, as
\nu\rightarrow 0
, the whole family
u^{\nu}
necessarily converges to
u^{+}
in
\mathcal{H}^{1/2-\varepsilon}(\Omega)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.11
.

Follows by the application of Theorem
2.5
, Theorem
4.15
and the uniqueness result of Proposition
5.1
. The stated stability result, cf. Remark
2.12
, follows from these results as well.
∎

It remains to prove Proposition
5.1
.
Our proof is inspired by a similar result in
[
8
]
, where uniqueness was proven for a non-standard variational formulation of
[
33
]
. We use a similar approach to prove uniqueness for (
91
).
The following result follows at once from the Green’s formula of Theorem
3.14
.

Corollary 5.3
.

For all
u,v\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)
, the following integration by parts formula holds true:

\displaystyle(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u),v)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-(u,\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla v))_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=-\langle\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u,\overline{[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}v]}\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}+\overline{\langle\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}v,\overline{[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u]}\rangle}_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}.

(92)

Proof.

This result follows by a direct computation from Theorem
3.14
: we apply it to
\Omega_{p}
and
\Omega_{n}
and sum up the obtained identities; recall that for the elements
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)
,
[\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u]=0
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Applying (
92
) with
v=u
yields

\displaystyle 2i\operatorname{Im}\langle\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u,[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u]\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=0.

Since
[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u]=-i\pi a_{11}^{-1}\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u
the above implies
\int_{\Sigma}|\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u|^{2}a_{11}^{-1}=0,

hence
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u=0
. Then
\left.u\right|_{\Omega_{\lambda}}\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{\lambda})
,
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
, satisfies the homogeneuos Neumann problem (
22
) with vanishing data.
By Theorem
3.1
,
u=0
.
∎

6
Results for a domain with a hole and extension to more general cases

In this section we argue how the results of the previous sections can be extended to different geometries, first for a particular case of geometries with a hole and
\omega=0
, and next comment on the results for
\omega\neq 0
and a more general class of geometries (see Section
6.2
).

6.1
A domain with a hole,
\omega=0

We are given
\Omega_{ext}
and
\Omega_{int}
, two bounded Lipschitz simply connected domains in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, s.t.
\overline{\Omega}_{int}\subset\Omega_{ext}
; we assume
\Omega_{int}\neq\emptyset
(see Remark
6.8
). With their help, we define a domain
D:=\Omega_{ext}\setminus\overline{\Omega_{int}}
. Let
I
be a
C^{3}
-loop inside
D
without self-intersections, which surrounds
\Omega_{int}
, as shown in Figure
2
. This loop divides
D
into two
C^{2,1}
-subdomains
D_{p}
and
D_{n}
. Let
I_{p},I_{n}
be the outer and inner boundaries of
D_{p}
and
D_{n}
respectively:

\displaystyle I_{p}:=\partial D_{p}\setminus I,\quad I_{n}:=\partial D_{n}\setminus I.

(93)

On the loop
I
we define a unit normal
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{n}_{I}
, which is oriented from
D_{n}
into
D_{p}
.

I
D_{n}
D_{p}

Figure 2:
Illustration to the geometric configuration of Section
6.1
.

Now that the necessary geometrical preliminaries have been defined, let us introduce the coefficients of the model. In particular, let
\alpha:\,D\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfy the following assumptions.

Assumption 6.1
.

•

\alpha\in C^{1,1}(\overline{D};\mathbb{R})

•

\alpha>0
in
\overline{D_{p}}\setminus I
and
\alpha<0
in
\overline{D_{n}}\setminus I
;

•

there exists an open neighborhood
U_{I}
of
I
, s.t.

\displaystyle\alpha(\mathbf{x}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left|\operatorname{dist}(\mathbf{x},I)\right|,&\mathbf{x}\in D_{p}\cap U_{I},\\
-\left|\operatorname{dist}(\mathbf{x},I)\right|,&\mathbf{x}\in D_{n}\cap U_{I}.\end{array}\right.

Let us remark that the above definition is compatible with the regularity of
\alpha
, as argued in
[
26
]
or
[
20
, the proof of Lemma 14.6]
.

We are given two matrix-valued functions that satisfy the following assumptions.

Assumption 6.2
.

•

\mathbb{H},\,\mathbb{N}\in{C}^{1,1}(\overline{D};\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
.

•

For all
\mathbf{x}\in\overline{D}
,
\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{x}),\mathbb{N}(\mathbf{x})
are both Hermitian, positive-definite matrices. In particular,

\displaystyle\text{ for all }\mathbf{x}\in\overline{D},\,\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{C}^{2},\quad\mathbb{N}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{p}\cdot\overline{\mathbf{p}}\geq c_{\mathbb{N}}|\mathbf{p}|^{2},\quad\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{p}\cdot\overline{\mathbf{p}}\geq c_{\mathbb{H}}|\mathbf{p}|^{2},\quad c_{\mathbb{N}},\,c_{\mathbb{H}}>0.

•

by
\mathbb{H}_{I}
, resp.
\mathbb{N}_{I}
, we will denote the values of
\mathbb{H}
, resp.
\mathbb{N}
on the interface
I
.

We do not repeat the rest of the notations, recalling however that
\gamma_{n}^{I}u=\left.\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla u\right|_{I}\cdot\mathbf{n}
and
\gamma_{n,\nu}^{I}u=\left.(\alpha\mathbb{H}+i\nu\mathbb{N})\nabla u\right|_{I}\cdot\mathbf{n}.

We consider the following BVP. Given
f\in L^{2}(D)
, let
(v^{\nu})_{\nu>0}\subset H^{1}_{0}(D)
solve

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}((\alpha\mathbb{H}+i\nu\mathbb{N})\nabla v^{\nu})=0\text{ in }D,\\
&\gamma_{0}v^{\nu}=0.\end{split}

(96)

The following result is proven just like Lemma
2.4
.

Lemma 6.3
.

For each
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
,
\nu>0
, the problem (
96
) admits a unique solution
v^{\nu}\in{H}^{1}_{0}(D)
, which also belongs to
H^{2}(D)
.

To state a generalization of the results of Section
2.2
, let us introduce the following spaces, for
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(D_{\lambda})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{v\in L^{2}(D_{\lambda}):\||\alpha|^{\delta/2}\nabla v\|<\infty,\gamma_{0}^{I_{\lambda}}v=0\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{reg}(D_{\lambda})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{v\in L^{2}(D_{\lambda}):\||\alpha|^{1/2}\nabla v\|<\infty,\,\gamma_{0}^{I_{\lambda}}v=0\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{sing}(D_{\lambda})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{v\in L^{2}(D_{\lambda}):\|\alpha\nabla v\|<\infty,\gamma_{0}^{I_{\lambda}}v=0\},

and set

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{reg}(D)=\mathcal{V}_{reg}(D_{n})\times\mathcal{V}_{reg}(D_{p}),\quad\mathcal{V}_{sing}(D)=\mathcal{V}_{sing}(D_{n})\times\mathcal{V}_{sing}(D_{p}).

Additionally, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla.);D):=\{v\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(D):\,\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla v)\in L^{2}(D),\,\gamma_{n}^{I}v\in H^{1/2}(I)\}.

Then the following counterparts of the results of Section
2.2
hold true.

Theorem 6.4

(Limiting absorption principle)
.

Given
f\in L^{2}(D)
, consider the family of solutions to (
96
)
(v^{\nu})_{\nu>0}\subset H^{1}_{0}(D)
. Then, as
\nu\rightarrow 0+
,
v^{\nu}\rightarrow v^{+}
in
\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}H^{1/2-\varepsilon}(D)
, and
v^{+}\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla.);D)
.

Like before, the function
v^{+}
as defined in the above theorem will be referred to as a ’limiting absorption solution’.
Let us state a counterpart of Proposition
2.8
. For this let us define following function on
I
:

\displaystyle h_{I}:=\left.\mathbf{n}_{I}\cdot\mathbb{H}\nabla\alpha\right|_{I}=\mathbf{n}_{I}\cdot\mathbb{H}_{I}\mathbf{n}_{I},

(97)

where we used that, since
\alpha
is a signed distance in the vicinity of the interface, necessarily,

\displaystyle\left.\nabla\alpha\right|_{I}=\mathbf{n}_{I},

(98)

see
[
20
, proof of Lemma 14.16]
. The function
h_{I}
plays a role of
a_{11}
in the previous sections.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
v\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla.);D)
.
Then
u
can be decomposed in a unique manner as follows:

\displaystyle v=v_{h}\log|\alpha|+v_{reg},

(99)

where
v_{h}\in H^{1}_{0}(D)
is a piecewise-
\mathbb{H}
-harmonic function that satisfies

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(\mathbb{H}\nabla v_{h})=0\text{ in }D\setminus I,

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{I}v_{h}=h_{I}^{-1}\gamma_{n}^{I}v,

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{I_{p}\cup I_{n}}v_{h}=0,

and
v_{reg}\in{H}^{1-}(D\setminus I)\bigcap\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(D\setminus I)
.

Next, for functions like in Proposition
6.5
, we denote by
v_{reg,\lambda}=\left.v_{reg}\right|_{D_{\lambda}}
. The regular part of such functions carries the Dirichlet trace.

Definition 6.6
.

Let
v
be like in Proposition
6.5
. We define the one-sided trace of
\left.v\right|_{D_{\lambda}}
,
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
, on
I
as a trace of its regular part:

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{I,\lambda}v:=\gamma_{0}^{I,\lambda}v_{reg,\lambda}\in H^{1/2-}(I),\quad\lambda\in\{n,p\}.

The jump of the traces is then defined via
[\gamma_{0}^{I}v]:=\gamma_{0}^{I,p}v-\gamma_{0}^{I,n}v.

Like before, the notion of trace enables us to single out the limiting absorption solution among all the functions
v\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla.);D)
that satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla v)=f\text{ in }D.\end{split}

(100)

The following result is the second main result of this section.

Theorem 6.7
.

Given
f\in L^{2}(D)
, the limiting absorption solution
v^{+}
as defined in Theorem
6.4
is a unique solution to the following well-posed problem: find
v\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla.);D)
that satisfies (
100
) and

\displaystyle[\gamma_{0}^{I}v]=-i\pi h_{I}^{-1}\gamma_{n}^{I}v.

(101)

The proofs of the statements of Propositions
2.8
, Theorem
2.5
,
2.11
in the domain
\Omega
can be quite easily adapted to a more general geometric setting. An argument on why this can be done is the following.

First of all, our previous
L^{2}(\Omega)
-stability estimates on
u^{\nu}
are based on the integration by parts arguments, well-posedness and regularity results for the homogeneous decoupled Neumann problem and quite delicate lifting lemmas, cf. the proof of Theorem
4.2
. The lifting lemmas are easily adapted by a change of coordinates to the curve
I
; well-posedness and regularity for the homogeneous Neumann problem are taken from
[
4
]
. This enables us to obtain the estimate
\|v^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{sing}(D)}\lesssim\|f\|
.

Once this estimate is obtained, we localize the problem in the vicinity of the interface
I
with the help of the cut-off function
\chi
to
\operatorname{div}(\alpha\nabla(\chi v))=f_{\chi}
, with
\|f_{\chi}\|\lesssim\|v\|_{\mathcal{V}_{sing}}+\|f\|\lesssim\|f\|
, and next perform an appropriate change of coordinates to get back to the problem of Section
2.1
, and repeat all the regularity derivations for this problem, including decompositions of the functions from
\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla.);\Omega)
into a regular and a singular parts. Since, compared to the results of the previous sections, only technical modifications are necessary, all the required derivations and modifications are moved to Appendix
I
.

Remark 6.8
.

The assumption on
\Omega_{int}\neq\emptyset
is important, since it ensures that the homogeneous Neumann problem for
\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{N}\nabla u)=f
in
D_{p}
is well-posed, due to the additional Dirichlet boundary condition on
I_{p}
.

6.2
Case
\omega\neq 0
, other types of domains

The results of the previous section translate verbatim to the case when one replaces
\operatorname{div}((\alpha\mathbb{H}+i\nu\mathbb{N})\nabla v^{\nu})=f
by
\operatorname{div}((\alpha\mathbb{H}+i\nu\mathbb{N})\nabla v^{\nu})+\omega^{2}v^{\nu}=f
in (
96
), and the domain
D_{I}
by a simply connected domain, provided that
\omega
satisfies the following assumption.

Assumption 6.9
.

Assume that
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
is s.t. the following problem is well-posed, for
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
. Given
f\in L^{2}(D_{\lambda})
, find
u_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(D_{\lambda})
s.t.
\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{\lambda})+\omega^{2}u_{\lambda}=f\text{ in }D_{\lambda},
and
\gamma_{0}^{\partial D_{\lambda}\setminus I}u_{\lambda}=0
.

Because the space
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(D_{\lambda})
is compactly embedded into
L^{2}(D_{\lambda})
(this follows e.g. from Lemma
B.7
and compact embedding of
H^{s}
into
L^{2}
,
s>0
), and the problem can be written in a self-adjoint form, there exists only a discrete number of frequencies, for which it is not well-posed. See
[
4
]
for more details.

The above assumption ensures in particular that Proposition
6.5
holds true provided a non-vanishing zero order term, since the corresponding problem with the frequency
\omega\neq 0
admits a unique solution, see Remark
6.8
.

A
Derivation of the model (
1
)

The following presentation is due to Stix
[
39
]
, and Freidberg
[
15
, Sections 15.5, 15.6]
.
Time-harmonic electromagnetic wave propagation in one-species cold magnetized plasma under the magnetic field
\boldsymbol{B}_{0}=(0,0,B_{0})
is described by the Maxwell’s equations (with an appropriate rescaling, so that
\varepsilon_{0}=\mu_{0}=c=1
)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\textbf{curl}}\mathbf{E}-i\omega\mathbf{B}=0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\textbf{curl}}\mathbf{B}+i\omega\uuline{\varepsilon}(\omega)\mathbf{E}=0,\qquad\omega>0,

with the cold plasma dielectric tensor

\displaystyle\uuline{\varepsilon}=\left(\begin{matrix}S&-iD&0\\
iD&S&0\\
0&0&P\end{matrix}\right),\text{ where }S=1-\frac{\omega_{p}^{2}}{\omega^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2}},\quad D=\frac{\omega_{c}}{\omega}\frac{\omega_{p}^{2}}{\omega^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2}},\;P=1-\frac{\omega_{p}^{2}}{\omega^{2}}.

Here
\omega_{c}=c_{c}B_{0}
is an algebraic cyclotron frequency, while
\omega_{p}=c_{p}\mathcal{N}
is a plasma frequency; the constants
c_{c},\,c_{p}\in\mathbb{R}
depend only on the nature of the particles the plasma is comprised of, while
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{x})
is the plasma density. Variations in the plasma density in space
\mathbf{x}
can lead to various degeneracies of the above model.

The above PDE is posed in a bounded smooth domain
\mathcal{D}_{3D}
, and equipped with appropriate boundary conditions. One could think of
\mathcal{D}_{3D}
being a tore (tokamak).

The model presented above does not take into account damping due to collisions, which occur with the collision frequency
\nu>0
(i.e. we took
\nu=0
to derive the above); to account for them, it suffices to replace in
\uuline{\varepsilon}
the following quantities, see
[
39
, p.38]
,

\displaystyle\omega_{p}^{2}\rightarrow\omega_{p}^{2}\frac{\omega}{\omega_{\nu}},\quad\omega_{c}\rightarrow\omega_{c}\frac{\omega}{\omega_{\nu}},\qquad\omega_{\nu}:=\omega+i\nu.

We will denote the corresponding tensor by
\uuline{\varepsilon}^{\nu}
and use the index
\nu
in
S^{\nu}
,
D^{\nu}
,
P^{\nu}
.

We are interested in the situation when the fields do not depend on the
z
-variable. The field
(E_{1},\,E_{2},\,B_{3})
then satisfies the 2D Maxwell equations in a domain
\mathcal{D}
(e.g. a cut of
\mathcal{D}_{3D}
with a plane
z=0
):

\displaystyle\operatorname{curl}\mathbf{E}_{\perp}-i\omega B_{3}=0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbf{curl}}_{\perp}B_{3}+i\omega\uuline{\varepsilon_{\perp}}\mathbf{E}_{\perp}=0,

with

\displaystyle\uuline{\varepsilon_{\perp}}=\left(\begin{matrix}S&-iD\\
iD&S\end{matrix}\right).

As usual in the 2D case, we can rewrite the above in a more convenient, scalar, form

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}_{\perp}(\uuline{\varepsilon_{\perp}}^{-1}\nabla B_{3})+\omega^{2}B_{3}=0,

(102)

with

\displaystyle\uuline{\alpha}:=\uuline{\varepsilon_{\perp}}^{-1}=\frac{1}{S^{2}-D^{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}S&iD\\
-iD&S\end{matrix}\right).

(103)

In what follows, we will assume that
\uuline{\alpha}
is well-defined everywhere in the domain
\mathcal{D}
.

Assumption A.1

(
\uuline{\alpha}
is well-defined)
.

It holds that
S^{2}\neq D^{2}\text{ in }\mathcal{D}
.

Recall now that all the coefficients in the above depend on frequency, as well as on the spatial variable
\mathbf{x}
, through the plasma density.
An interesting situation occurs when
S=S(\mathbf{x})=0
on a given curve
I\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
D=D(\mathbf{x})\neq 0
.
Because the dependence on
\mathbf{x}
manifests through the plasma frequency
\omega_{p}
only, the level sets of
S
coincide with the level sets of
D
; let us denote
\left.D\right|_{I}:=D_{I}=\operatorname{const}
. Let us remark at this point that
D=\frac{\omega_{c}}{\omega}(1-S)=D_{I}(1-S)
, and it holds that

\displaystyle S^{-1}(D-D_{I})=-\frac{\omega_{c}}{\omega}=-D_{I}.

(104)

The above rewriting allows to decompose

\displaystyle\uuline{\alpha}=\frac{1}{S^{2}-D^{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}S&iD_{I}-iD_{I}S\\
-iD_{I}+iD_{I}S&S\end{matrix}\right)=-\frac{1}{D_{I}^{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}0&iD_{I}\\
-iD_{I}&0\end{matrix}\right)+\uuline{r},

\displaystyle\uuline{r}:=\frac{S}{S^{2}-D^{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}1&i\frac{S+DD_{I}}{D_{I}}\\
-i\frac{S+DD_{I}}{D_{I}}&1\end{matrix}\right),

where we used, cf (
104
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{S^{2}-D^{2}}+\frac{1}{D_{I}^{2}}=\frac{D_{I}^{2}+S^{2}-D^{2}}{(S^{2}-D^{2})D_{I}^{2}}=\frac{S(S+D_{I}(D+D_{I}))}{(S^{2}-D^{2})D_{I}^{2}},\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle\frac{S+D_{I}(D+D_{I})}{D_{I}}-D_{I}=\frac{S+D_{I}D}{D_{I}}.

Due to the anti-symmetry of the first term in the above expansion of
\uuline{\alpha}
, and the fact that this term is constant, we have that
\operatorname{div}(\uuline{\alpha}\nabla u)=\operatorname{div}(\uuline{r}\nabla u)
, which allows to rewrite (
102
) as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left(\uuline{r}\nabla B_{3}\right)+\omega^{2}B_{3}=0.

(105)

Up to now, the derivation had been rather formal. We aim at giving a more precise meaning to the above expression. Indeed, in the vicinity of
I
, where
S(\mathbf{x})
vanishes and
D=D_{I}=\operatorname{const}
, we have that

\displaystyle\uuline{r}=-\frac{S(\mathbf{x})}{D^{2}_{I}}\left(\begin{matrix}1&iD_{I}\\
-iD_{I}&1\end{matrix}\right)+O(S^{2}(\mathbf{x})),

which shows that the equation (
105
) has a degenerate coefficient in the principal part of the operator.

If we want to exclude a possible difficulty of different signs of eigenvalues of
S^{-1}\uuline{r}
, we can make the following assumption.

Assumption A.2

(Piecewise-ellipticity of the degenerate coefficient)
.

The matrix
S^{-1}\uuline{r}
is hermitian, sign-definite. In other words,
\uuline{r}(S^{2}-D^{2})
is positive definite (its trace is
>0
), i.e

\displaystyle 1-\left(\frac{S+DD_{I}}{D_{I}}\right)^{2}>0\text{ in }\mathcal{D}.

The latter condition is a condition on a relative variation of
D
and
S
.
It rewrites

\displaystyle(D_{I}-S-D_{I}D)(D_{I}+S+D_{I}D)>0.

When evaluated on
I
, it requires that
(D_{I}-D_{I}^{2})(D_{I}+D_{I}^{2})>0\iff(D_{I}^{2}-D_{I}^{4})>0\iff|D_{I}|<1
.

To ensure the above, we can impose a stricter assumption:

\displaystyle D_{I}(1-D)-S>0\text{ and }D_{I}(1+D)+S>0.

On the other hand, recalling that
D=D_{I}(1-S)
, cf. (
104
), this implies the following restriction on
S
:

\displaystyle S<\frac{D_{I}(D_{I}-1)}{(D_{I}^{2}-1)}<\frac{D_{I}}{D_{I}+1},\quad S>-\frac{D_{I}+D_{I}^{2}}{1-D_{I}^{2}}=\frac{D_{I}}{D_{I}-1}.

In other words, we can impose the following sufficient conditions on the coefficients.

Assumption A.3

(Simplification of Assumption
A.2
)
.

We assume that

\displaystyle 0<D_{I}<1\text{ and }-\frac{D_{I}}{1-D_{I}}<S<\frac{D_{I}}{1+D_{I}},

(106)

which implies that
\uuline{r}(\mathbf{x})=S(\mathbf{x})\uuline{a}(\mathbf{x})
, with
(S^{2}-D^{2})\uuline{a}(\mathbf{x})
being a Hermitian positive definite matrix. Remark that actually this assumption implies that
\uuline{a}
is a Hermitian negative definite matrix.

Indeed, for
D_{I},S
as above
S\neq\pm D
; this holds because
S=\pm D\iff S=\pm D_{I}(1-S)\iff S=\pm\frac{D_{I}}{1\pm D_{I}}
, which is excluded by our assumption. Therefore,
\operatorname{sign}(S^{2}-D^{2})=-\operatorname{sign}D_{I}^{2}<0
on
\mathcal{D}
.

Next, we formalize (
105
) more, by adding a source term
F
and equipping it with appropriate boundary conditions:

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left(\uuline{r}\nabla B_{3}\right)\equiv\operatorname{div}\left(\uuline{\alpha}\nabla B_{3}\right)=-\omega^{2}B_{3}+F,\qquad\text{ in }\mathcal{D},\qquad\gamma_{0}B_{3}=0.

(107)

To give a meaning to a ’physical’ solution to the above, which is far from being evident due to the degeneracy in
\uuline{r}
, one can pursue at least two approaches:

•

consider a non-vanishing collision frequency, which results in
\uuline{r}
replaced by
\uuline{r}^{\nu}
(resp.
\uuline{\alpha}
by
\uuline{\alpha}^{\nu}
); hoping that the resulting problem is well-posed, denote the solution by
B_{3}^{\nu}
, and next study the limit, if exists, as
\nu\rightarrow 0+
, of
B_{3}^{\nu}
.

•

consider
\nu=0
, add absorption to the frequency
\omega
(i.e. replace
\omega\rightarrow\omega+i\eta
), and next study the limit of the obtained solution, which we, with an abuse of notation, again denote by
B_{3}^{\eta}
, as
\eta\rightarrow 0+
.

In general, it is unclear whether these two limits commute. We start by considering the first approach. Without going to actual numbers, we start by studying a general case when
S
and
D
are perturbed with a small absorption parameter, and the tensor
\uuline{\alpha}
is replaced by its perturbation.

General computations.
Given a small parameter
\mu>0
and two expansions

\displaystyle{S}^{\mu}(\mathbf{x}):=S(\mathbf{x})+i\mu\delta_{S}(\mathbf{x})+O_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D})}(\mu^{2}),\quad{D}^{\mu}(\mathbf{x}):=D(\mathbf{x})+i\mu\delta_{D}(\mathbf{x})+O_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D})}(\mu^{2}),

we can rewrite the perturbed tensor

\displaystyle\uuline{\alpha}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(S^{\mu})^{2}-(D^{\mu})^{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}S^{\mu}&iD^{\mu}\\
-iD^{\mu}&S^{\mu}\end{matrix}\right)=\uuline{\alpha}+i\mu\uuline{t}+O(\mu^{2}),

(108)

\displaystyle\uuline{t}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{2S\delta_{S}-2D\delta_{D}}{(S^{2}-D^{2})^{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}S&iD\\
-iD&S\end{matrix}\right)+\frac{1}{S^{2}-D^{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}\delta_{S}&i\delta_{D}\\
-i\delta_{D}&\delta_{S}\end{matrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(S^{2}-D^{2})^{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}2DS\delta_{D}-\delta_{S}(S^{2}+D^{2})&i(\delta_{D}(S^{2}+D^{2})-2SD\delta_{S})\\
-i(\delta_{D}(S^{2}+D^{2})-2SD\delta_{S})&2DS\delta_{D}-\delta_{S}(S^{2}+D^{2})\end{matrix}\right).

(109)

In particular, on the interface
\mathbf{x}\in I
,

\displaystyle\uuline{t}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{D^{2}_{I}}\left(\begin{matrix}-\delta_{S}(\mathbf{x})&i\delta_{D}(\mathbf{x})\\
-i\delta_{D}(\mathbf{x})&-\delta_{S}(\mathbf{x})\end{matrix}\right).

(110)

The tensor
\uuline{t}
will be responsible for a ’regularization’ of the problem. Indeed, (
107
) with a perturbed tensor
\uuline{\alpha}^{\mu}
(where we use the same argument as before to replace
\uuline{\alpha}
by
\uuline{r}
) rewrites

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(\uuline{\alpha}^{\mu}\nabla\widetilde{B}_{3}^{\mu})=\operatorname{div}((\uuline{r}+i\mu\uuline{t}+O(\mu^{2}))\nabla\widetilde{B}_{3}^{\mu})=-\omega^{2}\widetilde{B}_{3}^{\mu}+F.

(111)

Because
\uuline{r}
vanishes on
I
, we would like
\uuline{t}
be sign-definite in the vicinity of
I
; in particular, this requires that

\displaystyle\delta_{S}^{2}-\delta_{D}^{2}>0\text{ on }I.

(112)

On the other hand, if
S
,
D
,
\delta_{S}
,
\delta_{D}
do not vary drastically in the domain,
\uuline{t}
remains sign-definite. Thus, a generalization of (
112
) reads.

Assumption A.4

(Ellipticity of
\uuline{t}
)
.

The coefficients
S,\,\delta_{S},\,D,\delta_{D}
satisfy in
\mathcal{D}
:

\displaystyle\left(2DS\delta_{D}-\delta_{S}(S^{2}+D^{2})\right)^{2}-(\delta_{D}(S^{2}+D^{2})-2SD\delta_{S})^{2}>0.

The above is equivalent to

\displaystyle-(\delta_{S}+\delta_{D})(S-D)^{2}(\delta_{D}-\delta_{S})(S+D)^{2}>0\iff\delta_{S}^{2}-\delta_{D}^{2}>0\text{ and }S\neq\pm D\text{ in }\mathcal{D}.

The above implies that
\uuline{t}
is a strictly positive (or negative)-definite matrix in
\mathcal{D}
.

Collision limit

When
\nu>0
is indeed a collision frequency, we can compute the perturbations of the coefficients
S,\,D
explicitly; in particular,

\displaystyle S^{\nu}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\omega_{p}^{2}\omega_{\nu}}{\omega(\omega_{\nu}^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2})}=S+i\nu\frac{(\omega^{2}+\omega_{c}^{2})\omega_{p}^{2}}{\omega(\omega^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2})^{2}}+O_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D})}(\nu^{2}),

\displaystyle D^{\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega_{c}\omega_{p}^{2}}{\omega(\omega_{\nu}^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2})}=D-i\nu\frac{2\omega_{c}\omega_{p}^{2}}{(\omega^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2})^{2}}+O_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D})}(\nu^{2}).

We then have

\displaystyle\delta_{S}=\frac{\omega^{2}+\omega_{c}^{2}}{\omega(\omega^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2})}\left(1-S\right),\quad\delta_{D}=-\frac{2\omega_{c}}{(\omega^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2})}\left(1-S\right).

(113)

For Assumption
A.4
to be fulfilled, we want that

\displaystyle|\delta_{S}|>|\delta_{D}|,

and as
S<1
by Assumption
A.3
, the above is equivalent to requiring that
|\omega|\neq|\omega_{c}|
and
\omega^{2}+\omega_{c}^{2}>2|\omega|\omega_{c}
, which is always true.

With these new expressions, we have the following important result.

Corollary A.5
.

Assume that
\omega>\omega_{c}>0
, and the following holds true:

\displaystyle-\frac{D_{I}}{1-D_{I}}<S<\frac{D_{I}}{1+D_{I}}.

(114)

Then, for all sufficiently regular
u
, it holds that

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(\uuline{\alpha}^{\nu}\nabla u)=\operatorname{div}(\left(S(\mathbf{x})\uuline{a}+i\nu\uuline{t}+\nu^{2}\uuline{c}^{\nu}\right)\nabla u),

where

\displaystyle\uuline{a}=\frac{1}{S^{2}-D^{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}1&i\frac{S+DD_{I}}{D_{I}}\\
-i\frac{S+DD_{I}}{D_{I}}&1\end{matrix}\right)

is a Hermitian, negative definite in
\mathcal{D}
matrix; the matrix
\uuline{t}
is given by (
109
) with the coefficients defined in (
113
) and is Hermitian, negative definite in
\mathcal{D}
.

Finally, for sufficiently regular
S,\,D
, the matrix
\uuline{c}^{\nu}(\mathbf{x})
is bounded in
\mathcal{D}
uniformly in
\nu
, in other words,
\sup\limits_{0\leq\nu<1}\|\uuline{c}^{\nu}\|_{L^{\infty}}<\operatorname{const}
.

Proof.

We use the expansion (
108
), and the same argument as before to pass from (
102
) to (
105
); we have
\uuline{r}=S\uuline{a}
. Remark that
\omega>\omega_{c}>0
implies that
0<D_{I}=\frac{\omega_{c}}{\omega}<1
, cf. (
104
). Then
the assumption (
114
) of the corollary is exactly Assumption
A.3
, which ensures that
\uuline{a}
is a Hermitian negative definite matrix.

Next, let us show that (
114
) implies Assumption
A.4
, which, in turn ensures, that
\uuline{t}
is a strictly positive- (or negative-)definite matrix in
\mathcal{D}
. As argued before the statement of the corollary, since Assumption
A.3
holds true, we have that

\displaystyle\delta_{S}^{2}-\delta_{D}^{2}>0\text{ and }|S|<|D|\text{ in }\mathcal{D}.

Thus,
\uuline{t}
is (strictly) sign-definite in
\mathcal{D}
. As its both eigenvalues do not change their signs in
D^{\nu}
, it suffices to examine what happens on
I
. Using the expression (
110
), we see that
\operatorname{sign}\operatorname{Tr}\uuline{t}=-\operatorname{sign}\left.\delta_{S}\right|_{I}=-\operatorname{sign}\frac{(1-S)}{1-\omega^{-2}\omega_{c}^{2}}=-\operatorname{sign}\frac{1}{1-D_{I}^{2}}<0,
where we remarked that
S<1
by (
114
), recalled (
104
), namely
D_{I}=\omega_{c}/\omega
and used
\omega>\omega_{c}
.

Finally, the uniform bound on
\uuline{c}^{\nu}
follows from the regularity of
\uuline{\varepsilon}^{\nu}(\mathbf{x})
, both in
\nu
and
\mathbf{x}
.
∎

We are thus interested in the following boundary-value problem:

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left(\left(S(\mathbf{x})(-\uuline{a})+i\nu(-\uuline{t})\right)\nabla B_{3}^{\nu}\right)-\omega^{2}B_{3}^{\nu}=F,\quad\text{ in }\mathcal{D},\quad\gamma_{0}B_{3}^{\nu}=0.

We omit the term
O(\nu^{2})
compared (
111
), since, as we will see later in the course of the article, for
\nu\rightarrow 0
, it does not seem to play a role in any of the conclusions of the paper.

Limiting absorption limit

When
\nu=\eta
is an absorption added to the frequency, we can compute the perturbations of the coefficients
S,\,D
explicitly; in particular,

\displaystyle S^{\eta}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\omega_{p}^{2}}{(\omega_{\eta}^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2})}=S+\frac{2\omega_{p}^{2}\omega}{(\omega^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2})^{2}}i\eta+O_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D})}(\eta^{2}),

\displaystyle D^{\eta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega_{c}\omega_{p}^{2}}{\omega_{\eta}(\omega_{\eta}^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2})}=D-i\eta\frac{\omega_{c}\omega_{p}^{2}(\omega^{2}+\omega_{c}^{2})}{\omega^{2}(\omega^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2})^{2}}+O_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D})}(\eta^{2}).

We then have

\displaystyle\delta_{S}=\frac{2\omega}{\omega^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2}}\left(1-S\right),\quad\delta_{D}=-\frac{\omega_{c}(\omega^{2}+\omega_{c}^{2})}{\omega^{2}(\omega^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2})}\left(1-S\right).

(115)

For Assumption
A.4
to be fulfilled, we want that

\displaystyle|\delta_{S}|>|\delta_{D}|,

and as
S<1
by Assumption
A.3
, the above is equivalent to requiring that, for
\omega\neq\pm\omega_{c}
,
\omega\neq 0
,

\displaystyle 2|\omega|^{3}>|\omega_{c}|(\omega^{2}+\omega_{c}^{2}),

and for the above to hold true it is sufficient that

\displaystyle|\omega|>|\omega_{c}|.

We then have the following result, proven like Corollary
A.5
.

Corollary A.6
.

Assume that
\omega>\omega_{c}>0
, and the following holds true:

\displaystyle-\frac{D_{I}}{1-D_{I}}<S<\frac{D_{I}}{1+D_{I}}.

(116)

Then, for all sufficiently regular
u
, it holds that

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(\uuline{\alpha}^{\eta}\nabla u)=\operatorname{div}(\left(S(\mathbf{x})\uuline{a}+i\eta\uuline{t}+\eta^{2}\uuline{c}^{\nu}\right)\nabla u),

where

\displaystyle\uuline{a}=\frac{1}{S^{2}-D^{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}1&i\frac{S+DD_{I}}{D_{I}}\\
-i\frac{S+DD_{I}}{D_{I}}&1\end{matrix}\right)

is a Hermitian, negative definite in
\mathcal{D}
matrix; the matrix
\uuline{t}
is given by (
109
) with the coefficients defined in (
115
) and is Hermitian, negative definite in
\mathcal{D}
. Finally, for sufficiently regular
S,\,D
, the matrix
\uuline{c}^{\eta}(\mathbf{x})
is bounded in
\mathcal{D}
uniformly in
\nu
, in other words,
\sup\limits_{0\leq\nu<1}\|\uuline{c}^{\eta}\|_{L^{\infty}}<\operatorname{const}
.

B
Properties of weighted spaces

We will state the results for
\Omega_{p}
only; they extend in a trivial manner to
\Omega_{n}
.

B.1
Basic properties and inequalities

Let us introduce an auxiliary Hilbert space

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\nu,\delta}(\Omega_{p})=\{v\in L^{2}_{loc}(\Omega_{p}):\,\int_{\Omega_{p}}(x^{\nu}|u|^{2}+x^{\delta}|\nabla u|^{2})d\mathbf{x}<\infty,\quad\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}}u=0,\quad\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}^{+}}u=\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}^{-}}u\},

so that
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})=\mathcal{H}^{1}_{0,\delta}(\Omega_{p})
