. We start with the Poincaré inequality.

Proposition B.1
.

Let
0\leq\delta\leq 2
. There exists
C_{\delta}>0
, s.t. for all
u\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta,\delta}(\Omega_{p})
,
it holds that

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}^{2}\leq C_{\delta}\int_{\Omega_{p}}x^{\delta}|\nabla u|^{2}d\mathbf{x}.

Proof.

By repeating the argument of
[
27
, Theorem 7.2]
,
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}_{p})
functions are dense in
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta,\delta}(\Omega_{p})
, hence it suffices to prove the desired result for
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}_{p})
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
u
is real-valued. By integration by parts we have

\displaystyle u^{2}(x,y)=-\int_{x}^{a}2\partial_{x^{\prime}}u(x^{\prime},y)u(x^{\prime},y)dx^{\prime},\quad(x,y)\in\Omega_{p},

where we used
u^{2}(a,y)=0
. The above yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{a}u^{2}(x,y)dx

\displaystyle=-2\int_{0}^{a}\int_{x}^{a}\partial_{x^{\prime}}u(x^{\prime},y)u(x^{\prime},y)dx^{\prime}dx=-2\int_{0}^{a}x\partial_{x}u(x,y)u(x,y)dx.

Using the Young inequality implies that, for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
C_{\varepsilon}>0
, s.t.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{a}u^{2}(x,y)dx\leq C_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{a}x^{2}(\partial_{x}u)^{2}dx+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{a}u^{2}dx.

Integrating the above in
y\in(-\ell,\ell)
and using
x^{2}\lesssim x^{\delta}
,
0\leq\delta\leq 2
, yields the desired statement.
∎

With the above, we obtain

Proposition B.2
.

Let
0\leq\delta\leq 2
. Then
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})=\overline{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}_{p})}^{\|.\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})}}
.

Proof.

This result follows from
[
27
, Theorem 7.2]
about the density of
C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}_{p})
functions in
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta,\delta}(\Omega_{p})
, once we argue that

\displaystyle\text{for $0\leq\delta\leq 2$, }\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})=\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta,\delta}(\Omega_{p})\text{ with equivalent norms. }

(117)

The inclusion
\subseteq
is evident, while
\supseteq
follows from the Poincaré inequality
B.1
.
∎

For
\delta\geq 1
, we have a stronger result.

Proposition B.3
.

Let
1\leq\delta\leq 2
. Then
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})=\overline{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{comp}(\overline{\Omega}_{p})}^{\|.\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})}}.

Proof.

Follows by a straightforward adaptation of the proof of
[
17
, Theorem 1.1]
, originally stated for
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta,\delta}(U)
with
U=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:x>0\}
, combined with Proposition
B.2
.
∎

We will also need the following Hardy inequality, which we state for the space
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
.

Proposition B.4

(Hardy inequality)
.

For all
u\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
, and all
\varepsilon>0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|x^{-1/2+\varepsilon}u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C(\Omega_{p},\varepsilon)\|u\|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})},\quad\text{ with some }C(\Omega_{p},\varepsilon)>0.

Proof.

See
[
35
, Lemma 6]
and references therein, in particular
[
34
, Theorem 1.5]
.
∎

An important corollary of Proposition
B.4
reads.

Lemma B.5
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
,
v\in\mathcal{H}_{\delta}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
, for some
0<\delta<1
. Then
uv\in L^{1}(\Omega_{p})
and
\partial_{x}(uv)\in L^{1}(\Omega_{p})
.

Proof.

We argue by density. Let
u,v\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega_{p}})
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}|\partial_{x}(uv)|\lesssim\int_{\Omega_{p}}|\partial_{x}u\,v|+\int_{\Omega_{p}}|\partial_{x}v\,u|.

The first integral is well-defined since
u\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
and
v\in L^{2}(\Omega)
; as for the second integral, we employ the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and next the Hardy inequality of Proposition
B.4
for
u
(recall that
\delta<1
):

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}|\partial_{x}v\,u|=\int_{\Omega_{p}}|x^{\delta/2}\partial_{x}v|\,|x^{-\delta/2}u|\leq\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})}\|x^{-\delta}u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C_{\delta}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})}\|u\|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})}.\qquad\qquad\qed

Another important property concerns the space
\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)
.

Proposition B.6
.

Assume that
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)
. Then
x\mathbb{A}\nabla u\in L^{2}(\Omega).

Proof.

Given
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)
, a straightforward computation yields that
v:=xu\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{n})
. Let us argue that
v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
. For this it is sufficient to show that
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}v=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,n}v=0
, or, equivalently, that
v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega_{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega_{n})
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega_{\lambda})=\{v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\lambda}):\,\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,\lambda}v=0\}=\overline{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{comp}(\Omega_{\lambda})}^{\|.\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\lambda})}}.

Let
\varphi_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{comp}(\Omega_{p})
be s.t.
\varphi_{n}\rightarrow u
in
\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega_{p})
(it exists by Proposition
B.3
). Then
x\varphi_{n}\rightarrow xu=v
in
H^{1}(\Omega_{p})
. Since
x\varphi_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{comp}(\Omega_{p})
, we conclude that
v=xu\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega_{p})
. Repeating the argument for
\Omega_{n}
yields the desired conclusion.
∎

B.2
Relation to fractional Sobolev spaces

The weighted spaces can be shown to be embedded into fractional Sobolev spaces.

Lemma B.7
.

For
0<\theta\leq 2
, the space
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\theta}(\Omega_{p})
is continuously embedded into
{H}^{1-\frac{\theta}{2}}(\Omega_{p})
.

Proof.

Up to our knowledge, this result dates back to the works
[
28
,
41
]
, in the case when
\Omega_{p}
is a half-space (in other words,
a=\ell=+\infty
). The corresponding result for
\Omega_{p}
follows by a localization argument, cf.
[
30
, Theorem 3.20]
. A different version of the proof can be found in
[
24
, Theorems 4.1, 4.2]
; remark that while the needed result is stated for harmonic functions, its proof does not rely on this property.
∎

An immediate corollary of the above result and the continuity of the trace in fractional Sobolev spaces of Theorem 3.37 in
[
30
]
reads.

Corollary B.8
.

For
0<\theta<1
, the trace operator
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}:\,\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{1/2-\theta/2}(\Sigma)
extends by density to a bounded linear operator from
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\theta}(\Omega_{p})
into
\mathcal{H}^{1/2-\theta/2}(\Sigma)
.

Proposition B.9

(Prop. 1.2 in
[
17
]
)
.

The space
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\theta;0}(\Omega_{p}):=\{u\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\theta}(\Omega_{p}),\text{ s.t. }\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,p}u=0\}
equals to
\overline{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{comp}(\Omega_{p})}^{\|.\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\theta}(\Omega_{p})}}
.

the latter space being well-defined, as follows by adapting the proof of
[
17
, Proposition 1.1’]
(remark that the norm in
[
17
]
is equivalent to the norm
\|.\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})}
we use, as shown in Proposition
B.1
).

C
Properties of a regular problem (
RP
)

C.1
Vanishing conormal trace

Proposition C.1
.

Any function
u\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
, s.t., for some
\varepsilon>0
,
\mathbf{x}\mapsto x^{1/2-\varepsilon}\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}(\mathbf{x})\nabla u(\mathbf{x}))\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, satisfies:
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u=0\text{ in }\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma).

To prove the above result, we rely on the following auxiliary statements. Let us denote
f:=\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}(\mathbf{x})\nabla u(\mathbf{x}))
,
and argue that
f
as in the statement of Proposition
C.1
satisfies
f\in(\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p}))^{\prime}
.

Lemma C.2
.

For all
0\leq\delta<1
, the space
L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
is continuously embedded into
\mathcal{V}_{reg}^{\prime}(\Omega_{p})
. In particular, for all
f\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
, the Lebesgue integral
\int_{\Omega_{p}}fvd\mathbf{x}
is well-defined for all
v\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
.

Proof.

Let
f\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
, for some
0\leq\delta<1
. It suffices to prove the second statement.
In virtue of the Hardy inequality of Proposition
B.4
the integral is well-defined as a scalar product of two
L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
-functions:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}fvd\mathbf{x}=\int_{\Omega_{p}}x^{\delta/2}f\,x^{-\delta/2}vd\mathbf{x}=(x^{\delta/2}f,x^{-\delta/2}\overline{v})_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})},\quad\forall v\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p}).

(118)

From the above and the Hardy inequality it is immediate that
\|f\|_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|x^{\delta}f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
.
∎

The next result is an auxiliary lifting lemma. Before stating it, let us recall the notation (
9
):
\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\delta}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in\Omega:\,|\operatorname{dist}(\mathbf{x},\Sigma)|<\delta\},\quad\delta>0.

Lemma C.3

(Lifting lemma)
.

Given
v\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
and
0<\delta<1
, let
V^{\delta}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
be a unique solution to the following boundary-value problem:

\displaystyle-\Delta V^{\delta}=0\text{ on }\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\delta}\cap\Omega_{p},

\displaystyle V^{\delta}(0,y)=v(y),\qquad V^{\delta}(\delta,y)=0,

\displaystyle\text{ periodic BCs at }y=\pm\ell.

Then, with a constant
C>0
independent of
\delta
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(a)\,\;\|V^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\delta\|V^{\delta}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C\delta^{1/2}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)},\quad(b)\,\;\|V^{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}.\end{split}

(119)

Before proving the above result, let us recall a standard equivalent definition of the
\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
-norm.
First of all, consider the 1D periodic Laplacian operator
-\partial_{y}^{2}
on
(-\ell,\ell)
; we define its eigenfunctions
\{\phi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
and the corresponding eigenvalues
\lambda_{m}^{2}
(where
0=\lambda_{0}^{2}\leq\lambda_{1}^{2}\leq\ldots\rightarrow+\infty
) via

\displaystyle-\partial_{y}^{2}\varphi_{m}=\lambda_{m}^{2}\varphi_{m},\quad\text{ on }(-\ell,\ell),

\displaystyle\text{periodic BCs at }y=\pm\ell,\qquad\|\varphi_{m}\|_{L^{2}(-\ell,\ell)}=1.

Identifying
\Sigma
with an interval
(-\ell,\ell)
, and given
v\in L^{2}(\Sigma)
, we can expand it into the Fourier series in
\varphi_{m}
. Let
v_{m}:=(v,\varphi_{m})_{L^{2}(-\ell,\ell)}
and define the norm
\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}_{per}(\Sigma)}^{2}:=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{+\infty}(1+\lambda_{m}^{2})^{1/2}|v_{m}|^{2}.

The norms
\|.\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}_{per}(\Sigma)}
and
\|.\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}
are equivalent, and

\mathcal{H}^{1/2}_{per}(\Sigma)=\{v\in L^{2}(\Sigma):\,\sum\limits_{m=0}^{+\infty}(1+\lambda_{m}^{2})^{1/2}|v_{m}|^{2}<\infty\}=\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma).

This follows by the same argument as in the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 2.10]
.

Proof of Lemma
C.3
.

It holds that
V^{\delta}(x,y)=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}V^{\delta}_{m}(x)\phi_{m}(y),

where each
V^{\delta}_{m}(x)
solves the ODE

\displaystyle-\partial_{x}^{2}V^{\delta}_{m}+\lambda_{m}^{2}V^{\delta}_{m}=0\text{ on }(0,\,\delta),\quad V^{\delta}_{m}(0)=v_{m},\quad V^{\delta}_{m}(\delta)=0.

Solving the above explicitly yields the identity:

\displaystyle V_{m}^{\delta}(x)=v_{m}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{m}(\delta-x)}-\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{m}(\delta-x)}}{\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{m}\delta}-\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{m}\delta}}.

To bound the
H^{1}
-norm, we use
\Delta V^{\delta}=0
in
\Omega_{p}\cap\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\delta}
and
V^{\delta}(\delta,y)=0
to integrate by parts:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}\cap\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\delta}}|\nabla V^{\delta}|^{2}=-\int_{\Sigma}\partial_{x}V^{\delta}(0,y)\overline{V^{\delta}}(0,y)d\Gamma_{y}=-\int_{\Sigma}\partial_{x}V^{\delta}(0,y)\overline{v}(y)d\Gamma_{y}.

Then a straightforward computation yields
\partial_{x}V^{\delta}(0,y)=-\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}\lambda_{m}v_{m}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{m}\delta}+\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{m}\delta}}{\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{m}\delta}-\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{m}\delta}}\phi_{m}(y)
, so that

\displaystyle\|\nabla V^{\delta}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}|v_{m}|^{2}\lambda_{m}\frac{1+\mathrm{e}^{-2\delta\lambda_{m}}}{1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda_{m}\delta}}\leq\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}|v_{m}|^{2}\frac{2\lambda_{m}}{1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda_{m}\delta}},

(120)

where we used
\mathrm{e}^{-2\delta\lambda_{m}}<1
. Next,
for some
0<\varepsilon<1
sufficiently small, there exists
C_{\varepsilon}>0
, s.t.

\displaystyle 1-\mathrm{e}^{-t}\geq C_{\varepsilon}t,\text{ for all }|t|<\varepsilon,\qquad\text{ and }\quad 1-\mathrm{e}^{-t}\geq C_{\varepsilon}\varepsilon,\text{ for all }|t|\geq\varepsilon.

We split the series in (
120
) into two parts, and use the above bounds for a fixed
\varepsilon>0
in both parts:

\displaystyle\|\nabla V^{\delta}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\leq\frac{1}{\delta C_{\varepsilon}}\sum\limits_{2\lambda_{m}\delta<\varepsilon}|v_{m}|^{2}+\frac{2}{C_{\varepsilon}\varepsilon}\sum\limits_{2\lambda_{m}\delta\geq\varepsilon}|v_{m}|^{2}\lambda_{m}\lesssim\max(1,\delta^{-1})\|v\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}.

(121)

To estimate the
L^{2}
-norm, we combine the above with the Poincaré inequality. Since
V^{\delta}(\delta,0)=0
, we use

V^{\delta}(x,y)=-\int_{x}^{\delta}\partial_{\tilde{x}}V(\tilde{x},y)d\tilde{x},\text{ a.e. }y\in(-\ell,\ell),

and by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, it holds a.e.
y\in(-\ell,\ell)
, that

\displaystyle|V^{\delta}(x,y)|^{2}\leq(\delta-x)\int_{x}^{\delta}|\partial_{\tilde{x}}V^{\delta}(\tilde{x},y)|^{2}d\tilde{x}\implies\int_{0}^{\delta}|V^{\delta}(x,y)|^{2}dx\leq\delta^{2}\int_{0}^{\delta}|\partial_{x}V^{\delta}(\tilde{x},y)|^{2}d\tilde{x}.

Therefore,
\|V^{\delta}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}=\|V^{\delta}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p}\cap\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\delta})}\lesssim\delta^{2}\|\nabla V^{\delta}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}.

The bound (
119
)(a) then follows with (
121
). The remaining bound (
119
)(b) is immediate from the above: since
\operatorname{supp}V^{\delta}\subseteq\overline{\Omega}_{\Sigma}^{\delta}
,

\displaystyle\|x^{1/2}\nabla V^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}=\|x^{1/2}\nabla V^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p}\cap\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\delta})}\leq\delta^{1/2}\|\nabla V^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p}\cap\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\delta})}\leq C\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)},

as follows from (
121
). Combining the above with
\|V^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\delta^{1/2}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}
yields the sought inequality.
∎

Now we have the necessary ingredients to prove Proposition
C.1
.

Proof of Proposition
C.1
.

Recall the variational definition of the conormal derivative: given
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
,

\displaystyle\langle\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u,\varphi\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}=-\int_{\Omega_{p}}f\Phi-\int_{\Omega_{p}}x\mathbb{A}\nabla u\,\nabla\Phi,

(122)

with
\Phi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
being such that
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\Phi=\varphi
. The right-hand side is well-defined by Lemma
C.2
.

To prove that
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u=0
, we fix
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
,
0<\delta<a
, and choose the lifting of
\varphi
as
\Phi=\Phi^{\delta}
defined in Lemma
C.3
. We introduce
\Omega_{p,\Sigma}^{\delta}:=\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\delta}\cap\Omega_{p}
, use Lemma
C.2
(see (
118
)), the fact that
\operatorname{supp}\Phi^{\delta}\subseteq\overline{\Omega_{p,\Sigma}^{\delta}}
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (with the hidden constant depending on
\varepsilon
):

\displaystyle\left|\langle\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u,\varphi\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|x^{1/2-\varepsilon}f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p,\Sigma}^{\delta})}\|\Phi^{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p,\Sigma}^{\delta})}+\|x^{1/2}\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p,\Sigma}^{\delta})}\|x^{1/2}\nabla\Phi_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p,\Sigma}^{\delta})}.

Using the bound (
119
)(b), we conclude that, with some
C>0
, independent of
\delta
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\langle\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u,\varphi\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|x^{1/2-\varepsilon}f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p,\Sigma}^{\delta})}+\|u\|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p,\Sigma}^{\delta})}\right)\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}.

Since
u\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
and
x^{1/2-\varepsilon}f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, we conclude that by taking
\delta\rightarrow 0
, the norm
\|\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}
can be made arbitrarily small, hence the conclusion of the proposition.
∎

C.2
Well-posedness for non-
L^{2}
-data

Proposition C.4
.

Let
0\leq\delta<1
, and let
f\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
.
Then the problem: find
u\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
, s.t.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)=f\text{ in }\Omega_{p},\\
&\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}}u=0,\quad\text{ periodic BCs at }\Gamma_{p}^{\pm},\end{split}

(123)

admits a unique solution
u\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
. It satisfies
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u=0
and

u\in\bigcap_{0<\varepsilon<1}\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta+\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})\subset\bigcap_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1-\frac{\delta}{2}}\mathcal{H}^{1-\delta/2-\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p}).

Also,
\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1-\delta/2-\varepsilon/2}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C_{\delta,\varepsilon}\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta+\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})}\leq\tilde{C}_{\delta,\varepsilon}\|x^{\delta/2}f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})},
for all
0<\varepsilon<1/2
.

Proof.

By Lemma
C.2
,
f\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}^{\prime}(\Omega_{p})
. Testing the problem (
123
) with
v\in\mathcal{C}_{comp}^{\infty}(\Omega_{p})
, and using the density of
\mathcal{C}_{comp}^{\infty}(\Omega_{p})
in
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
, see Proposition
B.3
, yields the well-posed problem examined in Theorem
3.6
. By Proposition
C.1
,
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u=0
.
It remains to argue about the regularity of
u
, more precisely, we would like to show that
\nabla u\in L^{2}_{\delta+\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})
, for all
\varepsilon>0
. We proceed by interpolation. According to
[
7
, Theorem 3.1]
stated in the form that we need (which is based on the results from
[
40
]
and
[
6
]
):

\displaystyle[L^{2}(\Omega_{p}),L^{2}_{\eta}(\Omega_{p})]_{\theta}=L^{2}_{{\theta\eta}}(\Omega_{p}),\quad 0<\theta<1,\quad\eta>0,

(124)

where
[,]_{\theta}
stands for complex interpolation.
Let us now consider the operator
\mathcal{S}:\,\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})\rightarrow L^{2}_{1}(\Omega_{p})
, defined by
\mathcal{S}f=\nabla u_{f}
, where
u_{f}\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
is a unique solution to

\displaystyle(x^{1/2}\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{f},x^{1/2}\nabla v)=-\langle f,\overline{v}\rangle_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}^{\prime}(\Omega_{p}),\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})},\quad\forall v\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})

(remark that with an abuse of notation we use
L^{2}_{\eta}(\Omega_{p})
both for scalar- and vector-valued functions).

By Theorem
3.7
,
\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\Omega_{p}),L^{2}(\Omega_{p}))
, and, with Lemma
C.2
, for any
0<\varepsilon<1
,

\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}_{1-\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p}),L^{2}_{1}(\Omega_{p})).

With (
124
) this shows that
\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}_{(1-\varepsilon)\theta}(\Omega_{p}),L^{2}_{\theta}(\Omega_{p}))
, for all
\theta\in(0,1)
. Choosing
\varepsilon
sufficiently close to
0
, and
\theta=\delta/(1-\varepsilon)<1
yields
\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p}),L^{2}_{\frac{\delta}{1-\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{p}))
, for all
\varepsilon
sufficiently close to
0
, thus all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Hence the statement of the proposition for bounds in the weighted space
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta+\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})
, for any
\varepsilon>0
.

On the other hand, the corresponding result on the fractional spaces stems from Lemma
B.7
.
∎

D
Proof of Theorem
3.7

In this Appendix we provide a proof of Theorem
3.7
from
[
4
]
, where we argue that we can reduce the regularity of the coefficients compared to the regularity considered in
[
4
]
; the statement of this theorem is repeated below for the convenience of the reader. The proof of this result relies on the elements introduced in Section
4.2.1
, that appear in the manuscript later compared to the statement of Theorem
3.7
.

Theorem D.1
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
. Then the unique solution
u
to (
RP
) belongs to
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
, and satisfies the following stability bound:
\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})}+\|xu\|_{H^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
.

If, moreover,
f\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
, then the unique solution to
u
to (
RP
) satisfies
u\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, and the following stability bound holds true:
\|u\|_{H^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\|xu\|_{H^{3}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})}
.

Before proving Theorem
D.1
, a couple of remarks are in order. In principle, the first part of the result (
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
), is probably true when
\mathbb{A}
is less regular, e.g.
C^{0,1}(\overline{\Omega}_{p};\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
; however, in our regularity proof we used a non-optimal Lemma
E.2
, and did not prove the corresponding result for
\mathbb{A}
being less regular.
Second, in the standard elliptic regularity estimates, cf.
[
30
, Theorem 4.18]
, for the uniform bound
\|u\|_{H^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
, it suffices that coefficients are of regularity
C^{0,1}(\overline{\Omega}_{p})
. We were not able to obtain such a result for our, degenerate, setting.

D.1
Proof of the statement of Theorem
D.1
for the case when
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})

The first part of the result is proven indirectly.
We introduce a regularized problem (see also Proposition
4.18
). This section is devoted to construction and analysis of such a regularized problem.

A regularized Dirichlet problem

We look for
u^{\nu}_{r}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
, s.t.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu)\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r})=f\text{ in }\Omega_{p},\\
&\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}_{r}=0,\\
&\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}}u^{\nu}_{r}=0,\text{ periodic BCs at }y=\pm\ell.\end{split}

(125)

Introducing
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega_{p}):=\{u\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p}),\quad\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u=0\}
, we
can write a variational formulation for this problem, namely: find
u_{r}^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega)
, s.t.

\displaystyle a_{r}^{\nu}(u^{\nu}_{r},v)=-(f,v),\quad\forall v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega_{p}),\quad a_{r}^{\nu}(q,v)=\int_{\Omega_{p}}(x+i\nu)\mathbb{A}\nabla q\overline{\nabla v}.

(126)

We have the following result, that enables us to use the solution to (
125
) to analyze properties of the solution to (
RP
).

Theorem D.2
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
.
Then, for
\nu>0
, the problem (
125
) admits a unique solution
u^{\nu}_{r}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\|u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})},

(127)

\displaystyle\nu^{1/2}\|u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}.

(128)

As
\nu\rightarrow 0+
, up to a subsequence,
u^{\nu}_{r}\rightharpoonup u_{r}
in
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
and
u^{\nu}_{r}\rightarrow u_{r}
in
H^{1/2-\varepsilon}(\Omega_{p})
, for all
0<\varepsilon\leq 1/2
.

Proof.

Cf. Lemma
2.4
and Theorem
3.3
on how to obtain the results up to (
128
) including.

From the bound in the statement of the theorem, it follows immediately that
u^{\nu}_{r}\rightharpoonup u_{r}
in
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
up to a subsequence. As for the statement about the strong convergence up to a subsequence, it follows from Lemma
B.7
, and the compactness of the embedding
H^{s}(\Omega_{p})\subset H^{s+\epsilon}(\Omega_{p})
, for all
s\geq 0
and
\epsilon>0
.

∎

The regularity of
u^{\nu}_{r}
for
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})

The key result of this section is the following statement.

Theorem D.3
.

Let
u^{\nu}_{r}
be the unique solution of (
125
), where the right-hand side
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
.

Then, for all
\nu>0
,
\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\|x\partial_{y}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\nu\|\partial_{y}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
.

Once we have proven the above result, the proof of the first part of the Theorem
D.1
is quite straightforward. Remark that it is impossible to prove the uniform boundedness of
\|\partial_{x}u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
in the above setting, cf. the discussion in the beginning of Appendix
G
.

Proof of Theorem
D.1
.

By Theorem
D.2
and Theorem
D.3
, and the uniqueness of the weak
L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
-limit of
u^{\nu}_{r}
, it holds that
\partial_{y}u_{r}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
, and, moreover,
\partial_{y}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{r})\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
. The uniform control of the corresponding norms by
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
is immediate from the weak convergence properties. It remains to consider
\|\partial_{x}u_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
. For this we rewrite

\displaystyle\partial_{x}(x\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{r})=f-\partial_{y}(x\mathbf{e}_{y}\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{r})\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p}).

The function
q:=x\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{r}
is thus in
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega_{p})
(see Lemma
3.5
for
\gamma^{\Sigma}_{0}q=\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{r}=0
), and, by Hardy’s inequality, cf. (
35
), it holds that
\mathbf{x}\mapsto q(\mathbf{x})/x\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
. This shows that
\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{r}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, hence the conclusion about
u\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
.

The estimates of Theorem
D.3
imply in particular that
x\partial_{y}^{2}u_{r},x\partial_{xy}u_{r}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
. From this and
\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{r})=f
it follows also that
\partial_{x}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{r}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{x})\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, and, in particular,
x\partial_{x}^{2}u_{r}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
.

The uniform control of the corresponding norms of
u
by
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
stated in the theorem is a corollary of the above reasoning and continuity estimates of Hardy’s inequality (
35
).

∎

The (remaining) proof of Theorem
D.3
is fairly tedious, and relies on several auxiliary results. We proceed as follows:

1.

we start by proving the bound

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{\Omega_{p}}\leq C\|f\|_{\Omega_{p}}.

(129)

2.

next, we prove that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r})\|_{\Omega_{p}}+\nu\|\partial_{y}(\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r})\|_{\Omega_{p}}\leq C\|f\|_{\Omega_{p}}.

(130)

3.

next, we will be able to argue that the above allows us to conclude that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{x}((x+i\nu)\nabla u^{\nu}\|_{\Omega_{p}}\leq C\|f\|_{\Omega_{p}}.

(131)

Remark that the stated inequality in Theorem
D.3
, namely,
\|u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})}+\|x\partial_{y}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\nu\|\partial_{y}\nabla u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
follows from the above bounds and Theorem
D.2
.

D.1.1
Proof of (
129
)

We will use the Fourier analysis techniques, to relate the weighted norm and the fractional Sobolev norms.
The definitions can be found in Section
4.2.1
. In particular, it suffices to consider the counterpart of (
125
) for
U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}=\chi_{\ell,\delta}\mathcal{E}_{\delta}u^{\nu}_{r}
that satisfies, cf. (
51
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu)\mathbb{A}\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta})=F^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a},

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}=0,

with
\|F^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\Omega_{p}}
.

The lemma that follows will enable us to work with second derivatives of
U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}
as
L^{2}
-functions. It can be proven by using a classical elliptic regularity argument, cf.
[
30
, Theorem 4.18]
.

Lemma D.4
.

Let
u^{\nu}_{r}
be the unique solution of (
125
), where the right-hand side
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
. For all
\nu>0
,
u^{\nu}_{r}\in H^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, and, moreover,
\partial_{x}u^{\nu}_{r}(x,\ell)=\partial_{x}u^{\nu}_{r}(x,-\ell)
.

As a corollary, for all
\nu>0
,
U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{a})
and also
\left.\mathcal{J}^{2}U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\right|_{x=a}=0
.

The first result recalls that
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
is embedded into the Bochner space
L^{2}((0,a);H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R}))
, see Lemma
B.7
.

Lemma D.5
.

For all
v\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
, it holds that
\|\mathcal{J}v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\leq C\|v\|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}
.

Proof.

See the proof of
[
4
, Lemma 1 and 2]
. Remark that in the above reference, the result is formulated for the domain
\mathbb{R}^{2,+}{+\infty}
, but extends easily to
\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}
,
a<\infty
.
∎

The key technical result of this section is the following. It was proven in
[
4
]
, but our point is to show that it is also valid for
\mathbb{A}
as in Assumption
2.1
.

Proposition D.6
.

It holds that
\|\mathcal{J}U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a})}\leq C_{\delta}\|f\|_{\Omega_{p}}
.

Proof.

Remark that
U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}
satisfies the following variational formulation:

\displaystyle((x+i\nu)\mathbb{A}_{\delta}\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta},\nabla v)_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}=-(F^{\nu}_{r,\delta},v),\quad\forall v\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\mathbb{R}_{a}^{2,+}),

(132)

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{\delta}=\mathbb{A}\chi_{\ell,2\delta}+(1-\chi_{\ell,2\delta})\mathbb{I},

where we used that
\chi_{\ell,2\delta}=1
on
\operatorname{supp}\chi_{\ell,2\delta}
, and
U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}=0
for
|y|>\delta
. Remark that this is a Hermitian positive definite
C^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a})
-matrix.

Choosing
v=\mathcal{J}^{2}U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}
, with
v=0
on
x=0
, cf. Lemma
4.9
, and using the symmetry of
\mathcal{J}
of Lemma
E.1
, yields

\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}(x+i\nu)\mathbb{A}_{\delta}\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta},\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta})_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}=-(F^{\nu}_{\delta},\mathcal{J}^{2}U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}).

The goal is to obtain a control on
\|\mathcal{J}U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}}
, thus we rewrite the left-hand side using a commutator
[\mathcal{J},(x+i\nu)\mathbb{A}_{2\delta})u]=[\mathcal{J},\mathbb{A}_{2\delta}](x+i\nu)
:

\displaystyle((x+i\nu)\mathbb{A}_{2\delta}\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta},\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta})_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}+([\mathcal{J},\mathbb{A}_{2\delta}](x+i\nu)\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta},\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta})_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}=-(F^{\nu}_{\delta},\mathcal{J}^{2}U^{\nu}_{r,\delta})_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}.

Remark that
\mathcal{J}\nabla=\nabla\mathcal{J}
. Taking the real part of the above yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|x^{1/2}\nabla\mathcal{J}U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}&\lesssim\left(\|x^{1/2}[\mathcal{J},\mathbb{A}_{2\delta}]\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}\|x^{1/2}\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}+\nu\operatorname{Im}([\mathcal{J},\mathbb{A}_{2\delta}]\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta},\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta})\right)_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}\\
&+\|F^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}\|\mathcal{J}^{2}U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}.\end{split}

(133)

Next, we estimate each term in the above.

With Lemma
E.2
, and next (
127
), which extends to
U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}
,

\displaystyle\|x^{1/2}[\mathcal{J},\mathbb{A}_{2\delta}]\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}=\|x^{1/2}[\mathcal{J},(\mathbb{A}_{2\delta}-\mathbb{I})\chi_{\ell,2\delta}]\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}\lesssim\|x^{1/2}\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}\lesssim\|F^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}.

(134)

Remark that
C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega}_{p})
-regularity of
\mathbb{A}
is sufficient to apply Lemma
E.2
, cf. Remark
E.3
.

By Lemma
E.4
, using that
[\mathcal{J},\mathbb{A}_{2\delta}]=[\mathcal{J},(\mathbb{A}-\mathbb{I})\chi_{\ell,2\delta}]
,

\displaystyle|\operatorname{Im}([\mathcal{J},\mathbb{A}_{2\delta}]\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta},\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta})_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}|\lesssim\|[\mathcal{J}^{2},\mathbb{A}_{2\delta}]\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}\|\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}},

and with the use of Lemma
E.2
, and next (
128
) adapted to
U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle|\operatorname{Im}([\mathcal{J},\mathbb{A}_{2\delta}]\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta},\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta})_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}|\lesssim\|\nabla U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}^{2}\lesssim\nu^{-1}\|F^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}^{2}.

(135)

Finally, by Lemma
D.5
,
\|\mathcal{J}^{2}U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}\lesssim\|x^{1/2}\nabla\mathcal{J}U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}
. Combining this estimate, (
135
), (
134
) into (
133
), and using
\|F^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\Omega_{p}}
, yields

\displaystyle\|x^{1/2}\nabla\mathcal{J}U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\Omega_{p}}.

∎

An immediate corollary of Proposition
D.6
and Lemma
D.5
is the bound

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{J}^{2}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\Omega_{p}}.

From
u^{\nu}_{r}=U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}
on
\Omega_{p}
, the above and (
50
) it is immediate that

Corollary D.7
.

Let
u^{\nu}_{r}
be the unique solution of (
125
), where the right-hand side
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
. With some
C>0
, for all
\nu>0
, it holds that
\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
.

At this point, we will stop working with
U^{\nu}_{r,\delta}
, and get back to the original problem (
125
).
We now proceed by proving the bound (
130
).

D.1.2
Proof of (
130
)

The key auxiliary result is

Proposition D.8
.

For all
\nu>0
,
\nu\|\partial_{y}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{\Omega_{p}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\Omega_{p}}
.

Proof.

The proof mimics the proof of Proposition
E.6
, cf. in particular (
161
) and the bounds of Theorem
D.2
.
∎

Proposition D.9
.

For all
\nu>0
,
\|\partial_{y}(x\nabla u^{\nu}_{r})\|_{\Omega_{p}}\leq C\|f\|_{\Omega_{p}}
.

Proof.

We start with the variational formulation (
126
). We use the Nirenberg’s finite difference quotient, defined as
\delta_{y}^{h}v(x,y)=\frac{v(x,y+h)-v(x,y)}{h}
, for
v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega_{p})
, where
v
is extended by periodicity for
|y|>\ell
(see the proof of Proposition
E.6
).

Let us test (
125
) with the Nirenberg’s finite difference quotient
x\delta_{y}^{h}\delta_{y}^{-h}u^{\nu}_{r}
, which yields, cf. (
126
),

\displaystyle a_{r}^{\nu}(u^{\nu}_{r},x\delta_{y}^{h}\delta_{y}^{-h}u^{\nu}_{r})=-\int_{\Omega_{p}}fx\overline{\delta_{y}^{h}\delta_{y}^{-h}u^{\nu}_{r}}.

The same computations as in Appendix
G
show that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}(x+i\nu)\mathbb{A}\delta^{y}_{-h}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r}\,\overline{x\delta^{y}_{-h}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r}}+\int_{\Omega_{p}}(x+i\nu)\mathbb{A}\delta^{y}_{-h}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r}\mathbf{e}_{x}\overline{\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}_{r}}

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega_{p}}(x+i\nu)(\delta_{-h}^{y}\mathbb{A})\nabla u^{\nu}_{r}\left(x\overline{\nabla\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}_{r}}+\overline{\mathbf{e}_{x}\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}_{r}}\right)=\int_{\Omega}fx\overline{\delta_{h}^{y}\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}_{r}}.

Taking the real part of the above, and using that, in particular,
\mathbb{A}\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega_{p}})
, with appropriate periodicity constraints, we conclude that

\displaystyle\|x\delta_{-h}^{y}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|x\delta_{-h}^{y}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\|\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\nu\|\delta_{-h}^{y}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\|\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}

\displaystyle+(\|x\nabla u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\nu\|\nabla u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})})(\|x\nabla\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\|\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})})+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\|x\delta_{-h}^{y}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}.

Using (
127
), (
128
), the estimate of
[
30
, Lemma 4.13]
and
\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
(cf. Corollary
D.7
), we obtain the stated estimate.
∎

The stated inequality (
130
) is a corollary of Propositions
D.8
and
D.9
together with the estimates of Theorem
D.2
.

D.1.3
Proof of (
131
)

We start by remarking that

\displaystyle\partial_{x}((x+i\nu)\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r})=-(x+i\nu)\partial_{y}(\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r})+f,

therefore, with Theorem
D.3
, Corollary
D.7
, Propositions
D.8
,
D.9
, and Assumption
2.1
, it holds that
\|\partial_{x}((x+i\nu)\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r})\|\lesssim\|f\|
. Since

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}\partial_{x}((x+i\nu)\nabla u^{\nu}_{r})=\partial_{x}((x+i\nu)\mathbb{A}\nabla u^{\nu}_{r})-(\partial_{x}\mathbb{A})\cdot(x+i\nu)\nabla u^{\nu},

we conclude, using Theorem
D.3
, about the validity of the inequality (
131
).

D.2
Proof of Theorem
D.1
for the case
f\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})

D.2.1
An auxiliary result

The proof of this result of
[
4
]
uses a Hardy inequality similar to the one of Lemma
D.10
, however, the latter is stated without proof. One proof can be found in Theorem (I.1) of
[
2
]
, however, it has to be adapted to take into account that we do not have a vanishing derivative at
0
.

Lemma D.10
.

Let
q\in H^{2}(0,1)
,
q(0)=0
,
q(1)=0
. Then
x\mapsto q(x)/x\in H^{1}(0,1)
, with
\|q/x\|_{H^{1}(0,1)}\lesssim\|q^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{2}(0,1)}
.

Proof.

Assume that
q^{\prime\prime}=f
,
f\in L^{2}(0,1)
, then, necessarily, it holds that

\displaystyle q(x)=\int_{0}^{x}f(t)(x-t)dt+cx,\text{ so that }q(x)/x=\int_{0}^{x}f(t)\left(1-\frac{t}{x}\right)dt+c,\quad c=-\int_{0}^{1}f(t)(x-t)dt.

By the standard Hardy’s inequality, cf. (
35
),
x\mapsto q(x)/x\in L^{2}(0,1)
. By a direct computation we obtain

\displaystyle(q(x)/x)^{\prime}=\frac{1}{x^{2}}\int_{0}^{x}f(t)tdt\implies|(q(x)/x)^{\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{x}\int_{0}^{x}|f(t)|dt.

Applying (
35
) to the above, we conclude that
\|(q(x)/x)^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(0,1)}\leq\|f\|_{L^{2}(0,1)}
.
∎

Remark D.11
.

By a direct computation, it can be verified that if
q\in H^{m}(0,1)
,
q(0)=0
, and
q^{(j)}(1)=0
,
j=0,\ldots,m-2
, then
x\mapsto q(x)/x\in H^{m-1}(0,1)
, and
\|q/x\|_{H^{m}(0,1)}\lesssim\|q^{(m)}\|_{L^{2}(0,1)}
.

The chosen boundary condition at
x=1
is not essential for the validity of the result.

By the density of
\mathcal{C}^{1}_{comp}(\Omega_{p})\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}(\Omega_{p})
in the space
\{v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\Sigma,0}:\,\partial_{x}^{2}v\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})\}
, the above extends to
\Omega_{p}
.

Corollary D.12
.

Assume that
q\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega_{p})=\{v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p}):\,\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}v=0\}
, and, additionally,
\partial_{x}^{2}q\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
. Then
(x,y)\mapsto\partial_{x}(q(x,y)/x)\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, moreover,
\|\partial_{x}(q/x)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|\partial_{x}^{2}q\|_{L^{2}(0,1)}
.

D.2.2
Proof of Theorem
D.1
for
f\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})

Consider the unique solution
u_{r}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
(exists by the first part of Theorem
D.1
) to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{r})=f,\quad f\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p}),\\
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}u_{r}=0,\,\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}}u_{r}=0,\text{ periodic BCs at }y=\pm\ell.\end{split}

(136)

The key idea is again to regularize the problem by adding an absorption term and using the Dirichlet boundary conditions on
\Sigma
, i.e. rewrite the problem in the form (
125
). Then by the standard elliptic regularity result,
u^{\nu}_{r}\in\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Omega_{p})
, see
[
30
, Theorem 4.18]
or
[
18
, Theorem 2.5.1.1]
. In this case it is not difficult to verify that
\partial_{y}u^{\nu}_{r}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
satisfies (remark that we used below the periodicity of
\mathbb{A}
in the direction
y
to conclude from the original formulation that
\partial_{y}u^{\nu}_{r}
satisfies periodic boundary conditions at
y=\pm\ell
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu)\mathbb{A}\nabla\partial_{y}u^{\nu}_{r})+\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu)(\partial_{y}\mathbb{A})\nabla u^{\nu}_{r})=\partial_{y}f,\\
&\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\partial_{y}u^{\nu}_{r}=0,\qquad\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}}\partial_{y}u^{\nu}_{r}=0,\text{ periodic BCs at }y=\pm\ell.\end{split}

(137)

With Theorems
D.2
and
D.3
, the term below is uniformly bounded in
\nu>0
, provided that
\partial_{y}\mathbb{A}\in C^{0,1}(\overline{\Omega}_{p})
:

\displaystyle\|\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu)(\partial_{y}\mathbb{A})\nabla u^{\nu}_{r})\|\leq\|\partial_{x}((x+i\nu)\nabla u^{\nu}_{r})\|+\|(x+i\nu)\nabla u^{\nu}\|+\|(x+i\nu)\partial_{y}\nabla u^{\nu}\|.

We conclude that

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu)\mathbb{A}\nabla\partial_{y}u^{\nu}_{r})=\tilde{f}^{\nu},\qquad\|\tilde{f}^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})},

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\partial_{y}u^{\nu}_{r}=0,\qquad\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}}\partial_{y}u^{\nu}_{r}=0,\text{ periodic BCs at }y=\pm\ell.

By Theorem
D.2
, we see that
\partial_{y}u^{\nu}_{r}
is uniformly bounded in
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
. Therefore, with Theorem
D.3
, and using the uniqueness of the weak
L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
-limit, we conclude that
\partial_{y}u_{r}\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
. We have extracted the necessary information from (
137
), and now start working with the original problem (
136
).

Weakly,
\partial_{y}u_{r}\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
satisfies, cf. (
136
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla\partial_{y}u_{r})+\operatorname{div}(x(\partial_{y}\mathbb{A})\,\nabla u_{r})=\partial_{y}f,

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\partial_{y}u_{r}=0,\qquad\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}}\partial_{y}u_{r}=0,\text{ periodic BCs at }y=\pm\ell.

and, as shown in the part 1 of Theorem
D.1
, if
\mathbb{A}\in C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega_{p}};\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
, then

\displaystyle\|\operatorname{div}(x(\partial_{y}\mathbb{A})\,\nabla u_{r})\|\lesssim\|f\|.

Thus
\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla\partial_{y}u_{r})\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, and we can apply to
\partial_{y}u_{r}
the part 1 of Theorem
D.1
. In particular,

\displaystyle\|\nabla\partial_{y}u_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\|x\partial_{y}u_{r}\|_{H^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})}.

(138)

It remains to show that the result holds true when
\partial_{y}u_{r}
is replaced in the above by
\partial_{x}u_{r}
. More precisely, we want to show that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{x}^{2}u_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\|x\partial_{x}^{3}u_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})}.

(139)

For this, we rewrite (
136
) once again, to see that

\displaystyle\partial_{x}(x\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{r})=f-x\mathbf{e}_{y}\cdot(\partial_{y}\mathbb{A})\nabla u_{r}-x\mathbf{e}_{y}\mathbb{A}\cdot\nabla\partial_{y}u_{r},

so that, with
q:=x\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{r}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{2}q

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}f-\mathbf{e}_{y}\cdot(\partial_{y}\mathbb{A})\nabla u_{r}-x\mathbf{e}_{y}\cdot(\partial_{xy}\mathbb{A})\nabla u_{r}-x\mathbf{e}_{y}\cdot\partial_{x}\nabla u_{r}-\mathbf{e}_{y}\mathbb{A}\nabla\partial_{y}u_{r}

\displaystyle-x\mathbf{e}_{y}\partial_{x}\mathbb{A}\cdot\nabla\partial_{y}u_{r}-x\mathbf{e}_{y}\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla\partial_{xy}u_{r}.

From the bounds (
138
), part 1 of Theorem
D.1
and regularity assumptions on
\mathbb{A}
, we conclude that
\|\partial_{x}^{2}q\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})}.

Since
\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{r}=0
by Lemma
3.5
, and
q\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
by the part 1 of Theorem
D.1
, we have that
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}q=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}(x\mathbf{e}_{x}\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{r})=\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{r}=0
. Therefore,
q
satisfies conditions of Corollary
D.12
, and we conclude that
\|\partial_{x}(q/x)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}=\|\partial_{x}(\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{r})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})}
. With (
138
) and
\|\nabla u_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{x}^{2}u_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})}.

(140)

Next, rewriting
\partial_{x}^{2}q
by definition of
q
, we we obtain that

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{2}q=x\partial_{x}^{2}(\mathbf{e}_{x}\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{r})+2\partial_{x}(\mathbf{e}_{x}\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{r}),

and, therefore, with (
140
) and (
138
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\|x\partial_{x}^{3}u_{r}\|_{H^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})},

thus the desired bound (
139
).

E
Proof of Theorem
3.14

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma}
be as in (
9
),
and the family of cut-off functions
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
be defined in (
2.1.2
); recall that
\varphi_{\varepsilon}=1
on
\Omega^{\varepsilon/2}_{\Sigma}
and vanishes on
\Omega\setminus\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma}
; also, it depends on
x
only.
Then

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)\overline{v}=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\int_{\Omega_{p}}\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)\overline{v}(1-\varphi_{\varepsilon})d\mathbf{x}=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\int_{\Omega_{p}\setminus\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\varepsilon/2}}\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)\overline{v}(1-\varphi_{\varepsilon})d\mathbf{x}.

We integrate by parts and use that
(1-\varphi_{\varepsilon})|_{|x|=\varepsilon/2}=0
,
v=0
on
\Gamma_{p}
:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}\setminus\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\varepsilon/2}}\operatorname{div}

\displaystyle(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)\overline{v}(1-\varphi_{\varepsilon})d\mathbf{x}=-\int_{\Omega_{p}\setminus\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\varepsilon/2}}x\mathbb{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\overline{v}(1-\varphi_{\varepsilon})d\mathbf{x}+\int_{\Omega_{p}\setminus\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\varepsilon/2}}x\mathbb{A}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{e}_{x}\overline{v}\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}d\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{p}\setminus\Omega^{\varepsilon/2}_{\Sigma}}u\overline{\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla v)}(1-\varphi_{\varepsilon})-\int_{\Omega_{p}\setminus\Omega^{\varepsilon/2}_{\Sigma}}xu\overline{\mathbb{A}\nabla v}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{x}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}+\int_{\Omega_{p}\setminus\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\varepsilon/2}}x\mathbb{A}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{e}_{x}\overline{v}\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}d\mathbf{x}.

Taking
\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}
of both sides of the above yields the following identity:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla u)\overline{v}

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\underbrace{\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}x\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\left({\mathbb{A}\nabla u}\overline{v}-\overline{\mathbb{A}\nabla v}u\right)\;\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}_{\mathcal{I}^{\varepsilon}}+\int_{\Omega_{p}}u\overline{\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}\nabla v)}.

(141)

With the decomposition
u=u_{h}\log|x|+u_{reg}
,
v=v_{h}\log|x|+v_{reg}
, the term
\mathcal{I}^{\varepsilon}
rewrites

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j}\mathcal{I}_{j}^{\varepsilon},\quad\mathcal{I}^{\varepsilon}_{1}:=\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}x\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\left(\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{reg}\,\overline{v_{h}}-\overline{\mathbb{A}\nabla v_{reg}}{u_{h}}\right)\log|x|\partial_{x}\varphi_{\varepsilon},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\varepsilon}_{2}:=\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}x\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\left(\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{reg}\,\overline{v_{reg}}-\overline{\mathbb{A}\nabla v_{reg}}{u_{reg}}\right)\partial_{x}\varphi_{\varepsilon},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\varepsilon}_{3}:=\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}x\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\left(\mathbb{A}(\log|x|\nabla u_{h}+\frac{\mathbf{e}_{x}}{x}u_{h})\,\overline{v_{reg}}-\overline{\mathbb{A}(\log|x|\nabla v_{h}+\frac{\mathbf{e}_{x}}{x}v_{h})}{u_{reg}}\right)\partial_{x}\varphi_{\varepsilon},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\varepsilon}_{4}:=\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}x\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\left(\mathbb{A}(\log|x|\nabla u_{h}+\frac{\mathbf{e}_{x}}{x}u_{h})\,\overline{v_{h}}-\overline{\mathbb{A}(\log|x|\nabla v_{h}+\frac{\mathbf{e}_{x}}{x}v_{h})}{u_{h}}\right)\log|x|\partial_{x}\varphi_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}x\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\left(\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{h}\overline{v_{h}}-\overline{\mathbb{A}\nabla v_{h}}u_{h}\right)\log^{2}|x|\partial_{x}\varphi_{\varepsilon}.

In the last identity we used
\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\mathbb{A}\mathbf{e}_{x}=\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\overline{\mathbb{A}\mathbf{e}_{x}}=\mathbb{A}_{11}\in\mathbb{R}
, valid since
\mathbb{A}
is Hermitian. We will see that only
\mathcal{I}^{\varepsilon}_{3}
will not converge to zero as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Therefore, let us now examine the remaining integrals.

Proof that
\mathcal{I}_{j}^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 0
, as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, with
j\in\{1,2,4\}
.
We treat these integrals in a similar manner, therefore, let us combine the relevant estimates. First of all, remark that with
C
independent on
\varepsilon
, it holds

\displaystyle\|x\partial_{x}\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})}\leq\|x\partial_{x}\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma})}\leq C\|x/\varepsilon\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma})}\leq C,

(142)

where we used the definition of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
and the fact that
\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\overline{\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\varepsilon}}
.
Also, for functions
q,p\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
, with
\delta<1
, it holds, for any
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and
a\in\{0,1,2\}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}|\log^{a}|x|\;\mathbf{e}\cdot\nabla q\,p|d\mathbf{x}\lesssim\left(\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}|x^{\delta/2}\nabla q|^{2}dx\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}|\log^{a}|x|\;x^{-\delta/2}p|^{2}dx\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}|x^{\delta/2}\nabla q|^{2}dx\right)^{1/2}\|p\|_{\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\left(\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}|x^{\delta/2}\nabla q|^{2}dx\right)^{1/2}\|p\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})}\rightarrow 0,\quad\text{ as }\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+,

(143)

where we used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in the first line and the Hardy inequality of Proposition
B.4
in the second line (remark that
\delta<1
).

By Theorem
3.9
, we have that
u_{reg},\,v_{reg},\,u_{h},\,v_{h}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
, for any
\delta>0
. Now we have all necessary ingredients to prove the desired result.
First, to show that
\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\varepsilon}=0
, we use (
142
) which yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}\left(|\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla u_{reg}\,\overline{v_{h}}|+|\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\overline{\mathbb{A}\nabla v_{reg}}\,u_{h}|\right)|\log x|d\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=C\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}\left(|\mathbb{A}^{t}\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\nabla u_{reg}\,\overline{v_{h}}|+|\mathbb{A}\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\overline{\nabla v_{reg}}\,u_{h}|\right)|\log x|d\mathbf{x}.

It remains to use the bound (
143
) twice, first with
\mathbf{e}=\mathbb{A}^{t}\mathbf{e}_{x}
,
a=1
,
q=u_{reg}
and
p=\overline{v_{h}}
, and then with
\mathbf{e}=\mathbb{A}\mathbf{e}_{x}
,
a=1
,
q=\overline{v_{reg}}
and
p=u_{h}
. The terms
\mathcal{I}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
,
\mathcal{I}_{4}^{\varepsilon}
are treated similarly.

Proof that
\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\mathcal{I}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
yields the thought boundary terms.
By the same reasoning as above, some terms in the expression for
\mathcal{I}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
converge to zero, and it holds that
\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\mathcal{I}_{3}^{\varepsilon}=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\widetilde{\mathcal{I}_{3}^{\varepsilon}}
, where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{I}_{3}^{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}\partial_{x}\varphi_{\varepsilon}\mathbb{A}_{11}(u_{h}\overline{v}_{reg}-u_{reg}\overline{v}_{h})d\mathbf{x},\text{ with }\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\overline{\mathbb{A}\mathbf{e}_{x}}=\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\mathbb{A}\mathbf{e}_{x}=\mathbb{A}_{11}.

It remains to integrate by parts the above (the integration by parts is justified by
[
18
, Theorem 1.5.3.1]
and Lemma
B.5
in Appendix
B
, using Theorem
3.9
that states that
u_{reg}
,
v_{reg}
are from
\bigcap_{0<\delta<1/2}\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
):

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{I}_{3}^{\varepsilon}}=-\int_{\Sigma}a_{11}(u_{h}\overline{v}_{reg}-u_{reg}\overline{v}_{h})d\mathbf{x}-\int_{\Omega_{p}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}\partial_{x}\left(\mathbb{A}_{11}(u_{h}\overline{v}_{reg}-u_{reg}\overline{v}_{h})\right)d\mathbf{x},

By the Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, the last term tends to
0
, therefore

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\widetilde{\mathcal{I}_{3}^{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma}a_{11}(u_{h}\overline{v}_{reg}-u_{reg}\overline{v}_{h})d\mathbf{x}=-\int_{\Sigma}\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u\overline{\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}v_{reg}}+\int_{\Sigma}\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}u_{reg}\overline{\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}v_{reg}}.

This, together with previous considerations and (
141
), proves the desired result.

E.1
Proof of Propositions
4.12
and
4.13

The proof of Proposition
4.12
relies on some auxiliary facts on commutators of
\mathcal{J}
with multiplication operators. The result would have been easy to obtain, had we considered
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{T}=\operatorname{Id}
, cf. the proof of Proposition
E.5
; this seems to be not the case when
\mathbb{A},\,\mathbb{T}
are matrices.

Some of the results below are well-known; we chose to present them for the sake of completeness.

E.1.1
Preliminary results: properties of the Bessel potential

We start by remarking that
\mathcal{J}
is a symmetric operator, as follows from the Plancherel identity.

Lemma E.1
.

For all
u,v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
, it holds that
(\mathcal{J}u,v)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}=(u,\mathcal{J}v)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}.

Also, the operator
\mathcal{J}
commutes with multiplication by
y
-independent functions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\varphi(x)h)=\varphi(x)\mathcal{J}h,\quad\forall h\in H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}).

In what follows we will also need the following property, which can be verified by the density argument.
Let the trace operator
\gamma_{0}^{x=\pm a}
be defined via
\gamma_{0}^{x=\pm a}u(x,y)=u(\pm a,y)
for sufficiently regular
u:\,\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
. Then for all
u\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
, s.t.
\gamma_{0}^{x=\pm a}u=0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{x=\pm a}\mathcal{J}u=0,\qquad\gamma_{0}^{x=\pm a}\mathcal{J}^{2}u=0.

(144)

The result below is non-optimal, but sufficient for our needs.

Lemma E.2
.

Let
n\in\{1,2\}
,
\beta_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
. Then, there exists
C>0
, s.t. for all
p\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{a}^{2})
,
n\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle\|[\mathcal{J}^{n},\beta_{0}]p\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\leq C\|p\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}.

Remark E.3
.

We will often apply the above result in the case when
\beta_{0}\in C^{1,1}([-a,a]\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C})
and is compactly supported.

Proof.

By the density argument, it suffices to prove that
\|[\mathcal{J}^{n},\beta_{0}]p\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\leq C\|p\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}
, for all
p\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
.

We denote by
\xi
the Fourier variable in the direction
y
and introduce
\mu(\xi)=(1+\xi^{2})^{1/4}
, so that

\displaystyle[\mathcal{J}^{n},\beta_{0}]p=\mathcal{F}_{y}^{-1}\left(\mu^{n}\mathcal{F}_{y}\left(\beta_{0}p\right)\right)-\beta_{0}\mathcal{F}_{y}^{-1}\left(\mu^{n}\mathcal{F}_{y}p\right).

(145)

We use the notation
\hat{v}=\mathcal{F}_{y}v
. Also, for
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
,
v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{a}^{2})
, we denote the convolution in
y
-direction by

\displaystyle u\ast v(x,y):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(x,y-y^{\prime})v(x,y^{\prime})dy^{\prime}.

With
\mathcal{F}_{y}(uv)=\hat{u}\ast\hat{v}
, we can rewrite the equation (
145
) (recall that we assume that
p\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{y}\left([\mathcal{J}^{n},\beta_{0}]p\right)(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\left(\mu^{n}(\xi)\hat{\beta}_{0}\ast\hat{p}-\hat{\beta}_{0}\ast(\mu^{n}(\xi)\hat{p})\right)(x,\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{\beta}_{0}(x,\xi^{\prime})(\mu^{n}(\xi)-\mu^{n}(\xi-\xi^{\prime}))\hat{p}(x,\xi-\xi^{\prime})d\xi^{\prime}.

Next, we employ a Lipschitz bound on
\mu^{n}
, so that
|\mu^{n}(\xi)-\mu^{n}(\xi-\xi^{\prime})|\leq|\xi^{\prime}|\sup_{t\in[\xi^{\prime}-\xi,\xi]}\frac{n|t|}{2(1+t^{2})^{1-n/4}}\leq C|\xi^{\prime}|
,
since
n\in\{1,2\}
. This yields a.e.
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}
,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{F}_{y}\left([\mathcal{J}^{n},\beta]p\right)(x,\xi)|\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\xi^{\prime}\hat{\beta}_{0}(x,\xi^{\prime})||\hat{p}(x,\xi-\xi^{\prime})|d\xi^{\prime}.

We recognize in the right-hand side of the above a convolution, and use the Young inequality for convolutions:

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{F}_{y}\left([\mathcal{J}^{n},{\beta}_{0}]p\right)(x,.)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq C\|\hat{p}(x,.)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\,\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\xi\hat{\beta}_{0}(x,\xi)|d\xi\right)^{2},\quad\text{a.e. }x\in(-a,a).

(146)

Next, we use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\xi\hat{\beta}_{0}(x,\xi)|d\xi\right)^{2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\xi\hat{\beta}_{0}(x,\xi)|^{2}(1+\xi^{2})d\xi\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+\xi^{2})^{-1}d\xi\leq C\|\hat{\beta}_{0}(x,.)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

Therefore, from (
146
), the Plancherel identity, and the above bound, we conclude that

\displaystyle\|[\mathcal{J}^{n},\beta_{0}]p\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}^{2}=\|\mathcal{F}_{y}[\mathcal{J}^{n},\beta_{0}]p\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}^{2}\leq C\|p\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\|\beta_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}.

∎

We will also need a corresponding result on a commutator of a self-adjoint operator and
\mathcal{J}
.

Lemma E.4
.

Let
E:=(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}))^{2}
.
Let
\mathcal{A}:D(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow E
be a self-adjoint operator, with a domain
D(\mathcal{A})\subset E
. Then, for all
\mathbf{v}\in E
s.t.
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v},\mathcal{J}^{2}\mathbf{v}\in D(\mathcal{A})\text{ and }\mathcal{A}\mathbf{v}\in\left(H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})\right)^{2},
it holds that

\operatorname{Im}([\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},\mathcal{A}]\mathbf{v},\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v})_{E}=\frac{1}{2i}([\mathcal{J}^{2}\mathbb{I},\mathcal{A}]\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v})_{E}.

Proof.

By the definition of the commutator, for
\mathbf{v}
as in the statement of the lemma,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}([\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},\mathcal{A}]\mathbf{v},\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v})_{E}=\operatorname{Im}(\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}\mathbf{v},\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v})_{E}-\operatorname{Im}(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v},\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v})_{E}.

By self-adjointness,
(\mathcal{A}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u})_{E}=(\mathbf{u},\mathcal{A}\mathbf{u})_{E}=\overline{(\mathcal{A}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u})_{E}}
, thus the second term in the above vanishes. Therefore,

\displaystyle 2i\operatorname{Im}([\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},\mathcal{A}]\mathbf{v},\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v})_{E}=(\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}\mathbf{v},\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v})_{E}-(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v},\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}\mathbf{v})_{E}=(\mathcal{J}^{2}\mathcal{A}\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v})_{E}-(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{J}^{2}\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v})_{E},

where in the last identity we used first the self-adjointness of
\mathcal{J}
, see Lemma
E.1
, and next of
\mathcal{A}
.
∎

E.1.2
Proof of Proposition
4.12

Proposition
4.12
is a simple corollary of its counterpart with
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{I}
, as we argue on p.
E.1.2
.

Proposition E.5
.

Let
(u^{\nu})_{\nu>0}
solve (
P

ν

), and
U^{\nu}_{\delta}=\mathcal{E}_{\delta}u^{\nu}
satisfy (
51
). Then there exists
C>0
, s.t.

\displaystyle\nu\|\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|^{2}\leq C\left(\|f\|^{2}+\|f\|\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|\right),\text{ for all }0<\nu<1.

(147)

Proof.

To prove (
147
), we rewrite the extended problem (
51
) in a more convenient form. In particular, we decompose the matrix
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{D}+\mathbb{H}
, with
\mathbb{D}=\operatorname{diag}\mathbb{A}=\operatorname{diag}(\mathbb{A}_{11},\mathbb{A}_{22})
. Next, we define the modified matrices:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}&:=\mathbb{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbb{A}\chi_{\ell,2\delta}+\mathbb{I}(1-\chi_{\ell,2\delta})=\mathbb{I}+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{A}},\quad\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{A}}=(\mathbb{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbb{A}-\mathbb{I})\chi_{\ell,2\delta},\\
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}&:=\mathbb{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbb{T}\chi_{\ell,2\delta}+\mathbb{I}(1-\chi_{\ell,2\delta})=\mathbb{I}+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{T}},\quad\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{T}}=(\mathbb{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbb{A}-\mathbb{I})\chi_{\ell,2\delta},\\
\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}&:=\mathbb{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbb{D}\chi_{\ell,2\delta}+\mathbb{I}(1-\chi_{\ell,2\delta})=\mathbb{I}+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{D}},\quad\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{D}}=(\mathbb{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbb{D}-\mathbb{I})\chi_{\ell,2\delta},\\
\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}&:=\mathbb{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbb{H}\chi_{\ell,2\delta}+\mathbb{I}(1-\chi_{\ell,2\delta})=\mathbb{I}+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{H}},\quad\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{H}}=(\mathbb{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbb{H}-\mathbb{I})\chi_{\ell,2\delta}\end{split}

(148)

In the above,
\chi_{\ell,2\delta}
is the same truncation function as in (
4.2.1
). The above defined matrix-valued functions are constant and equal to
\mathbb{I}
for
|y|\geq\ell+2\delta
. The matrices
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}},\widetilde{\mathbb{T}},\widetilde{\mathbb{D}},\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}
are Hermitian and positive definite, and are
C^{1,1}([-a,a]\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
.
Moreover,
\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}_{11}(\mathbf{x})=1
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}
. As we will see further, this will allow us to avoid appearance of
\|F^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}\|\partial_{x}\mathcal{J}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}
in the right-hand side, which would have prevented us from obtaining the sharp bound (
147
).

Let us now rewrite the problem satisfied by
U^{\nu}_{\delta}
(
51
). We start by remarking that, as
\operatorname{supp}U^{\nu}_{\delta},\operatorname{supp}F^{\nu}_{\delta}\subseteq\operatorname{supp}\chi_{\ell,\delta}
, and
\chi_{\ell,2\delta}=1
on
\operatorname{supp}\chi_{\ell,\delta}
, the matrices
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{T}
in (
51
) can be replaced by
\mathbb{A}_{11}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}},\,\mathbb{A}_{11}\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left(\left(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T}\right)\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left(\mathbb{A}_{11}\left(x\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}+i\nu\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}\right)\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\right)=\nabla\mathbb{A}_{11}\cdot\left(x\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}+i\nu\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}\right)\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}+\mathbb{A}_{11}\operatorname{div}\left(\left(x\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}+i\nu\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}\right)\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\right).

The above can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left(\left(x\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}+i\nu\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}\right)\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\right)=\widetilde{F}^{\nu}_{\delta},\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a},

(149)

where the right-hand side
\widetilde{F}^{\nu}_{\delta}
satisfies the following bound, with some
C_{j}>0
,
j=1,2
, independent of
\nu>0
:

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{F}^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\leq C_{1}(\|x\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\nu\|\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})})+\|F^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\leq C_{2}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

(150)

with the latter bounds following from (
53
) and (
54
). Moreover,
\operatorname{supp}\widetilde{F}^{\nu}_{\delta}\subseteq{\operatorname{supp}}\chi_{\ell,2\delta}.

We test (
149
) with
\mathcal{J}^{2}U^{\nu}_{\delta}
, which belongs to
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})
, because of the identity (
50
) and the elliptic regularity for
U^{\nu}_{\delta}
as stated in Lemma
4.9
; moreover,
\mathcal{J}^{2}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\in H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
according to (
144
). Integrating by parts, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}(x\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}

\displaystyle+i\nu\widetilde{\mathbb{T}})\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{J}^{2}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}\widetilde{F}^{\nu}_{\delta}\overline{\mathcal{J}^{2}U^{\nu}_{\delta}}.

With Lemma
E.1
on the symmetry of
\mathcal{J}
, we obtain the new identity:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}(x\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}&+i\nu\widetilde{\mathbb{T}})\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}}+\overbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}[\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},(x\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}+i\nu\widetilde{\mathbb{T}})]\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\cdot\overline{\nabla\mathcal{J}U^{\nu}_{\delta}}}^{\mathcal{S}}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}\widetilde{F}^{\nu}_{\delta}\overline{\mathcal{J}^{2}U^{\nu}_{\delta}}.\end{split}

(151)

Taking the imaginary part of (
151
), and using the fact that
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}
is a Hermitian matrix, and
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
is Hermitian positive definite, yields the following inequality, with some
C>0
,

\displaystyle\nu\|\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}^{2}\leq C\left(|\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\|\widetilde{F}^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\|\mathcal{J}^{2}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\right).

(152)

The first term
|\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}|
in the right-hand side of the above needs to be treated with care, since a naïve bound using Lemma
E.2
and (
53
) would allow to replace in (
152
)
|\operatorname{Im}S|
by
\|F^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\|\nabla\mathcal{J}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}
, which would not yield (
147
) because of the loss of a half a power of
\nu
.

Bounding
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}
.
We rewrite
\mathcal{S}=\sum_{j}\mathcal{S}_{j},

with
\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{2},\mathcal{S}_{3}
defined as follows. With
\mathbf{v}=\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}
, and recalling that
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}=\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}+\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}x[\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}]\mathbf{v}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v}},\qquad\mathcal{S}_{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}x[\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}]\mathbf{v}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v}},\qquad\mathcal{S}_{3}=i\nu\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}x[\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}]\mathbf{v}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v}}.

(153)

A bound on
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}_{1}
.
We have, since
\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}_{11}=1
, see (
148
), and
\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}
is a diagonal matrix:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}_{1}=\operatorname{Im}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}x[\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}]\mathbf{v}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Im}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}x[\mathcal{J},\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}_{22}]v_{2}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{J}v_{2}}=\operatorname{Im}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}x[\mathcal{J},\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{D},22}]v_{2}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{J}v_{2}}.

(154)

Next, we make use of Lemma
E.4
, with
\mathcal{A}\mathbf{v}=x\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{D},22}v_{2}
(we recall that
\mathbb{A}
is Hermitian, thus
\mathbb{D}
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}
,
{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{D},22}
are real-valued). This yields

\displaystyle 2i\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}x[\mathcal{J}^{2},\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{D},22}]v_{2}\overline{v_{2}}.

Next, we employ Lemma
E.2
with compactly supported
\beta_{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{D},22}=(\mathbb{A}_{22}/\mathbb{A}_{11}-1)\chi_{\ell,2\delta}
, cf. Remark
E.3
, and use the regularity Assumption
2.1
, which results in

\displaystyle|\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}_{1}|\leq C\|xv_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\|v_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}.

Recalling that
v_{2}=\partial_{y}U^{\nu}_{\delta}
, the bound (
53
), namely
\|x\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
, and the bound (
49
) on
\|\partial_{y}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}_{1}\right|\leq C(\|u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)})\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

(155)

Remark that it is due to our rewriting (
149
) that
\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}=1
, and we do not have terms of the type
\|x\partial_{x}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|\|\partial_{x}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|
occurring in the bound on
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}_{1}
.

A bound on
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}_{2}
.

Let us consider

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}_{2}=\operatorname{Im}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a}}x[\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}]\mathbf{v}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v}}=\frac{1}{2i}\left(x[\mathcal{J}^{2}\mathbb{I},\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}]\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}\right),

(156)

where the last identity follows again by Lemma
E.4
with
\mathcal{A}\mathbf{v}=\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}\mathbf{v}
, and using the fact that
\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}
is Hermitian.
Since
\operatorname{diag}\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}=0
, and using the decomposition (
148
), we rewrite the above as follows:

\displaystyle|\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}_{2}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|x[\mathcal{J}^{2},\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}_{21}]v_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\|v_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\|[\mathcal{J}^{2},\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}_{12}]v_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\|xv_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}

\displaystyle=\|x[\mathcal{J}^{2},\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{H},21}]v_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\|v_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\|[\mathcal{J}^{2},\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{H},12}]v_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\|xv_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}.

By the repeated application of Lemma
E.2
, first with
\beta_{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{H},21}
, next with
\beta_{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{H},12}
, and using
\mathbf{v}=\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}
,

\displaystyle|\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}_{2}|\lesssim\|x\partial_{x}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\|\partial_{y}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}(\|u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}),

(157)

with the last bound obtained like in (
155
).

A bound on
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}_{3}
.
It remains to consider the remaining term, namely,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}_{3}=\nu\operatorname{Re}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{a}^{2}}[\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},\mathbb{\widetilde{T}}]\mathbf{v}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v}}=\nu\operatorname{Re}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{a}^{2}}[\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{T}}]\mathbf{v}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v}}.

(158)

With the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we obtain that

\displaystyle|\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}_{3}|\leq\nu\|[\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{T}}]\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\|{\mathcal{J}\mathbf{v}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}.

Next we use Lemma
E.2
(where
\beta_{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{T},ij},\;i,j\in\{1,2\}
), which gives, together with
\mathbf{v}=\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}
,

\displaystyle|\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}_{3}|\lesssim\nu\|\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\|\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\overset{\eqref{eq:stabU}}{\lesssim}\nu^{1/2}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}.

(159)

The final bound on
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}
.
Gathering (
155
), (
157
), (
159
) into
\mathcal{S}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\mathcal{S}_{j}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle|\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{S}|\leq\nu^{1/2}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}(\|u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}).

(160)

Bounding
\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}
.
We get back to (
152
). We employ (
160
), the bound for
\widetilde{F}^{\nu}_{\delta}
in (
150
), as well as (
50
), namely,
\|\mathcal{J}^{2}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\leq\left(\|U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\|\partial_{y}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\right)
. This results in the following inequality:

\displaystyle\nu\|\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\nu^{1/2}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\left(\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}(\|U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\|\partial_{y}U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}).

Next, we use (
49
), which allows to replace
U^{\nu}_{\delta}
by
u^{\nu}
in the last term:

\displaystyle\nu\|\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\nu^{1/2}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\left(\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

Finally, with (
43
), we deduce that the following inequality holds true with some
C>0
independent of
\nu
, uniformly in
0<\nu<1
:

\displaystyle\nu\|\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\leq C(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\times\nu^{1/2}\|\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)})

and the desired bound follows by applying the Young’s inequality to the above.
∎

Now we are fully equipped to prove Proposition
4.12
.

Proof of Proposition
4.12

Since
U^{\nu}_{\delta}
vanishes outside of
\operatorname{supp}\chi_{\ell,2\delta}
, we can define, like in the proof of Proposition
E.5
, cf. (
148
),

\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}=\mathbb{B}\chi_{\ell,2\delta}+\mathbb{I}(1-\chi_{\ell,2\delta})=(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I})\chi_{\ell,2\delta}+\mathbb{I},

so that
\mathbb{B}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}=\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}
. Then

\displaystyle\nu^{1/2}\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{B}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})

\displaystyle=\nu^{1/2}\mathcal{J}(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})=\nu^{1/2}\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}+[\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}]\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}=\nu^{1/2}\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}\mathcal{J}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}+\nu^{1/2}[\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},\left(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}\right)\chi_{\ell,2\delta}]\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}.

The first term is bounded with Proposition
E.5
. For the second term we use Lemma
E.2
, cf. Remark
E.3
:

\nu^{1/2}\|[\mathcal{J}\mathbb{I},\left(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}\right)\chi_{\ell,2\delta}]\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\lesssim\nu^{1/2}\|\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{a})}\overset{\eqref{eq:stabU}}{\lesssim}\|f\|.

E.1.3
Proof of Proposition
4.13

Proposition
4.13
is again a corollary of its counterpart for
u^{\nu}
, with
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{I}
, namely

Proposition E.6
.

There exists
C>0
, s.t. for all
\nu>0
, the following holds true:

\displaystyle\nu\|\nabla\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|+\nu^{3/2}\|\nabla\partial_{x}u^{\nu}\|\leq C\|f\|.

Proof.

Proof of the bound
\nu\|\nabla\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|f\|
.
We use the Nirenberg’s quotient techniques.
For
v
sufficiently regular, and
h\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
small enough, we define the Nirenberg’s quotient which takes into account periodic boundary conditions, in the following manner:

\displaystyle\delta_{h}^{y}v=\frac{\tau_{h}^{y}v-v}{h},\quad\tau_{h}^{y}v=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v(x,y+h),&y+h\text{ and }y\in[-\ell,\ell],\\
v(x,y-2\ell+h),&y+h>\ell,\,y\in[-\ell,\ell],\\
v(x,y+2\ell+h),&y+h<-\ell,\,y\in[-\ell,\ell].\end{array}\right.

Remark that
\delta_{h}^{y}v(x,\ell)=\delta_{h}^{y}v(x,-\ell)
for
v
s.t.
v(x,\ell)=v(x,-\ell)
.

Next, we test the problem with absorption (
P

ν

) with
\delta_{h}^{y}\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}
and integrate by parts, first at the continuous level, and next at the discrete level; we make use of periodic boundary conditions as well. This allows to obtain the following identity:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}\cdot\overline{\nabla\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}}+\int_{\Omega}\left(\delta_{-h}^{y}\left(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T}\right)\right)\nabla u^{\nu}\cdot\overline{\nabla\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}}=\int_{\Omega}f\,\overline{\delta_{h}^{y}\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}}.

Next, we take the imaginary part of the above, use that
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}^{*}
and
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}^{*}
, and bound the sign-indefinite terms (namely the second term in the left-hand side) with the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu\|\nabla\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}\|^{2}&\lesssim\left(\max_{i,j}\|\delta_{-h}^{y}\mathbb{A}_{ij}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\|x\,\nabla u^{\nu}\|+\max_{i,j}\|\delta_{-h}^{y}\mathbb{T}_{ij}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\nu\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|\right)\|\nabla\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}\|+\|f\|\|\delta_{h}^{y}\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}\|.\end{split}

(161)

We conclude, using the stability estimate of Theorem
4.2
and the estimate (
43
):

\displaystyle\nu\|\nabla\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}\|^{2}\lesssim\|f\|\|\nabla\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}\|+\|f\|\|\partial_{y}\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}\|,

where we employed as well
[
30
, Lemma 4.13]
to bound
\|\delta_{h}^{y}.\|
by
\|\partial_{y}.\|
. The above yields immediately

\nu\|\nabla\delta_{-h}^{y}u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|f\|,

and next we use again
[
30
, Lemma 4.13]
on the connection of difference quotients and derivatives, we obtain the first part of the desired bound in the statement of the proposition.

Proof of the bound
\nu^{3/2}\|\nabla\partial_{x}u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|f\|
.

We start by proving the desired bound in
\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\varepsilon}
,
\varepsilon>0
.
We consider the problem (
P

ν

) written for
u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(x,y):=u^{\nu}(x,y)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)
, where
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
is the cut-off function in
x
-direction defined in (
2.1.2
) and
0<\varepsilon<a/2
is fixed. This yields the new problem

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle=[\operatorname{div}\left((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla.\right),\varphi_{\varepsilon}]u^{\nu}+\varphi_{\varepsilon}f

\displaystyle=(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla u^{\nu}+\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}\cdot(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu}+\operatorname{div}((x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon})u^{\nu}

\displaystyle=f_{\varepsilon}^{\nu},\quad\text{ with }\|f^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\|\lesssim\|u^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\Omega)}+\nu\|\nabla u^{\nu}\|+\|u^{\nu}\|\lesssim\|f\|,

(162)

where the last bound follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, Theorem
4.2
and (
43
). Next we test the above with the Nirenberg’s quotient
\delta_{h}^{x}\delta_{-h}^{x}u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}
, defined via

\displaystyle\delta_{h}^{x}v=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{v(x+h,y)-v(x,y)}{h},&\quad|x|,|x+h|<a,\\
0,&\text{ otherwise, }\end{array}\right.\quad h\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\},

which, due to the localization, belongs to
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
. Integrating by parts yields
