\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla\delta_{-h}^{x}u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\overline{\nabla\delta_{-h}^{x}u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}}+\int_{\Omega}\left(\delta_{-h}^{x}\left(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T}\right)\right)\nabla u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\overline{\nabla\delta_{-h}^{x}u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}}=\int_{\Omega}f^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\,\overline{\delta_{h}^{x}\delta_{-h}^{x}u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}}.

Proceeding like before, and noting that
\delta^{x}_{-h}x\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}+x\delta^{x}_{-h}\mathbb{A}
, we obtain a counterpart of the bound (
161
):

\displaystyle\nu\|\nabla\delta_{-h}^{x}u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}\lesssim\left(\|\nabla u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\|+\|x\nabla u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\|\right)\|\nabla\delta_{-h}^{x}u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\|+\|f^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\|\|\delta_{h}^{x}\delta_{-h}^{x}u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\|.

Next, we employ the inequality (
43
) to bound
\|\nabla u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\|
, Theorem
4.2
for
\|x\nabla u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\|
and (
162
) for
\|f^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\|
:

\displaystyle\nu\|\nabla\delta_{-h}^{x}u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}\lesssim\nu^{-1/2}\|f\|\|\nabla\delta_{-h}^{x}u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\|+\|f\|\|\delta_{h}^{x}\delta_{-h}^{x}u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\|.

Proceeding like in before yields the desired estimate for
\nu\|\nabla\partial_{x}u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\|
. As for the
u^{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=u^{\nu}(x,y)(1-\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x,y))
, the interested reader can verify that it satisfies the problem analogous to (
162
), which can be posed on
\Omega\setminus\overline{\Omega_{\Sigma}^{\varepsilon/2}}
, with
\left.u^{\nu}(x,y)(1-\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x,y))\right|_{x=\varepsilon/2}=0
. The resulting problem decouples into two independent elliptic problems, and the respective stability estimate is
\nu
-independent, cf. e.g.
[
30
, pp. 133-141]
.
∎

Now we can prove Proposition
4.13
.

Proof of Proposition
4.13
.

First of all, remark that

\displaystyle\nu\|\partial_{y}(\mathbb{B}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta})\|\lesssim\nu\|\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|+\nu\|\partial_{y}\nabla U^{\nu}_{\delta}\|\lesssim\nu(\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|+\|u^{\nu}\|)+\nu\|\partial_{y}\nabla u^{\nu}\|,

where we used (
49
). It remains to apply the bound (
43
) for
\nu\|u^{\nu}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\lesssim\nu^{1/2}\|f\|
and the previous Proposition
4.13
to obtain the desired estimate in the statement of the proposition.
∎

We will also use the above bounds written in the following form:

\displaystyle\nu\|\partial_{y}(\mathbb{B}\nabla u^{\nu})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

(163)

F
Lifting lemma with improved regularity estimates

Below we will present the lifting lemma for functions defined on the real line
\mathbb{R}
.
We will construct the lifting using the same idea as in Lemma
C.3
, however, we need somewhat finer estimates which exploit the cases of low- and high-frequencies. We chose to keep all these results apart, despite the fact that we repeat some of the estimates, since such a generalization makes the elementary proof of Proposition
4.3
unnecessarily complicated.

Assume that
\psi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})
, and let us define
\Psi
equal to
0
for
|x|>\nu
and as a solution to the Dirichlet boundary-value problem for the Laplace equation on
(0,\nu)\times\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\Delta\Psi=0\text{ on }(0,\nu),\qquad\Psi(0,y)=\psi(y),\quad\Psi(\nu,y)=0,\,y\in\mathbb{R}.

The above problem is well-posed in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})
due to the Lax-Milgram lemma and the validity of the Poincaré inequality in infinite strips. More precisely, it can be shown that

\displaystyle\Psi=\mathcal{F}_{y}^{-1}\hat{\Psi},\quad\hat{\Psi}(x,\xi)=\lambda_{\nu}(x,\xi)\hat{\psi}(\xi)\mathbb{1}_{0<x<\nu},\quad\lambda_{\nu}(x,\xi):=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\xi(x-\nu)}-\mathrm{e}^{-\xi(x-\nu)}}{\mathrm{e}^{-\xi\nu}-\mathrm{e}^{\xi\nu}}.

For
0<\nu<a
, we denote by
L^{\nu}
the operator mapping
H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})\ni\psi
into
\Psi\in\{u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}):\,u(a)=0\}
; we will use the same notation for the operator mapping from
\Psi
into the restriction of
\Psi
to
\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu}
. Some of the properties of this operator are summarized below.

Lemma F.1
.

Let
0<\nu<a
. The operator
L^{\nu}
satisfies:

1.

L^{\nu}\in\mathcal{L}({H}^{s}(\Sigma_{\infty}),H^{s+1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu}))
, for all
s\geq 1/2
.

2.

L^{\nu}\in\mathcal{L}(H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty}),H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}))
.

3.

For all
w>0
,
L^{\nu}\mathcal{L}_{w}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{w}L^{\nu}
,
L^{\nu}\mathcal{H}_{w}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}_{w}L^{\nu}
.

4.

For all
\psi\in H^{3/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})
,
\partial_{y}L^{\nu}\psi=L^{\nu}\partial_{y}\psi
.

Let
0<\varepsilon\leq 1/2
be fixed. Then, for all
\psi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})
, with
C_{\varepsilon}>0
independent of
\nu
, but depending on
\varepsilon>0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}L^{\nu}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\nu^{-1}}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|\psi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})},

(164)

\displaystyle\nu^{1/2}\|\mathcal{J}\partial_{x}L^{\nu}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\nu^{-1}}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|\psi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})},

(165)

\displaystyle\|\nabla L^{\nu}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon\nu^{-1}}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|\psi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}.

(166)

As a corollary
\|\partial_{y}L^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty}),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu}))}\leq 2C_{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

The fact that
L^{\nu}\in\mathcal{L}({H}^{s}(\Sigma_{\infty}),H^{s+1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})),\,s\geq 1/2,
follows by adapting the elliptic regularity results to unbounded domains (cf.
[
30
, proof of Theorem 4.18]
). The fact that
L^{\nu}\in\mathcal{L}(H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty}),H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{a}))
stems from its definition. So do the commutator relations
L^{\nu}\mathcal{L}_{w}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{w}L^{\nu}
,
L^{\nu}\mathcal{H}_{w}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}_{w}L^{\nu}
. Similarly, as
\mathcal{F}_{y}(\partial_{y}L^{\nu}\psi)(\xi)=i\xi\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\hat{\psi}(\xi)
, the property
\partial_{y}L^{\nu}\psi=L^{\nu}\partial_{y}\psi
follows.

Proof of the operator bounds.

Let
\psi\in H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
.
We start with preliminary expressions.
From the definition of
L^{\nu}
and the Plancherel identity it follows, with
\lambda_{\nu}(x,\xi)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\xi(x-\nu)}-\mathrm{e}^{-\xi(x-\nu)}}{\mathrm{e}^{-\xi\nu}-\mathrm{e}^{\xi\nu}}
,

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}L^{\nu}\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2+}_{\nu})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi^{2}|\hat{\psi}(\xi)|^{2}\|\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,\nu)}d\xi,

(167)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{x}L^{\nu}\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2+}_{\nu})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{\psi}(\xi)|^{2}\|\partial_{x}\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,\nu)}d\xi,

(168)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{J}\partial_{x}L^{\nu}\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2+}_{\nu})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|\xi|^{2})^{1/2}|\hat{\psi}(\xi)|^{2}\|\partial_{x}\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,\nu)}d\xi.

(169)

Proof of (
164
), (
165
).

From (
167
) and (
169
), as well as the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{w}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}L^{\nu}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\nu^{-1}}\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2+}_{\nu})}

\displaystyle=\int_{|\xi\nu|<\varepsilon}\xi^{2}|\hat{\psi}(\xi)|^{2}\|\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,\nu)}d\xi\leq\sup_{\xi:|\xi\nu|<\varepsilon}(|\xi|\|\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,\nu)})\|\psi\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})},

(170)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{J}\partial_{x}L^{\nu}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\nu^{-1}}\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2+}_{\nu})}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\xi:|\xi\nu|<\varepsilon}\|\partial_{x}\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,\nu)}\|\psi\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}.

(171)

We proceed by bounding
\lambda_{\nu}
in the case when
|\xi\nu|<\varepsilon
. We use the mean-value theorem for the upper bound:

\displaystyle|\mathrm{e}^{\xi(x-\nu)}-\mathrm{e}^{-\xi(x-\nu)}|\leq\xi|x-\nu|\left(\sup_{t\in[-\xi\nu,0]}\mathrm{e}^{t}+\sup_{t\in[0,\xi\nu]}\mathrm{e}^{t}\right)\lesssim|\xi\nu|\mathrm{e}^{|\xi\nu|}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon}|\xi\nu|,

and the inequality
|\mathrm{e}^{x}-1-x|\leq\frac{e^{\varepsilon}|x|^{2}}{2}
,
\forall x\in[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon],
(the Lagrange form of the remainder), which yields

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{e}^{\xi\nu}-\mathrm{e}^{-\xi\nu}\right|\geq 2|\xi\nu|-\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon}|\xi\nu|^{2}\geq|\xi\nu|(2-\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon}\varepsilon)\geq|\xi\nu|,\text{ since $\varepsilon\leq 1/2$.}

(172)

This allows to bound

\displaystyle\|\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\|_{L^{\infty}(0,\nu)}\lesssim 1\implies\|\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,\nu)}\lesssim\nu\implies\sup_{\xi:|\xi\nu|<\varepsilon}|\xi|\|\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,\nu)}\lesssim\varepsilon\leq 1/2.

This, together with (
170
), proves (
164
). In a similar manner, for
|\xi\nu|\leq\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\lambda_{\nu}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\xi\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\xi(x-\nu)}+\mathrm{e}^{-\xi(x-\nu)}}{\mathrm{e}^{-\xi\nu}-\mathrm{e}^{\xi\nu}},\text{ therefore, with \eqref{eq:b}, }

\displaystyle\|\partial_{x}\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,\nu)}

\displaystyle\leq|\xi\nu|^{-2}\int_{0}^{\nu}\xi^{2}|\mathrm{e}^{\xi(x-\nu)}+\mathrm{e}^{-\xi(x-\nu)}|^{2}dx\leq 4\nu^{-2}\int_{0}^{\nu}\mathrm{e}^{2|\xi\nu|}dx\leq 4\nu^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2\varepsilon}.

Plugging in this estimate into (
171
) yields (
165
).

Proof of (
166
).
Using (
167
) and (
168
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|\nabla L^{\nu}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon\nu^{-1}}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{\nu})}^{2}\leq\left(\sup_{\xi:\,|\xi\nu|>\varepsilon}\||\xi|^{-1/2}\partial_{x}\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,\nu)}+\sup_{\xi:\,|\xi\nu|>\varepsilon}\||\xi|^{1/2}\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,\nu)}\right)\|\psi\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\infty})}.

(173)

To bound
\lambda_{\nu}
, we make use of the following bound, valid for
|\xi\nu|>\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon\leq 1/2
,

\displaystyle|\mathrm{e}^{\xi\nu}-\mathrm{e}^{-\xi\nu}|=\mathrm{e}^{|\xi\nu|}|1-\mathrm{e}^{-2|\xi\nu|}|\geq\mathrm{e}^{|\xi\nu|}(1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\varepsilon})\geq\mathrm{e}^{|\xi\nu|}c_{\varepsilon}.

(174)

With this bound, for all
|\xi\nu|>\varepsilon
, it holds that, where the hidden constant depends on
\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle\|\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,\nu)}\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-2|\xi\nu|}\int_{0}^{\nu}(\mathrm{e}^{\xi(x-\nu)}-\mathrm{e}^{-\xi(x-\nu)})^{2}dx

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-2|\xi\nu|}\int_{0}^{\nu}\mathrm{e}^{2|\xi||x-\nu|}dx\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-2|\xi\nu|}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{2|\xi|\nu}-1}{|\xi|}\lesssim|\xi|^{-1}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\|\partial_{x}\lambda_{\nu}(.,\xi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,\nu)}\lesssim\xi^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2|\xi\nu|}\int_{0}^{\nu}(\mathrm{e}^{\xi(x-\nu)}+\mathrm{e}^{-\xi(x-\nu)})^{2}dx\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-2|\xi\nu|}\int_{0}^{\nu}\mathrm{e}^{2|\xi||x-\nu|}dx\lesssim|\xi|.

Plugging in the above two bounds into (
173
) yields the desired estimate (
166
).

The final bound on
\partial_{y}L^{\nu}
follows from the following identity, valid for all
w>0
,

\displaystyle\partial_{y}L^{\nu}=\partial_{y}L^{\nu}(\mathcal{L}_{w}+\mathcal{H}_{w}),

and the uniform bounds (
166
) and (
164
).
∎

Remark F.2
.

One sees easily in the derivation of the above estimates that in the low-frequency case,
|\xi\nu|<\varepsilon
, it seems impossible to control
\|\partial_{x}L^{\nu}\mathcal{L}_{\nu^{-1}\varepsilon}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2,+}_{(0,\nu)})}
uniformly in
\nu
, contrary to the derivative tangent to the interface.

G
Proof of Proposition
4.18

Let us remark that while in view of the regularity result Theorem
3.7
, the result may seem trivial, in its proof it is crucial that Neumann boundary conditions at
\Sigma
vanish. In particular, it can be checked on a toy 1D example that the corresponding statement with homogeneous Dirichlet BCs on
\Sigma
does not hold true.

We start by proving the corresponding result for
\delta=0
, and next arguing on how to extend it for
\delta>0
.

Proof for
\delta=0
.
The proof is similar to the analogous result of
[
4
]
. Since
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
, by the same arguments that led to Theorem
4.2
, the estimate (
43
), Proposition
4.8
and the bound (
163
), we have

\displaystyle\|v^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\|\partial_{y}v^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\|x\nabla v^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\nu^{1/2}\|\nabla v^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+\nu\|\partial_{y}\nabla v^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})},

(175)

for all
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
(with a fixed
\nu_{0}>0
). It remains to prove that
\|\partial_{x}v^{\nu}\|_{\Omega_{p}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\Omega_{p}}
. Assume that

\displaystyle\|x\partial_{y}\nabla v^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}.

(176)

We rewrite
\operatorname{div}((x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla v^{\nu})=f
as

\displaystyle\partial_{x}(\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot(x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla v^{\nu})=-x\partial_{y}(\mathbf{e}_{y}\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla v^{\nu})-i\nu\partial_{y}(r\mathbf{e}_{y}\cdot\mathbb{A}\nabla v^{\nu})+f,

and, by using (
175
) and (
176
) to bound the right-hand side of the above,
q^{\nu}:=\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot(x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla v^{\nu}

satisfies
\|\partial_{x}q^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}.

Moreover, by (
175
) and (
176
),
\|\partial_{y}q^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
. With (
175
) and the above, we conclude that

\|q^{\nu}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}.

From the definition of
q^{\nu}
it follows that
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}q^{\nu}=\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}v^{\nu}=0
, and thus we can use Hardy’s inequality on p. 313 of
[
5
]
:

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{q^{\nu}}{x}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|q^{\nu}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})},\text{ which implies }

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}\left(1+\frac{\nu^{2}r^{2}(\mathbf{x})}{x^{2}}\right)|\mathbb{A}\nabla v^{\nu}(\mathbf{x})|^{2}d\mathbf{x}\lesssim\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})},

hence
\|v^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
(the desired statement for
\delta=0
). Remark the importance of
\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}v^{\nu}=0
.

It remains to show (
176
). Test the problem stated in Proposition
4.18
with the Nirenberg’s quotient
x\delta_{h}^{y}\delta_{-h}^{y}v^{\nu}
,
\delta_{h}^{y}
defined like in Proposition
E.6
, and next integrate by parts, at the continuous and at the discrete level, to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}\delta^{y}_{-h}\left((x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\nabla v^{\nu}\right)\overline{\nabla(x\delta^{y}_{-h}v^{\nu})}=\int_{\Omega}fx\overline{\delta_{h}^{y}\delta_{-h}^{y}v^{\nu}}.

The above rewrites

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}(x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\delta^{y}_{-h}\nabla v^{\nu}\,\overline{x\delta^{y}_{-h}\nabla v^{\nu}}+\int_{\Omega_{p}}(x+i\nu r)\mathbb{A}\delta^{y}_{-h}\nabla v^{\nu}\mathbf{e}_{x}\overline{\delta_{-h}^{y}v^{\nu}}

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega_{p}}(x\delta_{-h}^{y}\mathbb{A}+i\nu\delta_{-h}^{y}(r\,\mathbb{A}))\nabla v^{\nu}\left(x\overline{\nabla\delta_{-h}^{y}v^{\nu}}+\overline{\mathbf{e}_{x}\delta_{-h}^{y}v^{\nu}}\right)=\int_{\Omega}fx\overline{\delta_{h}^{y}\delta_{-h}^{y}v^{\nu}}.

Taking the real part of the above, and using that
\mathbb{A}
is Hermitian, we observe that the first term in the above is sign-definite, while the remaining terms can be bounded using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (all the norms below are
\|.\|=\|.\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Omega_{p}}|x\delta_{-h}^{y}\nabla v^{\nu}|^{2}&\lesssim(\|x\delta_{-h}^{y}\nabla v^{\nu}\|+\nu\|\delta_{-h}^{y}\nabla v^{\nu}\|)\|\delta_{-h}^{y}v^{\nu}\|\\
&+\left(\|x\nabla v^{\nu}\|+\nu\|\nabla v^{\nu}\|\right)(\|x\delta_{-h}^{y}\nabla v^{\nu}\|+\|\delta_{-h}^{y}v^{\nu}\|)+\|f\|\|x\delta_{-h}^{y}\delta_{h}^{y}v^{\nu}\|.\end{split}

(177)

Together with
[
30
, Lemma 4.13]
(the latter links
\delta_{-h}^{y}
and
\partial_{y}
) and (
175
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\|x\partial_{y}\nabla v^{\nu}\|^{2}\lesssim\|f\|\|x\partial_{y}\nabla v^{\nu}\|+\|f\|^{2}.

The desired bound (
176
) follows with the Young inequality.

Proof for
\delta>0
.
We proceed by interpolation. In particular, by testing the problem stated in Proposition
4.18
with
v^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
, and using Lemma
C.2
, we remark that for any
\delta<1
, with a hidden constant independent of
\nu
,
|v^{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{1}(\Omega_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})},

and next proceed like in Proposition
C.4
.

H
Proof of Proposition
4.8

Let
\nu>0
, and let us define
p^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)
as a unique solution to the following problem:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}(x\mathbb{A}p^{\nu})=\partial_{y}u^{\nu},\text{ in }\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n},\\
&\gamma_{n}^{\Sigma}p^{\nu}=0,\quad\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}\cup\Gamma_{n}}p^{\nu}=0,\\
&\text{ periodic BCs on }\Gamma^{\pm}_{p}\cup\Gamma^{\pm}_{n}.\end{split}

(178)

The above problem is well-posed due to
\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
(cf. Lemma
2.4
), Theorem
3.1
(existence and uniqueness of
p^{\nu}\in\mathcal{V}_{reg}(\Omega_{p})
), which also affirms that
p^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)
. The solution to (
178
) satisfies the following variational formulation:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n}}\overline{x\mathbb{A}\nabla p^{\nu}}\cdot\nabla v=-\int_{\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n}}\overline{\partial_{y}u^{\nu}}v,\text{ for all }v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma).

(179)

Next, again, due to
\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
(by elliptic regularity, cf. Lemma
2.4
), and
\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}\cup\Gamma_{n}}\partial_{y}u^{\nu}=0
, we can take in (
179
)
v=\partial_{y}u^{\nu}
and use the fact that
\mathbb{A}
is a hermitian matrix; this yields

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n}}\overline{\nabla p^{\nu}}\cdot x\mathbb{A}\nabla\partial_{y}u^{\nu}=-\int_{\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n}}|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}|^{2}.

(180)

Next, by the elliptic regularity estimate of Theorem
3.7
, and using the fact that
u^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Omega)
(Lemma
2.4
), it holds that
p^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)
. Remark that
\gamma_{0}^{\Gamma_{p}\cup\Gamma_{n}}\partial_{y}p^{\nu}=0
.
This enables us to test the problem with absorption (
P

ν

) with
\partial_{y}p^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)
. Integration by parts yields (recall (
5
) for the notation
[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}.]
)

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n}}(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla u^{\nu}\cdot\overline{\nabla\partial_{y}p^{\nu}}-\int_{\Sigma}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\partial_{y}\overline{p^{\nu}}]=\int_{\Omega}f\,\overline{\partial_{y}p^{\nu}}.

Since
u^{\nu},p^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)
, in the above expression the integral over the interface
\Sigma
is a Lebesgue integral. Moreover,
\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,\lambda}\partial_{y}p^{\nu}=\partial_{y}\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma,\lambda}p^{\nu}
,
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
, the result being true by density of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega_{\lambda}})
functions in
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Omega_{\lambda})
(resp. their traces on
\Sigma
in
\mathcal{H}^{3/2}(\Sigma)
).
Integrating by parts on
\Sigma
(justified in particular by the bound (
45
) and can be proven using the usual density argument), and, next, on
\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n}
we obtain:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n}}(x\mathbb{A}+i\nu\mathbb{T})\nabla\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\cdot\overline{\nabla p^{\nu}}+\langle\partial_{y}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu},[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\overline{p^{\nu}}]\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}=\int_{\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n}}f\,\overline{\partial_{y}p^{\nu}}.

(181)

Replacing the first term in the above by the right-hand side of (
180
) yields the identity

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n}}|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}|^{2}=-i\nu\int_{\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n}}\mathbb{T}\partial_{y}\nabla u^{\nu}\,\overline{\nabla p^{\nu}}-\langle\partial_{y}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu},[\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\overline{p^{\nu}}]\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma),\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}-\int_{\Omega_{p}\cup\Omega_{n}}f\overline{\partial_{y}p^{\nu}}.

With the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the continuity of the trace operator on
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\lambda})
,
\lambda\in\{n,p\}
,

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left(\nu\|\partial_{y}\nabla u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{y}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right)\|p^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)}.

To bound the right-hand side of the above, we use Theorem
3.7
(
\|p^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega\setminus\Sigma)}\lesssim\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
), Proposition
4.13
in the form (
163
) (
\nu\|\partial_{y}\nabla u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
) and Proposition
4.7
on the control of the conormal trace, namely,
\|\partial_{y}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}u^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1/2}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\sqrt{\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}}
. Altogether, this yields

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|^{2}\leq C(\|f\|+\sqrt{\|f\|\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|})\|\partial_{y}u^{\nu}\|,\quad C>0,

uniformly in
\nu>0
.
Applying the Young inequality to the above yields the desired bound.

I
Proofs of the results for general domains

I.1
Proof of Proposition
6.5

We start with the following auxiliary problem.
Given
f\in L^{2}(D_{p})
,
g\in H^{1/2}(I)
, find
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(D_{p})
, s.t.

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla u)=f\text{ in }D_{p},

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}^{I}u=g,\quad\gamma_{0}^{I_{p}}u=0.

The following is a counterpart of Theorem
3.8
, combined with Theorem
3.7
.

Theorem I.1
.

If
g=0
, the above problem admits a unique solution
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(D_{p})
. This solution also satisfies the following. For
f\in L^{2}(D_{p})
,
u\in H^{1}(D_{p})
and
\|u\|_{H^{1}(D_{p})}+\|\alpha u\|_{H^{2}(D_{p})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(D_{p})}
.

Proof.

See the proof of Theorem
3.8
. Remark that we make use of the fact that the corresponding sesqulinear form is strictly elliptic, in other words, in virtue of the Poincarè inequality of Proposition
B.1
(which is true in particular due to the homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition at
I_{p}\neq\emptyset
), the above problem indeed admits a unique solution in
\mathcal{V}_{reg}(D_{p})
. The regularity results of
[
4
]
, cf. Theorem
3.7
, hold true in this case as well, cf. the respective proofs in
Appendix
D

and the change of coordinates described after Lemma
I.4
.
∎

At this point we will not need the corresponding result for regularity of
u
when
f\in H^{1}(D_{p})
, since at the point where it will be needed, we will work with a coordinate-transformed problem, mapped on the rectangular domain
\Omega
.

With this result, we obtain

Theorem I.2
.

The above problem admits a unique solution
u\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(D_{p})
, which writes

\displaystyle u=u_{sing}+u_{reg},\qquad u_{sing}=u_{h}\log|\alpha|,

where
u_{h}\in H^{1}(D_{p})
is s.t.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{div}(\mathbb{H}\nabla u_{h})=0\text{ in }D_{p},\\
&\gamma_{0}^{I}u_{h}=h_{I}^{-1}g,\qquad\gamma_{0}^{I_{p}}u_{h}=0,\end{split}

(182)

and
u_{reg}\in\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(D_{p})\bigcap\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}H^{1-\varepsilon}(D_{p})
. In the above,
\gamma_{n}^{I}u=\gamma_{n}^{I}u_{sing}
.

Proof.

The uniqueness follows from Theorem
I.1
. The existence follows verbatim by the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
3.9
. Indeed, remark that
u_{h}
that solves (
182
) satisfies

\displaystyle\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla(u_{h}\log|\alpha|)=u_{h}\mathbb{H}\nabla\alpha+\alpha\log|\alpha|\mathbb{H}\nabla u_{h}.

With the above we can prove that, in the sense of equality in
H^{-1/2}(I)
,
\gamma_{n}^{I}u_{sing}=\gamma_{0}^{I}u_{h}\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbb{H}\mathbf{n}=h_{I}\gamma_{0}^{I}u_{h}=g
, see (
•
‣
6.1
). This can be justified rigorously like in Theorem
3.9
.
∎

Proposition
6.5
follows immediately from the above.

I.1.1
The Green’s formula

To motivate Definition
6.6
, we need an appropriate Green’s formula.
Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla.);D_{p}):=\{v\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(D_{p}):\,\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla v)\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p}),\,\gamma_{n}^{I}v\in H^{1/2}(I)\}.

Then the following counterpart of Theorem
3.14
holds true.

Theorem I.3
.

For
u,v\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla.);D_{p})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{D_{p}}\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla u)\overline{v}-\int_{D_{p}}\overline{\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla v)}u=-\langle\gamma_{n}^{I}u,\overline{\gamma_{0}^{I}v}\rangle_{L^{2}(I)}+\overline{\langle\gamma_{n}^{I}v,\overline{\gamma_{0}^{I}u}\rangle}_{L^{2}(I)}.

The proof of this theorem is technical and relies on a well-chosen change of coordinates and the Green’s formula on rectangular domains of Theorem
3.14
. By a localization argument and the same argument as in Lemma
3.15
it is sufficient to prove the above result for
D_{p}
replaced by a vicinity of
I
. In particular, the following statement can be proven like Lemma
3.15
.

Lemma I.4
.

Assume that
v\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla.);D_{p})
admits two decompositions

\displaystyle v=v_{h,j}\log|\alpha|+v_{reg,j},\quad j=1,2,

where
v_{h,j}\in H^{1}(D_{p})
with
\gamma_{0}^{I_{p}}v_{h,j}=0
, and
v_{reg,j}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(D_{p})
,
0<\delta<1
,
j=1,2
. Then

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{I}v_{h,1}=\gamma_{0}^{I}v_{h,2},\qquad\gamma_{0}^{I}v_{reg,1}=\gamma_{0}^{I}v_{reg,2},

and, in particular,
\gamma_{0}^{I}v_{h,j}=h_{I}^{-1}\gamma_{n}^{I}v
,
\gamma_{0}^{I}v_{reg,j}=\gamma_{0}^{I}v
,
j=1,2
.

Let us now concentrate on establishing the necessary results in the vicinity of
I
. Since
I
is compact and the domain
D_{p}
is of
C^{3}
regularity, there exist open sets
\{\mathcal{U}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}
s.t.
I\subset\bigcup_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}
, and associated local
C^{3}
charts
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}:\,\overline{\Omega}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{U}_{k}}
, where
\Omega
is like in Section
2.1
, and

\mathcal{U}_{p,k}:=\mathcal{U}_{k}\cap D_{p}=\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\Omega_{p}),\quad\mathcal{U}_{k}\cap D_{n}=\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\Omega_{n}),\quad\mathcal{U}_{k}\cap I=\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\Sigma),

(this is a corollary of the definition
[
20
, p.94]
). Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\{\mathcal{U}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}\subset U_{I}
, where
\alpha
equals the signed distance. Next, we define the subordinate partition of unity
\{\chi_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, see
[
30
, Corollary 3.22]
, and functions

\displaystyle u_{k}:=\chi_{k}u,\quad v_{k}=\chi_{k}v.

By direct computation it follows that for
u,v
like in Theorem
I.3
,
u_{k},\,v_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla.);D_{p})
, and

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}u_{k}\subsetneq\mathcal{U}_{k},\quad\operatorname{supp}v_{k}\subsetneq\mathcal{U}_{k}.

(183)

We then have the following result.

Proposition I.5
.

Let
k,m\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
. Then for
\mathcal{U}_{p,k}
,
u_{k}
,
v_{m}
as above, it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}_{p,k}}\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla u_{k})\overline{v}_{m}-\int_{\mathcal{U}_{p,k}}\overline{\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla v_{m})}{u}_{k}=-\int_{I}\chi_{k}\gamma_{n}^{I}u\overline{\chi_{m}\gamma_{0}^{I}v}+\int_{I}\overline{\chi_{m}\gamma_{n}^{I}v}{\chi_{k}\gamma_{0}^{I}u}.

(184)

This result will be proven by a change of coordinates. Let us introduce several auxiliary results. Let us fix
k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
.
For
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
, it holds that
\mathbf{x}:=\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}})
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\Omega
. We define the Jacobian and its determinant:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{J}}=D\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k},\quad j=\operatorname{det}{\mathbb{J}},\quad\mathbb{J}_{\Sigma}=\left.\mathbb{J}\right|_{\Sigma},\quad j_{\Sigma}=\left.j\right|_{\Sigma}.

For any function
h:\,\mathcal{U}_{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we define its pullback
\widetilde{h}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}})=h({\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}))
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\Omega
. Moreover, we denote by
\widetilde{\operatorname{div}}
,
\widetilde{\operatorname{\nabla}}
etc. differential operators written in
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}
-coordinates.

Let
I_{k}:=\overline{\mathcal{U}_{k}}\cap I
. Denoting by
\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}=(1,0)
the unit normal to
\Sigma
, we recall that, see
[
3
, (2.1.62), (2.1.58)]
.

Lemma I.6
.

For
f\in L^{1}(I_{k})
, it holds that

\int_{I_{k}}fd\Gamma=\int_{\Sigma}\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{y})\rho_{\Sigma}(\tilde{y})d\tilde{y},\quad\rho_{\Sigma}=|\mathbb{J}^{-t}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}|j_{\Sigma}=|\left.\partial_{y}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}\right|_{\Sigma}|.

Next, let us see how
\widetilde{\alpha}
is transformed under
\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{k}
.

Lemma I.7
.

The coefficient
\widetilde{\alpha}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}})=\alpha(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}))
satisfies

\displaystyle(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto\widetilde{\alpha}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}})=\tilde{x}\gamma(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}),

where
\gamma\in C^{2}(\overline{\Omega})
and
\inf_{\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\Omega}\gamma(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}})>0
. Moreover,
\left.\gamma\right|_{\Sigma}=|\mathbb{J}^{-t}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}|^{-1}
.

Proof.

As
\mathcal{U}_{k}\subset U_{I}
, we use the expression (
•
‣
6.1
) of
\alpha
(below
\operatorname{dist}
is the signed distance to
I
,
\operatorname{dist}>0
in
\Omega_{p}\cap\mathcal{U}_{I}
)

\displaystyle\alpha(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{dist}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}),I)=\operatorname{dist}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}),\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\Sigma)),\text{ and it holds that }

\displaystyle|\operatorname{dist}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}),\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\Sigma))|=\inf_{\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma}|\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}})-\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{\Sigma})|\geq c\inf_{\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma}|\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{\Sigma}|=c\tilde{x},

\displaystyle|\operatorname{dist}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}),\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\Sigma))|=\inf_{\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma}|\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}})-\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{\Sigma})|\leq C\inf_{\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma}|\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{\Sigma}|=C\tilde{x},

for some constants
C,c>0
, since
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}
is bi-Lipschitz. From the above considerations, regularity of
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}
and of the distance function it follows that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto\alpha(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}))\in C^{2}(\overline{\Omega}),\qquad\text{ and for }\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}=(\tilde{x},\tilde{y}),\quad\alpha(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}))=\tilde{x}\gamma(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}),

where
\gamma(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}})\geq c
and
\gamma\in C^{2}(\overline{\Omega})
. One verifies that
\left.\gamma\right|_{\Sigma}=\left.\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{\alpha}\right|_{\Sigma}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma},

and with the change of coordinates relations, it holds that

\displaystyle\left.\gamma\right|_{\Sigma}=\mathbb{J}^{t}_{\Sigma}\left.(\nabla\alpha\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k})\right|_{\Sigma}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}=\mathbb{J}^{t}_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{n}_{I}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k})\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}=|\mathbb{J}^{-t}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}|^{-1},

where the identity before the last one follows from (
98
) and the last identity from
[
3
, (2.1.94)]
(namely,
\mathbf{n}_{I}\circ\mathbf{\psi}_{k}=|\mathbb{J}^{-t}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}|^{-1}\mathbb{J}^{-t}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}
).
∎

Equipped with these results, we can prove Proposition
I.5
.

Proof of Proposition
I.5
.

Rewriting the desired identity in
\Omega_{p}
.

Following the change of coordinates as described in
[
3
, Section 2.1.3]
, we have that

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla u_{k})(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{j}\widetilde{\operatorname{div}}(j\widetilde{\alpha}\mathbb{J}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}\mathbb{J}^{-t}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u}_{k})(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}^{-1}(\mathbf{x})).

Let us introduce, using the notation of Lemma
I.7
, the positive definite Hermitian matrix, cf. the above:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}:=\gamma j\mathbb{J}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}\mathbb{J}^{-t}\in C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}),

(185)

remark the regularity of
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}
compared to Assumption
2.1
(which justifies Assumption
6.2
and our requirements on the regularity of
D_{p},\,D_{n},I
).

Parametrizing the integral in the left-hand side, we see that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}_{p,k}}\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla u_{k})\overline{{v}}_{m}=\int_{\Omega_{p}}\widetilde{\operatorname{div}}(\tilde{x}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u}_{k})\overline{\widetilde{v}_{m}},

(186)

so that the left-hand side of (
184
) equals to:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}:=\int_{\Omega_{p}}\widetilde{\operatorname{div}}(\tilde{x}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{u}_{k})\overline{\widetilde{v}_{m}}-\int_{\Omega_{p}}\overline{\widetilde{\operatorname{div}}(\tilde{x}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{v}_{m})}{\widetilde{u}_{k}}.

(187)

We will apply to the above the integration by parts Theorem
3.14
, more precisely, its Corollary
3.16
. Remark that while the matrix
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}
in the above does not satisfy periodicity constraints, the functions
\widetilde{u}_{k}
,
\widetilde{v}_{m}
satisfy

\operatorname{supp}\widetilde{u}_{k}\subsetneq\Omega,\quad\operatorname{supp}\widetilde{v}_{m}\subsetneq\Omega,

due to (
183
). This allows to extend the statement of Theorem
3.14
and of Corollary
3.16
to this case in a trivial manner.

Evaluating
\mathcal{I}
.
To evaluate
\mathcal{I}
, by Corollary
3.16
, it suffices to find a decomposition of
\widetilde{u}_{k}
,
\widetilde{v}_{m}
, s.t.

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{k}=\widetilde{u}_{k,s}\log|\tilde{x}|+\widetilde{u}_{k,r},\quad\widetilde{v}_{m}=\widetilde{v}_{m,s}\log|\tilde{x}|+\widetilde{v}_{m,r},

(188)

where
\widetilde{u}_{k,s}
,
\widetilde{v}_{m,s}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
and
\widetilde{u}_{k,r}
and
\widetilde{v}_{m,r}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\delta}(\Omega_{p})
,
0<\delta<1
.
This decomposition will be constructed with the help of corresponding decomposition for the original functions
u_{k},\,v_{m}
. Using the decomposition defined in Theorem
I.2
, we write

\displaystyle u_{k}=\chi_{k}u_{h}\log|\alpha|+\chi_{k}u_{reg},\quad v_{m}=\chi_{m}v_{h}\log|\alpha|+\chi_{m}v_{reg},

so that

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{k}=\widetilde{\chi}_{k}\widetilde{u}_{h}\log|\widetilde{\alpha}|+\widetilde{\chi}_{k}\widetilde{u}_{reg},\quad\widetilde{v}_{m}=\widetilde{\chi}_{m}\widetilde{v}_{h}\log|\widetilde{\alpha}|+\widetilde{\chi}_{m}\widetilde{v}_{reg},

and we rewrite the above to match with (
188
) and using Lemma
I.7
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{u}_{k}=\widetilde{u}_{k,s}\log|\tilde{x}|+\widetilde{u}_{k,r},\qquad\widetilde{u}_{k,s}=\widetilde{\chi}_{k}\widetilde{u}_{h},\quad\widetilde{u}_{k,r}=\widetilde{\chi}_{k}(\widetilde{u}_{reg}+\widetilde{u}_{h}\log|\gamma|),\end{split}

(189)

and similarly for
\widetilde{v}_{m}
. The regularity of the above functions allows to apply Corollary
3.16
to
(
187
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}=-\int_{\Sigma}\widetilde{a}_{11}\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{u}_{k,s}\,\overline{\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{v}_{m,r}}+\int_{\Sigma}\widetilde{a}_{11}\overline{\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{v}_{m,s}}\,{\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{u}_{k,r}}.

(190)

It remains to apply the change of coordinates of Lemma
B.5
. We rewrite, recalling the definition (
185
) of
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}
,
\left.\gamma\right|_{\Sigma}=|\mathbb{J}^{-t}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}|^{-1}
, cf. Lemma
I.7
, and
\rho_{\Sigma}=|\mathbb{J}^{-t}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}|j_{\Sigma}
, cf. Lemma
I.6
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{11}=\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}=|\mathbb{J}_{\Sigma}^{-t}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}|^{-1}j_{\Sigma}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}\cdot\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{\Sigma}\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}\mathbb{J}^{-t}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}=\rho_{\Sigma}|\mathbb{J}_{\Sigma}^{-t}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}|^{-2}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}\cdot\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{\Sigma}\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}\mathbb{J}^{-t}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}.

By
[
3
, (2.1.94)]
,
\mathbf{n}_{I}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}=\mathbb{J}^{-t}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}|\mathbb{J}^{-t}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}|^{-1}
, thus

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{11}=\rho_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{n}_{I}\cdot\mathbb{H}\mathbf{n}_{I})\circ{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{k}=\rho_{\Sigma}\widetilde{h_{I}}.

Rewriting (
190
) with Lemma
B.5
and recalling the definition (
189
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle=-\int_{I}h_{I}\chi_{k}u_{h}\,\overline{\chi_{m}(v_{reg}+v_{h}\log|\gamma\circ{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{k}^{-1}|)}+\int_{I}h_{I}\overline{\chi_{m}v_{h}}\,{\chi_{k}(u_{reg}+u_{h}\log|\gamma\circ{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{k}^{-1}|)}

\displaystyle=-\int_{I}h_{I}\chi_{k}u_{h}\,{\overline{\chi_{m}}v_{reg}}+\int_{I}h_{I}\overline{\chi_{m}v_{h}}\chi_{k}u_{reg}=-\int_{I}\chi_{k}\gamma_{n}^{I}u\overline{\chi_{m}\gamma_{0}^{I}v}+\int_{I}\overline{\chi_{m}\gamma_{n}^{I}v}{\chi_{k}\gamma_{0}^{I}u},

where in the last identity we used definitions of
u_{h},\,v_{h}
in Definition
6.6
, and the fact that
\operatorname{supp}\chi_{k}\subsetneq\mathcal{U}_{k}
.
∎

Proposition
I.5
enables us to prove Theorem
I.3
.

Proof of Theorem
I.3
.

Let
\chi
be a regular function
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2};[0,\,1])
, equal to
1
in the vicinity of
I
that is included into
\bigcup\limits_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}
, and vanishing outside of
\bigcup\limits_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}
. We rewrite

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}:=\int_{D_{p}}\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla u)\overline{v}-\int_{D_{p}}\overline{\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla v)}{u}=\int_{D_{p}}\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla(\chi u))\overline{\chi v}-\int_{D_{p}}\overline{\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla(\chi v))}{\chi u},

where the desired identity follows by a classical Green’s formula and the fact that
(1-\chi)u
, resp.
(1-\chi)v
vanishes in the vicinity of
I
. It remains to decompose
\chi u
,
\chi v
using the partition of unity
\{\chi_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}
, with
\operatorname{supp}\chi_{k}\subset\mathcal{U}_{k}
, and use the result of Proposition
I.5
(evidently valid with
u,v
replaced by
\chi u
,
\chi v
).
∎

I.1.2
The key stability bound

The counterpart of Theorem
4.2
reads.

Theorem I.8

(The first stability estimate)
.

There exists
C>0
, s.t. for all
\nu>0
sufficiently small, the solution to (
96
) satisfies the following stability bound:
\|v^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{sing}(D)}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(D)}.

The proof of this result relies on the following proposition.

Proposition I.9
.

Given
u^{\nu}
as in (
96
), let the co-normal derivative at the interface
\Sigma
be denoted by

g^{\nu}:=\gamma_{n,\nu}^{I}v^{\nu}=\left.(\alpha\mathbb{H}+i\nu\mathbb{N})\nabla v^{\nu}\right|_{I}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{I}.

There exists
C>0
, s.t. for all
\nu>0
sufficiently small, it satisfies the following bound:

\displaystyle\|g^{\nu}\|_{{H}^{-1/2}(I)}\leq C\left(\nu^{1/2}\|f\|+\sqrt{\|f\|\|v^{\nu}\|}\right).

(191)

Proof.

The proof mimics the proof of Proposition
4.3
, which is based on integration by parts, estimates of Lemma
2.4
(obtained by integration by parts), and an appropriate lifting lemma, which follows from Lemma
4.5
by a standard localization/change of variables argument (cf.
[
38
, proof of Lemma 2.7.3]
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
I.8
.

The proof mimics the proof of Theorem
4.2
. The latter relies on two ingredients:

•

Proposition
4.3
, see its counterpart Proposition
I.9
;

•

integration by parts combined with Theorem
3.7
.

The second result transfers almost verbatim by using the general results of
[
4
]
.
∎

I.1.3
An improved regularity of the conormal derivative

The next result is a counterpart of Theorem
4.6
.

Theorem I.10
.

Given
v^{\nu}
as in (
96
), let the co-normal derivative at the interface
I
be denoted by
g^{\nu}:=\gamma_{n,\nu}^{I}v^{\nu}=\left.(\alpha\mathbb{H}+i\nu\mathbb{N})\nabla v^{\nu}\right|_{I}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{I}
. Then
g^{\nu}\in{H}^{1/2}(I)
, and there exist
C,\nu_{0}>0
, s.t.

\displaystyle\|g^{\nu}\|_{{H}^{1/2}(I)}\leq C\|f\|,\quad\text{ for all }0<\nu<\nu_{0}.

(192)

Proof.

This result is proven by recalling the following equivalent expression to the norm in
H^{1/2}(I)
, cf.
[
30
, (3.29)]
, see also the notation after Proposition
I.5
:

\displaystyle\|g^{\nu}\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}(I)}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\|(\chi_{k}g^{\nu})\circ{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{k}\|_{\widetilde{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}^{2},

(193)

where we also recall that
(\chi_{k}g^{\nu})\circ{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{k}
has a support strictly included into
\Sigma
for all
k=1,\ldots,N
and define, for
q\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma)
its extension
q_{0}
by
0
to
\mathbb{R}
together with the associated norm

\|q\|_{\widetilde{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}:=\|q_{0}\|_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}.

To prove Theorem
I.10
we will rely on localization techniques. Let us fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
. It is straightforward to see that
v^{\nu}_{k}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U}_{k})
satisfies the following problem, cf. (
96
):

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}((\alpha\mathbb{H}+i\nu\mathbb{N}\nabla)v^{\nu}_{k})=2(\alpha\mathbb{H}+i\nu\mathbb{N})\nabla v^{\nu}\cdot\nabla\chi_{k}+v^{\nu}\operatorname{div}((\alpha\mathbb{H}+i\nu\mathbb{N})\nabla\chi_{k})+\chi_{k}f=:f^{\nu}_{k}\text{ in }\mathcal{U}_{k},

\displaystyle v^{\nu}_{k}=0\text{ in a vicinity of }\partial\mathcal{U}_{k}.

By Theorem
I.8
and Lemma
6.3
,
\|f^{\nu}_{k}\|_{L^{2}(D)}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(D)}.

Next, we transform the above problem to
\Omega
. In particular, following the proof and the notation of Proposition
I.5
, we rewrite the above as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\operatorname{div}}((\tilde{x}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}+i\nu\widetilde{\mathbb{T}})\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{v}^{\nu}_{k})=j\widetilde{f}^{\nu}_{k}\text{ in }\Omega,

\displaystyle\widetilde{v}^{\nu}_{k}=0\text{ in a vicinity of }\partial\Omega.

with
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}=j\mathbb{J}^{-1}\mathbb{N}\mathbb{J}^{-t}
. We have thus obtained the problem (
P

ν

), modulo the boundary conditions and the periodicity constraints on the tensors
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
. Using the fact that
\widetilde{v}_{k}^{\nu}=0
in a vicinity of
\Omega
, in particular, there exists
\delta>0
, s.t.
\widetilde{v}^{\nu}_{k}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=0
for
|\tilde{y}|<\ell-\delta
, we fix
\delta
and recall the definition (
4.2.1
) of a cutoff function
\chi_{\ell-3\delta/2,\delta}
. Then
\widetilde{v}^{\nu}_{k}
satisfies as well the problem where

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{\operatorname{div}}((\tilde{x}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta}+i\nu\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\delta})\widetilde{\nabla}\widetilde{v}^{\nu}_{k})=j\widetilde{f}^{\nu}_{k}\text{ in }\Omega,\\
&\widetilde{v}^{\nu}_{k}=0\text{ in a vicinity of }\partial\Omega,\end{split}

(194)

where the new matrices satisfy now periodicity constraints, and remain Hermitian and positive-definite:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta}=\chi_{\ell-3\delta/2,\delta}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}+(1-\chi_{\ell-3\delta/2,\delta})\mathbb{I},\quad\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\delta}=\chi_{\ell-3\delta/2,\delta}\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}+(1-\chi_{\ell-3\delta/2,\delta})\mathbb{I}.

Remark that evidently,
\widetilde{v}_{k}^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
. From explicit expressions, cf. (
185
), it follows that
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\delta}
satisfy as well regularity constraints in Assumption
2.1
. By Theorem
4.6
, we conclude that, see also Remark
4.1
,

\displaystyle\|\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma,\delta}\widetilde{v}_{k}^{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}\leq C\|j\widetilde{f}^{\nu}_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

(195)

where
\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma,\delta}\widetilde{v}_{k}^{\nu}=\gamma_{0}^{\Sigma}(\widetilde{x}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta}+i\nu\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\delta})\widetilde{\nabla}\tilde{v}_{k}^{\nu}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma}
.

Because
\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma,\delta}\widetilde{v}_{k}^{\nu}=\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{v}_{k}^{\nu}
and has a support strictly included into
\Sigma
, the bound (
195
) implies that

\displaystyle\|\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{v}_{k}^{\nu}\|_{\widetilde{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}\leq C^{\prime}\|j\widetilde{f}^{\nu}_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

(196)

for some
C^{\prime}>0
(see
[
21
, Theorem 4.2.1]
for the equivalence of different norms on
H^{1/2}(\Sigma)
).
Finally, to use the above bound in (
193
), it remains to establish a connection between
\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{v}_{k}^{\nu}
and
\chi_{k}g^{\nu}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}
:

\displaystyle\rho^{-1}_{\Sigma}\gamma_{n,\nu}^{\Sigma}\widetilde{v}_{k}^{\nu}=\chi_{k}g^{\nu}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k},

with
\rho_{\Sigma}>c>0
is defined Lemma
I.6
. The above result can be obtained either by direct computations, or recalling that the conormal derivatives can be defined variationally, cf. the expression after (
8
), and next using (
186
), an analogous result for
\int_{\Omega_{p}}\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v
, and Lemma
I.6
.

With (
196
), and by using the fact that
\rho_{\Sigma}
is regular, we conclude that

\|\chi_{k}g^{\nu}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}\|_{\widetilde{H}^{1/2}(\Sigma)}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

which, when inserted into (
193
), implies the desired bound (
192
).
∎

I.1.4
An important property of the regular part

The following result is a counterpart of Theorem
4.15
.

Theorem I.11
.

Let
(v^{\nu})_{\nu>0}\subset{H}^{1}_{0}(D)
be a sequence of solutions to (
96
). Then there exists a subsequence
(v^{\nu_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
which converges weakly in
L^{2}(D)
to a limit
{v}^{*}\in\mathcal{V}_{sing}(\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla.);D)
. This limit necessarily satisfies

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(\alpha\mathbb{H}\nabla{v}^{*})=f\text{ in }\Omega,

\displaystyle[\gamma_{0}^{I}{v}^{*}]=-i\pi h_{I}^{-1}\gamma_{n}^{I}{v}^{*}.

Proof.

The existence and convergence results for the subsequence follow by the same argument as in Theorem
4.15
.
To prove the desired result about the decomposition, we rely on the localization and change of variable techniques, cf. the proof of Proposition
I.5
. By Lemma
I.4
, we see that it is sufficient to prove the result about the jump of
v
with
v^{*}
replaced by
\chi v^{*}
, where
\chi
is a cutoff equal to
1
in the sufficiently small vicinity of the interface
I
and vanishing otherwise.
In particular, with the notation of the proof of Proposition
I.5
, Proposition
4.17
shows that
\widetilde{v}_{k}^{\nu}:=(v^{\nu}\chi_{k})\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}
writes

\displaystyle\widetilde{v}_{k}^{\nu}=\widetilde{v}_{k,h}^{\nu}\log(\widetilde{x}+i\nu\widetilde{r})+\widetilde{v}_{k,cont}^{\nu}\text{ in }\Omega,

where
\widetilde{r}=\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{11}/\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}_{11}
, with
\widetilde{v}_{k,cont}^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega)
,
0<\varepsilon<1
,
\widetilde{v}_{k,h}^{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
. Passing to the limit like in the proof of Theorem
4.15
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\widetilde{v}_{k}^{*}=\widetilde{v}_{k,h}^{*}\log(|\widetilde{x}|+i\pi\mathbb{1}_{\widetilde{x}<0})+\widetilde{v}_{k,cont}^{*}\text{ in }\Omega,

with
\widetilde{v}_{k,cont}^{*}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega)
,
0<\varepsilon<1
,
\widetilde{v}_{k,h}^{*}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
. With Lemma
I.7
the above rewrites

\displaystyle\widetilde{v}_{k}^{*}=\widetilde{v}_{k,h}^{*}\log(|\widetilde{\alpha}|-\log|\gamma|+i\pi\mathbb{1}_{\widetilde{x}<0})+\widetilde{v}_{k,cont}^{*}\text{ in }\Omega,

and coming back to the original coordinates we rewrite

\displaystyle v_{k}^{*}=v^{*}_{k,h}\log(|\alpha|+i\pi\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{U}_{k}\cap D_{n}})+v_{k,cont}^{*}-{v}^{*}_{k,h}\log|\gamma\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}^{-1}|,

with
{v}_{k,cont}^{*}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(D)
,
0<\varepsilon<1
,
{v}_{k,h}^{*}\in{H}^{1}_{0}(D)
. By Lemma
I.4
, using the decomposition of
v_{k}^{*}
from Proposition
6.5
, namely,
v_{k}^{*}=\chi_{k}(v_{h}^{*}\log|\alpha|+v_{reg}^{*})
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{I}v_{k,h}^{*}=\gamma_{0}^{I}\chi_{k}v_{h}^{*},\text{ and }\gamma_{0}^{I,\lambda}(v_{k,cont}^{*}-{v}^{*}_{k,h}\log|\gamma\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}|+i\pi\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{U}_{k}\cap D_{n}}v^{*}_{k,h})=\gamma_{0}^{I,\lambda}\chi_{k}v_{reg}^{*},\quad\lambda\in\{n,p\},

so that, since
\operatorname{supp}\chi_{k}\subset\mathcal{U}_{k}
,

\displaystyle[\gamma_{0}^{I}\chi_{k}v_{reg}^{*}]=-i\pi\gamma_{0}^{I}v^{*}_{k,h}=-i\pi\gamma_{0}^{I}\chi_{k}v_{h}^{*}=-i\pi h_{I}^{-1}\gamma_{0}^{I}\chi_{k}\gamma_{n}^{I}v^{*}.

By the argument of Lemma
I.4
and
\sum\limits_{k}\chi_{k}=1
on
I
, we conclude that
[\gamma_{0}^{I}v^{*}]\equiv[\gamma_{0}^{I}v_{reg}^{*}]=-i\pi h_{I}^{-1}\gamma_{n}v^{*}
.
∎

I.1.5
Proof of Theorems
6.4
,
6.7

Since all the required tools have been given in the previous sections, the proof repeats verbatim the corresponding proof in Section
5
.
